at the bend of the river. Devereurs eyes followed her gaze. "•The Manor House!" he exclaimed. "Then I have had the honour of performing a slight service to--" "The daughter of Mr. Vincent, whose gratitude will not he less than mine," she replied as she stepped into the boat. He was loth to allow the boat to depart. He felt he wanted to chat, to tell her who he was, to send a message ,to her father; but he re- membered that for the moment he was not Sir Neville Brayton, merely Arthur Devereux, an absolute stranger, 'in whom Jacob Vincent was net likely to interest himself. • But he was wrong in his surmise. When Lucy Vincent moored her boat to the little landing-stage, her rescuer was still standing on the bank. He ''waved hie .hand cis if to signal that he saw tehe was safe. She re- turned the signal, and then proceeded along the path through the wood, strangely im- pressed by her morning's adventure. Jacob Vincent wds also moved when he heard his daughter's. Story. He despatched a note to Lucy'e rescuer there and then, warmly thanking him for his timely service. The occupant of Timothy Bright's cottage was invited to call on the morrow, so that he could receive the personal thanks of a grate-

and his vutfians i.)?iow7 Iry nat aia tney in- tend to do with him? Put him out of the way? Possibly. DeVereux resolved to be pre- pared for aily attack. He had smashed a chair, intending to use the heavy legs as weapons. He would sell his life dearly. But he was left alone. For several nights the dull hammering nois.:o went on. Then it ceased, and Devereux divined that the house was deserted. Now for escape. But how? The lock of the door could not be forced, and mangled limbs would assuredly follow any attempt of leaping from the window. Suddenly an idea shot into his brain. "The secret staircase!" he exclaimed. __

"4-1;:y didnit-I think of it?" The old mansion had been put to queer uses in those stormy days, centuries back, when men had to hide for their lives, and the fugi- tives, to whom the Braytons gave shelter, often owed their safety to the secret staircase. As a boy the prisoner had frequ'ently amused himself by ascending and descending the staircase which led to all the rooms at the back of the house. He had to remove a chest of drawers before he could find the secret panel. It was still there. Pressing his full weight on the floor at the bottom of the panel, he had the satisfac- tion of seeing it open inwardly. The next moment he had passed through the aper- ture; the panel resumed its position in the wall, and he found himself on the damp

learned subsequently that what prisoner had told him was false, and gave information to the police. Detective Davies, who received the prisoner from. the Stockton Heath. police, said when charged with the offence prisoner replied: -" I don't know what made me do it." - - Prisoqer asked that the letter he wrote to the magistrates from gaol should be read in Court. It contained a very pathetic plea to the magis- trates not to commit him for trial for the sake of his dear wife, his brother and sisters, and father's sake. He had wrongfully deceived his wife. He had a father, brother, and sisters; they were very respectable people, and they resided in Cumberland. He had broken his mother's heart, and had sent her to the grave, and he did not want to do the same with his aged father. By sending him for trial it would kill his wife, who was in trouble. He wished to be dealt with summarily. "Believe me," he concluded, "I will make amends if you will deal with me at once." Superintendent Ennion, in asking for Wilson to be committed to the Chester Quarter Sessions, said that he was wanted on similar charges by the Seaforth police. He had been convicted several times before, and was well known at Scotland Yard. He had deceived his wife, whom he married only last March. Prisoner, on hearing this, burst into tears, and called out loudly to the magistrates: "Oh, don't commit me for trial. As true as there is a God above me, my wife knows nothing about this offence. I plead for mercy. See, my wife is weeping. Don't send me for trial; it will break her heart. It is killing me by inches. Don't send me for trial." Prisoner was committed to take his trial at he next Chester Quarter Sessions.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RUNCORN DOCTOR SUMMONED. Dr. Warrington Yorke, head a the Runcorn I)rl_nch of the Liverpool School of Tropical icine, NNas summoned at Runcorn on Mon- day, for cruelty to an ass by failing to provide proper attention when it was in a state of suffering. The summons had been taken out on the information of Inspector H. L. Dee, of the R.S.P.C.A. The case was adjourned until September 1.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN TRAGEDY.

----- A tragic discovery was made on Monday morning, when Mrs. Booth: who resides at 34. Surrey-street, Runcorn, hearing a noise in her son's apartments, went to investigate the cause, and on opening the door was horrified to see him lying in a pool of blood, with a deep pall in the neck, supposed to have been self- inflicted with a razor, which was found lying on the floor close to. The deceased is John James Booth, said to be aged about 22 years, and it is stated that he had been for the last few weeks very peculiar in his manner.

HARVESTER'S JOLLIFICATION .AT RUNCORN.

"The whole neighbourhood was in a state of panic," said Constable Prince at Runcorn, on Monday, when three Irish farm labourers, named Dominic Sherlock, William Cain, and John Dowling, were charged together • with drunkenness and disorderly behaviour in Hal- ton on Sunday night. It was stated that there were 13 harvesters together, having a jollification. It was a regular occurrence on Sunday evenings in the village. They disturbed the whole com- munity. The Bench warned the prisoners of the seriousness of the charge, and if any of them or their friends came before them in future a severe penalty would be imposed. Fines of ss. was inflicted.

BRAVERY REWARDED. THIRTEEN LIVES SAVED BY TWO RUNCORN MEN.

----- --- Bravery was recognised on Monday at the Widnes Police Court, when Horace, Atherton, Mersey-road, and William Taylor, Bank-street, received at the hands of the Mayor a postal order for £l, a silver medal, and an illumin- ated address from the .Liverpool Shipwreck and Humane Society. Superintendent Foster stated that on June 22nd a little girl, Eliza Roberts, while playing near the Transporter Bridge, fell, into the river, and was, carriedl away rapidly with the tide. Taylor ran 8,0 yards on hearing the alarm,- and when he saw The ch:ld she was 30 yards out in the river. He entered the water and secured her near the Old Runcorn Bridge, but he was soon in diffi- culties, though he struck out -manfully to We side. Atherton then entered the water. :•nd took possession of the child and brought 1 uth safely to land. .. The little child .was suite ..ng from shock, and was unconscious. Athe'on • brought her round by artificial respiration. .he was taken to the Accident Hospital, and released next day.., The Mayor congratulated both men on their gallantry. He expressed pleasure that they were. born . and bred Widnes men.

Both men responded modestly that they were prepared to do the same again if necessary. Taylor has saved eight livcs and Atherton live, a record of which the Mayor himself felt proud. . :. ...

KNUTSFORD LIBERALS.

Sir J. Emmott Barlow, M.P., and the Government's Fulfilled Pledges.

Liberals from all parts of the Knutsford Division attended in large numbers a demon- stration in Poynton Park on Saturday. Sir Jons EIVIMOTT BARLOW, M.P., said Liberals had good reason to rejoice, because they had passed many useful measures, and they could now look to the future. They- could ask for a renewal of the people's mandate with confidence. They had fulfilled their pledges and the man- date of 1910. They had curbed ;the power of the Lords. They had done something to put the two political parties, to some extent, uron an eluality of opportunity—(hear, hear)—such as they had not had in our time before. Lil,eral ' members deserved well of the people, because they had done what they promised. Colonel BROCRLEHURST, M.P., said it seemed to him only just that every man should have a vote, and one only. The question of agricul- tural wages and cottages would rie tii4.'ussed in the land scheme. The present riovernimnt lad given 20,000 acres to small tiolders, :tad he believed the system .was working well. In Cheshire they had Do deficit to face. The meeting was presided over by Mr. H. N. Bickerton, of Thornycroft Hall, Siddington. Others present included Sir John FA. Barlow, Bart.. M.P.' Colonel W. B. -Brockleriurst, M.P., Mr. Philip M. Oliver (prospective Liberal can- didate), Mr. and Mrs. Henry P. Greg, Mr. and Mrs. J. R. Oliver, Mr. and Mrs. W. B. Hodgkin- son, Mr. and Mrs. Tom Stott. Mr. J. o—Nichol- son, Mr. and Mrs. C. J. PROTECTION FOR CHICKENS.

A very important detail in the manage- ment of half-grown chickens is the pro- vision of shelter both from cold winds and from too much sun. Some poultry-yards are so excellently situated that there is abundant natural shade, and when this is so the houses should be placed in close proximity to the trees or hedges. In many yards, however,there is:little or no natural shelter, and in this case it is necessary' to provide the growing chickens with

;ome artificial- shade-. I have several times told how capital shelter can be provided at a small expenditure of time and money. Two hurdles tied together, with a layer of straw between, some flake hurdles, a bundie of gorse or twigs, or a canvas shelter, may all be used to protect the birds from biting winds and cold rains if the weather is bad, or from two much , sun if the weather be warm.—E. T. Brown lin the "Farm and Home."

But when his daughter's rescuer stood before him, the speech and the reward were both for- gotten. He had expected to see a young man of the middle class, perhaps a London clerk, who had come up the river for a holiday. He had never dreamt of associating Timothy Bright's tenant with a man of distinguished mien and perfect manners. "I feel you have saved my daughter's life," he began. "It was lucky you were on the spot, for she could not have held on to that tree much longer. We are both very grateful to you, and our gratitude will be shared by my neighbour, Sir Neville Brayton, who, I see, is coming up the avenue." Arthur Devereux gave a start. Jacob Vin- cent did not notice it, for his eyes were on his daughter. Lucy's head fell. There was that in her face which would have told a keen observer that for her,. at any rate, a pleasant little ceremony had been destroyed by the advent of the Baronet. "Mr. Devereux," began. ,Jacob Vincent when the Baronet entered the room, "this is Sir Neville Brayton, of whom you have no doubt heard. Sir Neville, Mr. Devereux rescued Lucy yesterday morning from an awkward plight. She was floundering in the river clutching the branch of a tree when, happening to be on the opposite bank, he promptly, went to her assistance. Lucky for you as well as for her. eh?" The two men looked straight into each other's eyco—the one with a fearlessness as much as to say: "Now I know with whom I have to deal," the other with studied indif- ference. as if to give the impressidn that he had not been in the least interested in the tale of knight-errantry he had just heard. In that one glance 4rthur Devereux saw suffi- cient to convince him that the usurper of his name belonged to that gang of splendid scoundrels who operate everywhere. Again he was tempted to reveal himself, to tell. Jacob Vincent that he was cultivating the friendship of an impostor. He turned round, and found Lucy's eyes fixed upon him. There was a world of meaning in her glance. It told him that whatever were the aspirations cherished by her father she did not share them. He left the Manor House more than ever determined to play the role he had assumed to the end. Lucy and he met often. They were chancZ meetings at first, marked by a shyness on her part and a 'little hesitancy on his. But the time:came when each looked .for the other on the path through the wood. Devereux was always in high spirits. He talked long and interestingly about his travels, but never about himself. And Lucy listened entranced. She came to regard him as her knight-errant, her hero, who had invaded her peaceful en- vironment in a mysterious fashion as if to save her from the odious Baronet whom her father intended should be her husband. She was sitting on a mossy mound one morning, enjc.)yjng the sunshine and the songs of the birds LA the branches overhead, when she descried his tall form advancing towards her. An indefinable feeling had taken posses- sion of her—a feeling premonitory of mo- mentous happenings. The first greetings over, Devereux. seemed unusually preoccupied. She divined that something was on his mind, something struggling for expression. And she was right in her surmise. "Miss Vincent," he began, "we are some- thing like old friends now, are we not?" Lucy fancied she saw a new li ht in the grey eyes fixed upon her. "It. seems like it," she replied, in a low tone. Devereux drew closer to her. "Could we not be something more than friends?" he went on. "I have learnt to love you, Lucy. Yes, I, who until a few days ago was a com- plete stranger to you, find myself thinking about you every hour of the day. A sudden passion, you may think, but it is a deep and abiding one." She was silent, her eyes fixed on the green undergrowth. "You are thinking that you know so little about me," he continued. True, my coming to Brayton may seem a little abrupt, but— well, you will find that my credentials will bear examination. What is more to the point, I am confident that I can make you happy. Tell me," he added, seizing her hand, "have I spoken in vain?" She had already raised her head, her eyes, filled with the love-light, met his, and she was about to speak, when there was a crushing of twigs behind them. The next moment the branches of trees parted, and Sir Neville Brayton appeared .before them. There was no mistaking the sinister expres- Sion on his face, the chagrin in his heart. "Ah, Miss Vincent," he began, "I did not expect to find you here. This is a new walk you've found out, is it not? It's an ideal one for those in search of solitude. And our new neighbour has found it out too, though it's a long way round by the bridge. What a pity you haven't a boat, Mr. Dewireux. He had noted the absence of " Sir Neville " from that re- joinder. For a moment his dark eyes sought "Devereux's face. What he read in it caused zim to turn sharply to Lucy. ' "I was on my way to your father," he said. "You'll give me the pleasure of your com- pany. Good-morning, Mr. Devereux. You've still a long walk before you, and I hope you'll enjoy it." The sneer in the voice was accompanied by unspeakable revenge in the eyes. As Devereux walked home he was under no illu- sion as to the lengths the adventurer would go if he found himself in a light corner. The adventurer's brain was busy that day. Be had an intuition of danger ahead. Of one thing he was certain—he had a dangerous rival in Arthur Devereux, who, lie divined. from what he had seen in the wood, could have Lucy Vincent for the asking. There was another matter which troubled him. Did Devereux know anything? He struck yet another match—he had taken care to leave no •traces of the burnt ones— found the front door, pushed back the strong latch, and passed into the darkness. Leaving the carriage drive he struck into a thicket, then across fields until he came to a road. It was familiar to him in the starlight. lie was about three miles from his cottage, which he reached in three-quarters of an hour. Mrs. Jones returned to the cottage next morning, • accompanied by Timothy Bright. Both were .relieved on seeing Devereux. - "They told me you had gone," said thg yeoman. "I wouldn't believe it, and came to see for myself. I am right, you see," he added, turning to, the housekeeper. The eyes of the honest farmer sparkled with pleasure. "Then you think of remaining in the dis- trict?" he asked. "I have made up my mind never to leave it," Devereux replied. "You can have the cottage and welcome, sir." "Thank you. Kindly have the contract of lease for twenty years drawn up at your earliest convenience. Meanwhile, I would be glad if you would render me a little service. Have these telegrams sent off on your return to Brayton. You may read the messages, but not a word, please. you see, I am placing the fullest confidence in you." Timothy Bright scanned the telegrams. When he came to the signature his mouth opened, and he literally gasped with astonish- ment. "He's at the Manor House now," he whis- pered. "He arrived by the first train from London this morring with two other gentle- men." " Ah, that's good news, Mr. Bright. Now send off those telegrams. There's not a mo- ment to spare." Timothy Bright hurried back to Brayton, and saw that the messages were clicked off at the post-office. Sir Neville Brayton, too, pro- ceeded to the village and had an interview with the police. An hour afterwards four local policemen lay concealed in the thicket skirting the avenue leading to Brayton Hall. - • • .

"I am desperately earnest, Lucy. I mean you to be my wife. Your father desires the marriage to take place at once—by special licence if necessary. We've both -got beyond the sentimental stage. Love will come soon enough after marriage. At any rate, I am willing to take all risks. See how great is the faith I have in you. You will not refuse. Nay, you cannot refuse." , The _ adventurer's masterfulness was in vain. Lucy Vincent shrank from his touch. "You do me great honour, Sir Neville," she said, "but—but I cannot marry you." "But you must," he hissed, discarding the mask of studied politeness, and seizing her wrist in a grip of iron. "I will not take a refusal. You understand? As you value your happiness, your very life, you will marry me. You see how desperate I am. Mine is a pas- sion which must be gratified." As his wrath rose so his grip on the slender wrist tightened. She felt his hot breath on her face, saw the dangerous fire in his eyes, and out of sheer helplessness cried out. Jacob Vincent, who was awaiting the result of the interview, not doubting that it would end in Lucy becoming Lady Brayton, rushed into the room. "What is it?" he demanded, alarmed on noting Lucy's distress. Before he could receive an answer a foot- man appeared and handed him a card which bore the name, " Sir Neville Brayton." "Sir Neville Brayton!" he gasped. does this mean?" "It means that it is high time I introduce 4 myself to you properly," said his daughter's rescuer, who appeared before him smiling. On beholding her hero Lucy gave a little cry of gladness and rushed to his side. "What stupid hoax is this?" demanded the

pseudo Sir Neville, angrily. "Send for the police at once, Mr. Vincent, and have this man arrested." "We have provided for that, sir," said Sir

"Whether you know your man .or not," continued Sir Neville, addressing the detec- tive, "I give him in charge for assuming my title, taking possession of my house, and con= verting it into a coiner's den. His two accom- plices have been ar:ested at the Hall." "We ought to know each other, Roger Huggins," said the detective, slipping the handcuffs on the adventurer's wrists. "Of course, you've not forgotten that affair of the necklace." Roger Huggins • remembered, but did not utter a word. 'lt is real enough," laughed. Sir Neville. "As to your other question, I, at any rate, have loved Lucy since the day I rescued her from the river." Lucy pressed closer to her. hero. Her eyes met his, and he read in their deep blue depths a similar confession. .

ENGINEERING.

For the carrying out of a great engineer- ing scheme there is needed the co-operation of the civil engineer, the mechanical engineer, and the contractor. Of these the first does '41114 we call the mathematical part of the work, the second provides the heavy machin- ery, and the third employs the machinery of the second to realise the plans of the fir,t. Each of the three must, of course, be versed , in the general science of the departments of his two colleagues, while specialising in his Own. The civil englneer, though his name often looms largest in connection with some daring design successfully accomplished, is very dependent on the versatility of his me- chanical coadjutor. It is his part to sug- gest; the other's to make; and so inter- tw:ned are the two branches of engineering, that one can hardly decide which of them leads the way. The, invention of a new ma- chine makes. it possible for the designer to design on a larger scale; and, on the other hand, the nececsity of the civil engineer is the mother or the mechanical engineer's in-

vention

In this article we will pass in review some of the more striking developments of mechan- isms of the larger sort. THE HYDRAULIC PRESS. About a hundred and ten years ago Joseph Bramah patented his hydrostatic or, as we call it now, hydraulic press. In theory it was perfect, but in practice it failed at first, owing to water leaking between the rani and the side of the cylinder in which it worked, if the joint were ,packed loosely enough to permit the ram to move. Bramah was unable to solve the problem, and had al- ready despaired of reaping the reward of his ingenuity, when a subordinate, Henry Maudslay by name, solved the problem in a most masterly fashion. In the wall of the cylinder near the neck he cut a deep groove, and placed thergin a collar of leather having a U-shaped section, the concave side turned towards the closed end of the cylinder. The pressure of the water forced the collar so tightly against the ram during the outstroke that none of the fluid could pass; and yet the collar, by collapsing, allowed the ram to descend easily the moment the pressure was taken off. The hydraulic Dress, thus made •a prac- tical machine, has been of incalculable value to the engineer and manufacturer. A mere catalogue of its uses would occupy the rest of this article; and we must therefore limit ourselves to a few notable instances of recent adaptation'. The most powerful individual hydraulic presses in existence are those used at the Bethlehem Steel Works, Pennsylvania, and at the Openshaw Works' of Sir W. G. Arm- strong, Whitworth and Co., for consolidating white-hot armour-plate ingots by squeezing. This method has replaced the pounding with steam hammers formerly, employed. Th.e [ Bethlehem machine exerts a total pressure of 15,000 tons, that at Openzhaw one of 12,00 Q tons; the water-pressures being respectively 101 and, 3 tons to the square inch of ram. The hydraulic bender will curve a flat slab of 8-inch steel into a semi-circular shape to form part of a man-of-war's turret, or turn over the edge of a 4-inch plate to provide a flange for riveting to another pldte. In ord- nance works the hydraulic press forces the copper "driving band" on to the outside of a shell. Another important application is found in the hydraulic lift. When Robert Stephenson was building the Britannia Tubu- lar Bridge across the Menai Straits he host- ad the gigantic tubes into position by ni45.,,as of presses each sustaining a weight of 1,400 tons.. Again, the 3,200-ton bascules of the Tower Bridge are opened by water power. At the Union Iron Works, San'., Francisco, a cradle resting on hydraulic rams raises a vessel of 4,000 tons bodily from the water; and in several places one may see barges, transferred from water-way tO another on a much higher level by sl.milar devices. To take an example, the canal lift at Peterbor- ough in Canada handles boats of 400 tons burthen through a vertical distance of 65 bet.

ELECTRIC AND STEAM CRANES.

The .gigantic electric and steam crane also deserves notice. At Messrs. Harland and Wolff's shipbuilding yard, Belfast, is a •rnas- jve " gantry " crane spanning two slips, into which it lowers shafting and heavy ma- chinery used in constructing the vessels be- neath. The travelling frame 'on which the crane moves is about 180 feet high and 650 feet long, and weighs 6,000 tons. This frame is propelled along rails laid on a solid founda- tion of several- thousand tons of concrete e and itself carries transverse rails at the top as a runway for a 'great -cantilever. crane which has a reach of about 110 feet in all. di- rections. , The power is entirely electric. An at- tendant has merely to shift a few levers to make the crane pick up a 20-ton load, carry it to the required point, and lower it into position. In some workshops the actual, grip- ping of a load is electrical, a large magnet replacing the usual hook tackle ;—a very convenient method when steel plates have to be stacked or shifted. In connection with large harbour works, such as those at Dover, Vera Cruz, Gibraltar, and Cape Town, the steam "Titan" crane dces yeoman E. crvice. The largest Titen in the world is probably that lately used at Table Bay. Like others of its class, it has a Massive travell'ng base running on rails. On the top is pivoted a huge cantilever crane, carrying the - engine on one—the shorter— arm, and lifting the load . with the other, through the medium of a four-inch steel wire cable. It is capable of setting 40-ton con- erete blocks anywhere within a radius of 115 eet from the pivot. It need hardly be said that for mole-building a crane of this sOrt. is invaluable; in 'fact, the existence of our Mod- ern: .harbours is largely due to the "_Titan." The mechanical engineer, has greatly helped the harbour and ecanal, maker by perfecting the team. dredger. The, various types of thie machine are capable of removing enor- iroas quantities of materials, ranging in their nature from hard rock to soft. mud, in a shoet 'Erne from the bottom of the waterway. A. century and a half ago no vessel drawing more than 3 feet of water ,could approach Glasgow at high water even in spring tides, whereas now craft with a draught of 27 feet float up on any tide. The improvement is entirely due to the dredger. ,Some dredgers scoen up the bottom materi- al with a cha.n of huge -buckets, each of two tons capacity, running round the end of a great. ladder, which with the buckets may weigh upwards of .200 tons. The "spoil" is either poured into barges alongside to be car- ried out to sea and dumped dawn,, or the dredger itself receives it in large hoppers and deposits it in deep water. A powerful buck- et-lad•dcr machine will pick up 2,000 tons of stuff in an hour from as great a depth as 60 feet.. Sometimes the ladder is replaoed. Tay hugs centrifugal pumps, which suck the ma-- feria' up through capacious 'pipes; and in narrow Waterways such :as canals the rubbish is put ashore on either side through shoots suspended from a tall mast on the dredger. The last method was very largely employed durlng the construction of the _Suez Canal.• one dredger . alone banking -150,000 cubic yards of and a month. Even more ingenious is the- pneumatic ejector to squirt the sand and water through long pipes to the .point of delivery, which, in the case of: the Millwall Dock dredger was 450 feet away. WONDERFUL MACHINERY.

The creation of the huge engines now found -in our mammoth steam ships, and the pre- paration of armour plates for ironclads, de- wands the ,xistenee of workshop nmPh'rery of proportionate size. Of tne hydraulic press and steam hammer we have already spoken. Equally imposing are the monster lathes and planers to be found in first-class, steel works. The person who has been accustomed to small foot lathes would be astonished by the ease with which a Goliath of its kin will bore a hole 14 inches in diamater clean through a 30-inch propeller shaft, 670 feet lour. or

FARM AND GARDEN.

IMPROVED PROSPECTS.

The harvest. has begun amid surround- ings seldom associated with the ingather- jug of the, corn crops, says the "Times." The sunless skies and low temperatures are not the only, nor the chief, distinguishing characteristics, for even these are more common than the verdure of the landscape beyond the limits of the corn fields. The hedges are as full and rich in foliage, and the pastures and meadows as green and vigorous as in June, while even the spring barley and oats have stretched in an extra- ordinary way since they have been in full ear. Thus there is an incongruity in the appearance of the countryside that can be accounted for only by exceptional seasonal conditions. In spite of the unwonted variety and the absence of harmonious change in the colouring, the country looks remarkably well. Indeed, the deep green of the hedges and grass-land makes an effective setting for the white or yellow fields of corn. The strange mixture of hues indicates improvement in the farming situation. The recent rains and sunless skies base retarded the ripening of the cereals but the compensating advantages of added bulk, both of straw and grain, and the revived activity of the root crops and pastures make insignificant anything that can be put on the opposite side of the account. _

A cheerful tone characterises every reference to live stock. Breeders of pedi- gree animals have cleared at high prices almost every animal they could spare, cattle, sheep, and pigs meeting with an equally spirited demand for export or for home use. The breeding of high-class animals has never been in a more pros- perous condition or offered brighter pros- pects for the future. The export trade is growing as regards both the number of countries participating and the breeds chosen. Commercial classes of animals are meeting with a correspondingly brisk demand at remunerative prices.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPROVING HEAVY LAND.

The farmer of stiff ,clay land has many difficulties of cultivation to contend with which are unknown to the light land farmer, but he has the compensation that his land is retentive of any manure he gives it, whilst it usually has more natural reserves ofoplant food than a light soil. What it needs most is mechanical improve- ment to make it work more easily. There is much in knowing the right way to cultivate, for a clay that comes up in impossible solid, glazed clods in spring may be ploughed up in beautiful loaming con- dition in August or September, t after a dry summer. Every effort should, there- fore, be made to get heavy land stubbles ploughed early after harvest. All cleaning operations should be done then, as there is often little chance in spring. Steam tackle gets over the work quickly, and is nowhere more useful. If theland lies rough for the winter, it becomes pulverised by frost and breaks down into fine condition at a stroke of the harrow in spring. Drainage is, of course, the first essential.—" Farm and Home."

RESULTS OF THE FARM STRIKE.

To the executive committee of the Agricultural Labourers' Union, Mr. George Edwards, the secretary, has presented a report claiming that al; a result of the recent strike in Lancashire great advan- tages were won for the labourers, viz. : Saturday half-holiday. 6d. an hour overtime. Various increases of wages. _ _ _

Future provided for by c-onstitution of a board of arbitration consisting of six farmers and six labourers, presided over by the district coroner.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
SHEEP-CHASING DOGS.

At the West London County Court on Tuesday, John Smith, a farmer, of Chis- wick, who, by contract with the Commis- sioner of Works, has a flock of sheep in Kensington Gardens, was awarded £2O damages and costs against Count Hollander, of Queen's Elm Gardens. According to the evidence, two bull-terriers belonging to the Count were in the gardens in charge of a Frenchman. When released from the leash they ran the sheep, some of which injured themselves by running into the shin rails in the park. It was said that fright caused the sheep to depreciate from the butcher's point of view.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE WHEAT.

One of the latest districts to be opened up for new settlement in the State of New I South -Wales is that of Pallamallawa, which is situated some 20 miles from the famous Moree centre. The new area, Which returned but 5,000 bushels of wheat in 1911, produced 45,000 bushels last year. The area under -crop-- has been increased threefold this year—or to 4,120 acres—and the harvest estimate for 1913 has been set down at over 100,000 bushels. The average annual rainfall of the district for 31 years is 23 inches, with 11 inches during the growing period. Last.year's crop gave the fine average of 231/2 bushels. Many of the newcomers, baying secured large wheat areas are inviting the assistance of share ' farmers with a view of securing immediate returns from their holdings.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

PROPAGATING ROCK PLANTS BY CUT- TINGS.—The majority of rock plants that have now finished flowering can be readily raised from cuttings. Select the young growths and place them in sandy soil under a hand-light, and keep a moist atmosphere by occasional overhead syringing during ,bright weather. By these means many plants that are not easily raised from seed can be readily increased. When rooted they should be potted off, and will make nice plants for the rock garden the following spring.—" The Garden." AMONG THE ROSES.—Early August usually marks the termination of the first crop of flowers, and it is then that the cultivator must see about securing autumn blooms. The prompt renewal of faded and dead flowers down to a good sound bud, spraying the bushes with insecticide, and applying some good artificial manure, such as guano, with a top-dressing of some short stable manure, will work wonders in a week or two, partipularly if followed with a really heavy sdaking of water, unless rain falls in abundance, which it seldom does at this time. To keep down mildew spray the bushes frequently with sulphide of potassium, loz. dissolved in two gallons of, preferably, soft water.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review." THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST. RURAL HOUSING.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
Suggested State Grants for Che

Cottages.

A memorial signed by notable men Of the three political parties has been sent to the Prime Minister pressing for legislation on rural housing, to include State grants based on the annual loss which rural authorities who show sufficient enterprise to tackle this crying need in the villages sustain in building cottages and letting them at a cheap rent An interesting object-lelsson in the problem of the lowest price at which healthy and suffi- ciently bedroomed cottages can be built was afforded by Mr. a. Tiae Strachey near. Guildford on Saturday. A cottage in occupation was inspected, which, not reckoning. In the land, was built for otlso—a figure adopted by the Presi- dent of the. Board of Agriculture for his scheme of building cottages on wholesale lines.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
WAGES ), ITOUSING

The National Land und Home League Coun- cil's. memorial. gives; mike figures collected from rural. district councils in reply to a circular issued last year; 182 councils said that cottages were rectuired in their. districts, 261 councils that cottages could not be built without placing a burden on the rates, and 169 councils declared themselves, in favour of State aid for building. Remembering, no doubt, Mr. Burns's declara-

tion in the House of Commons last month that if it was desired to help the labourer it was better either by trade union effort or by legis- lation to raise wages by 25., 33., or 4s. a week so that they could command a -house that was nol,tied to the .farm or the land they tilled, than tonvoke the .aid of Parliament in suggesting !t. subventions that would work out at not more than 4d. or scl. a week a cottage, the memorialists say that so far as they know no official inquiry has been made by the President of the Local Government Board to ascertain the wishes of rural authorities in the matter of housing. They express their approval of the

principles of Sir Randolph Baker's Housing Bill, which was given a second reading by a majority of 140 last April. The Bill provided that a special housing department of the Local Government Board should be established, that the Local Government Board should have power theMselves to deal with slums and build houses in the areas of defaulting authorities, and should have two funds of half a million each from which to make grants, for urban and rural housing, the grants in the case of rural housing not exceeding 4-5 of the difference be- tween the nest of building and the rent a work- ing man could be expected to pay. The memorialists, however, say that there are obvious advantages of a grant in aid in respect of annual loss, instead of a capital grant to local authorities. _ _ _

Among those who have signed are Lord Henry Bentinck, M.P. (President of the League), Lord Says and Sele, the Bishops of Birmingham, Carlisle, Gloucester, Hereford, Peterborough, the Dean of Lincoln, Canon Scott Holland, the Very Rev. Monseigneur Benson, Chamberlain to His Holiness Pius X.; Sir Arthur Boscawen, ALP., Charles Bathurst, M.P., Mr. Russell Rea, M.P., Sir Alfred Mond, M.P., Lord Wolmer, M.P., Mr. Noel Buxton, M.P., Mr. Chiozza- Money,. CUR PARKS.

Corporation Scheme to Treat Them

on Aesthetic Lines.

The Corporation has in hand a scheme for

the. laying out of parts of Victoria Park and St. Elphin's Park to the best advantage, and application has been made to the Local Govern- ment Board for permission to borrow suns of £270 and £355 reSpectively for that purpose. On Saturday morning Mr. F. H. Tulloch, an inspector of the Board, attende,d at the Town Hall to inquire into the application. Very little interest was taken in' the proceedings, no members of the general public "putting in an appearance. The Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. A. T. Hallaway) explained that £lO,OOO had been sanctioned for improvements in Victoria Park, of which sum £8,427 had been borrowed, and £7,292 still remained as outstanding loans. With regard to St. Elphin's Park, the amount auth- orised was £3,511, of which £3,459 was unpaid. Warrington was a great industrial town, with comparatively few parks and open spaces, and it was the desire of the Corporation to give a little more pleasure to those who were not as well catered for in that respect as they ought to be. They intended, as far as possible, to lay out all the parks in a similar manner.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
"A MISNOMER."

"You see the beautiful park near you," said Mr. Hallaway. ." You have the 'opportunity of viewing it through the windows of this Council Chamber." With regard to Victoria Park, he continued, the word " Park " was rather a misnomer. It was a large field, not properly laid out, but there was a bowling green, and the Corporation intended to treat the land in a proper, Eesthetic manner. St. Elphin's Park was a very small one, and part of it was devoted to a 'bowling green.

Mr. M. A. Piercy --(deputy borOugh surveyor) haying produced and explained plans of the improvements. the -inquiry closed, and - the Inspector visited the parks.

FARM AND GARDEN.— Continued. that the plants should be shown as grown. In nearly every instance the plants staged have been those of the intermediate section, many of which are of a perennial or semi- perennial character. These are naturally much larger and more branching than the old Ten-week Stock, and undoubtedly better plants for the garden. In judging we have hesitated to disqualify these exhibits, but it would be well if framers of schedules would ask for Stocks raised from seed sown during the current year, and thus make it quite legitimate for exhibitors to stage those of the intermediate section. —" The Garden." GOOSEBERRY MILDEW.—At the last meeting of the Herefordshire County Council it was reported that a notable feature of the disease this year is that it is chiefly making its appearance on the berries, and not nearly so much on the leaves and young wood as has been the case in former years. The disease was only noticed on a very few bushes in each of these cases.

CLIMBING ROSES.—Plants On arches Or pergolas should receive attention in having all the old wood thinned out and cut away, this allows 'plenty of air to reach the young growths, enabling them to become hardened before the frosts come, and it also encour- ages new and stronger growths to push out. "Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review." A GOOD INCOME FROM BEES.—Five pounds per week is a fairly good income in these competitive days, but in Ontario one may expect to make that amount out of bee keeping alone and has only about two months' actual labour. Last season a bee keeper cleared over £l4O from his hives. This year he is increasing the number of his hives and expects £250. This alone is not his only revenue. He makesiabout £lOO a year from chickens, and in addition there is a big revenue from potatoes, berries and other crops. It is interesting to note that this success sprung from very small beginnings and that equal success awaits those who are prepared to ally their industry with the bountiful nature of Ontario.

ALEXANDRA DAY PROCEEDS.

The magnificent success of the Alexandra 'Day celebrations in Wi.uTington was the- sub- iect, of general. congratulation at the final meet- !mg of. the ladies' committee, which was held at the Infirmary on Tuesday afternoon. The amount available for distribution among local charities is £351. result upon which , all the, workers • may justly pride themselves. It is too early to 'say whether Alexandra Day henceforth. have a permanent place in our local, calendar, but there is a possibility that .next year a rose: carnival, will be coupled with 'an. old. English. fair, to be held on behalf of the :Infirmary. Mr. .1: H. SMETHURST, who presided at the imeeting. on. Tuesday, read a letter from Mrs. W. Monks; horn- treasurer .of the committee, stating that the balance sheet was most satis- :factory, and, heartily, congratulating the com- mittee on 'the success of. their great efforts.

WAIIBINGTON COMPABED WITH LONDON. Mr. F. A. In comparing the two, he must say that Warrington was not the Slightest bit behind London in the energy and enthusiasm infused into the work, and the results in Warrington, taken all round, were better than those in the Metropolis. The expenses attendant upon the collection of the Money in .London were equal to about 30 per cent., whereas in Warrington they were only 1. per cent. (Applause.)

THE IDEAL HOSPITAL

Referring to the needs of the Infirmary, Mr. Smethurst. said that they were never so busy in the wards as at the present time. The Board had therefore decided that it was,abso- lutely necessary that another small ward should be opened to cope with the- work. .As the town grew the demands. upon the Infirm- ary and the District Nursing Association roust correspondingly increase. He looked forward to the time when there would be systematic coPections throughout the town, in the works and shops, on behalf of the Infirmary. When that came about, the institution would be able to deal with serious cases of tuberculosis and other diseases which, under the existing con- ditions. could not be kept there sufficiently long. They would also be able to provide their own sanatorium or convalescent home at the seaside. It was impossible to imagine the wonderful amount of good that could be done if children, after being treated in the Infirmary. were not sent home to surroundings which we-e probably not so clean as Was desiral,le, but were _ailtowed to spend some. Mlle, in the Warrington Infirmary Convalescent Home." (Anpinuse.) That was a. dream of the future, but when the ladies of Warrington were will- ing to work like they had done recently, they were drawing -mulch nearer to -the day of- the ideal hospital. (Applause.)

NEW TOWN CLERK OF TODMORDEN.

Mr. Herbert Garratt, deputy town clerk of Burnley, was on Wednesday night appointed Town Clerk of Tedmorden in succession to the late Mr. Dan Sutcliffe. Originally there were 39 candidates for the post, and of these four were selected for further consideration—Mr. J. Entwistle, town clerk of Bacup; Mr. J. J. Gledhill, solicitor, of Todmorden who recently resigned his seat on the Town Connell; Mr. ¶li A. Turton, deputy town clerk of St. Helens; and Mr. Garrett. The successful candidate is 33 years of age, utld was admitted to the roll of solicitors in 1904. He was appointed to the post he is leaving at Burnley in 1906.

TO TAKE BRUISES OUT OF FURNITURE. Wet the part with warm water; double a piece of brown paper five or six times, soak it, and lay it on the place; apply on that a hot flatiron till the moisture is evaporated; if the bruise he not gone, repeat the process. Generally after two or three applications the dent or bruise is level with the surface. If the bruise be very small, merely soak it with warm water and apply a red-hot poker near the surface; keep it constantly wet, and in a few minutes the bruises will disappew. •

" STAGE BUNKUM."

Sir Edward Carson's Talk about

Militant Ulster.

A FREE TRADE ANALOGY.

An earnest and convincing advocacy of. Home' Rule by a loyal son a Erin was listened. to, • with interest and appreciation by a large audience at .the Warrington Progressive Labour League weekly opett-air meeting, 'at Bridge Foot, on. Monday evoningi. The speaker, Mr. P.. 1, Kelly, of Manchester,: dealt with the subject in its historical, and. pwsent- day aspects.

FBOM THE WOBRING MAYS STANDPOINT„ Mr. 11. OSBORNE, who presided, touched- hoop .one side of the Home Buie_ question, which. closely affects the British working man.. He pointed out that. one reason for the decline of, the population of Ireland was- that large num- bers of her inhabitants came over to this country to seek employment., .because capital had .greater-,Opportunities of expansion here. If Ireland were allowed -self-government she would develop her own industriesi. and the result‘ would' be that the keen competition for employment in -England would be lessened. (Hear, heal.) - Touching upon; the fiscal question, Mr. Osborne contended .that, as applied locally, Protection would be unjust, as it would enable certain industries to -secure monopolies at the expense of others.

"ONE KING AND ONE PARLIAMENT." Mr. KELLY, whose address was lightened with many flashes of ',humour, said Sir Edward Carson had appealed to the democracy of this country to maintain the allegiance Of the Empire to ," one King, one flag, and one Parlia- ment." AS a matter of fact, tliere were •28 Parliaments, in .addition to the one at West- minster, which. worked under our " one King and one Hag," and, moreover, they had all declared themselves in favour of Home Rule. Mr. Kelly went on to relate how the Act Of Union came into being, and said the consent of the Irish Parliament, which was not popularly elected, was . obtained by bribery and base methods, fabulous sums being paid to secure the votes of pocket boroughs. ,•

REPAYING—OUREBT OF . GRATITUDE. . A. promise4as also made that Catholic eman- cipation would-be,granted immediately Ireland had been taken over by the English Parliament, but it was not until 1829 that that measure was conceded. The Irish- representatives at West- minster had consistently supported the claims and aspirations of democracy, and they were now asking the. people of England to repay only a very small part. of the debt of gratitude, they owed the Nationalists. (Applause.) The Irish members fought for the great electoral emancipation-Bill ot- 1832, which, although con- ferring only a nariow- franchise, was the thin end of the .wedge. 1 From that time to the pre- sent, he defied ally. Tory' or. -Orangeman to point- to an Act oft;'Parliament, whether Tory or Liberal, which 'tad for-its object the better- ing of the conditions of The people, and which the Irish members thad not supported.. At the resent time the deMocracy of England, Scot- land and Ireland waS marking time, waiting i'or the order tO march -forward. As Mr. *lire said, on Saturday, it was really of little prac- tical use to talk Of home 'Rule and Welsh Dis- establishment ; there would' be no more elec- tions on those measures. That was his answer ,to Sir Edward- CaYsOn and his "lifeguards." (Laughter.)

SOME TPIAGIC FIGVBES

, Sixty years ago the population of Ireland was over 8000,000. Great Britain had only a population' of 18,000,000 in those days, and Scot- land was accountable for only 2,500,000 of that number. To-day Scotland's population was in advance of that of Ireland, which had shrunk to less than 4,500,000. From 1815 to 1855 there were more people lost to Ireland than, had died in all the wars from the American War of Independence,down to he present day. While people were, perishing • during • the great Irish famine, millions of - pounds of foodstuffs were being taken out of the country by the land- lords. , •

, Alluding to the present anti-Home Buie eam,. paign, the speaker said it 'showed the poverty and degradation of the Tory party when they had to go to the political..ceSspools of Liverpool, Glasgow and Belfast to find men to stand up at street corners and denounce their own kith and kin. Sir Edward Carson had twitted gr. Winston Churchill with .being a "turn-coat," but Mr. Churchill wisely, retorted.: "What about yourself?" and recalled the days when Sir Edward was a member of the Reform Club.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW IRELAND HAS BEEN BLED.

Referring to the cry that Home, Rule would mean Rome Rule, the speaker laid stress upon the fact that from 1782 to 1913 Ireland, al- though an overwhelmingly Catholic nation, had had only two •Roman Catholic leaders in Parliament. (Applause.) As to the implied inability of Ireland to finance herself, it should be borne in mind that a Commission which sat in 1903 to inquire into the financial relationship of England and Ireland unanimously came to the conclusion that between 1817 and 1903, Ire- land had been robbed of £3,000,000 per year in over-taxation. Sixty 'years ago Ireland, with a population of 9,0(10,000, had not half the taxes which she had to bear to-day, when the popu- lation had been reduced by half. In Ireland there was practically no crime, but a huge force of police was maintained as a standing army to guard the interests of the landlords. While the cost of police in Scotland *as £573,348, the cost in Ireland—the poorest country in Europe—was £1,487,337. Education in Scotland cost three times more than police, but in Ireland the .police cost more than educa- tion. •

===== 0002601_19130802_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
A HOME RULE MAJORITY IN ULSTER.

As showing the extravagance of the present system of governing Ireland, Mr. Kelly pointed Out that the number of Government 'officials in Scotland who. paid Income Tax was 944, with an annual income Of £319,237, •whereas the number in Ireland was 4,397, with an income of £1,441,131.. Sir Edward Carson was in the habit of speaking of." Ulster" as being against Home Buie; simply, to blindfold the people; whereas the true position was that of 33 mem- bers representing Ulster in Parliament, the Home Rulers had .17, and Sir Edward Carson 16. (Applause.) If plural voting were abol- ished, the Home linters would sweep Ireland. (Applause.) In Belfast efforts were being brought to bear to induce landlords to evict those people who helped to make up Mr. 'Devlin's majority, and to fill the constituency with Orangeman from other districts. Yet tltose people talked about fair play.

A FREE TRADE ANALOGY.

Sir Edward Carson was asked in the ILiue of Commons by Mr. Asquith, If we have a- General Election, will it satisfy you?" and Sir Edward's reply was: "No.; I will defy you and fight you." Mr. Bonar Law was hoping one (lay. to become the Prime Minister, but he was preaching. the •doctrine that if one little cotritr of a country didLnot like-an• Act of ParliarrEt,t, it was justified.. in rising in arms 'against it. ===== 0002601_19130802_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME AMUSING INCIDENTS.

(" Examiner " Special.)

Although a recent innovation in Warrington, the motor 'bus has been sufficiently long in existence to make its own little history. Strange pranks the earlier petrol-driven vehicles play. Uncertainty proved one of the most characteristic features. gunning through hedges, bridge parapets and mangling unalert animals were little tricks perpetratOd almost.daily.

I have a keen recollection of the first motor 'bus I encountered, not through a collision. The type of 'buses now running upon Warring- ton streets are palatial vehicles compared with my old friend of 1898. Warrington 'buses of

the hour are as different from the old stager as a Great Central express locomotive differs from Stephenson's "Puffing Billy."

- It was not a new 'bus, that is, the body of if was not new, and I am confident the engine parts were not new, either. On ic ‘‘ erected the body of an old horse 'bus, that had • trundled many a mile through the bumpy Lon- don streets. The engines—they seemed past redemption. The invitation to have a trip on the motor 'bus was the most up-to-date pro- duction connected with the whole show. It was printed in gold letters II; on a card richly gifted.

At the starting point the 'bus arrived an hour late. "Only a few minor engine troubles," genially explained the genial guide—a Yankee— to the party. "She's all right and will rip us along." Assured upon the point we boarded the 'bus. The engine panted and made a sound like the firing of a Maxim gun. With a sud- den jerk we started; a crowd of small boys cheered us on the way. We did rip alomg for the first half-mile—it was all downhill. The Troubles Begin.

Next we encountered a stretch of level road. The engine groaned, moaned, sighed, thun- dered, and generally made other sounds which no English words can adequately describe. We worked up to a steady pace of about three miles an hour. Nasty-minded pedestrians, jealous, perhaps, because they were not upon the 'bus, made references to snails and tortoises, as they overtook and passed us. We had traversed about a mile at this break- neck speed, when there came a halt. One per- son, who had walked comfortably alongside of our port side, sarcastically inquired if a fly had got in our way and stopped our progress. The driver might have been able to answer, but he happened to be uhderneath the engine.. In time he emerged,' covered with dust, his face black with grimy oil and his clothes smelling like a petrol well. "Engine just a little out o' gear," explained our philosophical American guide. "It's all right now, and, once we come to that rise over there,. we'll show what the 'bus can do. It has clinalTed a steeper hill at fifteen miles an hour." In-'our innocence we believed him. Once more the vehicle got on its way, and, without undue haste, the hill was reached. The guide's com- placent smile spoke, without him using any un- necessary words. It indicated that we were going to see the motor do something wonderful at last.

All Hands to the Push. Gallant was the charge the 'bus made at the hill. Onward rushed the gallant six cwt. (that was the unladen weight, I was told), it meant to take the hill,- or stop. It stopped after a struggle of 25 yards. Under the engines again popped the driver-mechanic. Out he came, and in again he went, and the noted Harry Tate could not bestow more attention upon the engine of his stage motor car than the driver gave to the 'bus's petrol propeller. The conductor of the party was still philosophic. "The sparkling plug's got choked with the dust," he explained. "It'll soon be all right." A waggish brother journalist -inquired if the dust corrosion was due to that raised by the 'bus itself, or from the feet of people who had passed us on the way. The guide winked knowingly, unearthed a box of cigars and set us smoking. Perhaps he argued on the lines of a mother, who gives her baby a dummy feeder to suck and stop the cry- ing, and thought with a twopenny cigar in our mouths we would not be able to ask awkward questions. We sat and sat, and no start. Finally we got off the 'bus, and, telling the driver to come out from below, we put our shoulders to the wheels and pushed the 'bus to the hill top. •

===== 0002601_19130802_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
The Train to the Rescue.

Then actually the engine started. "It will be all right now," happily the guide told us. "It's a fine engine, but, like human beings, it has funny little ways. A marked peculiarity of that engine is that it hesitates to take the ' first hill, but once it has got over the first rise, it seems to become proud of the feat, and runs along like greased-lightning the rest of the dtty. Have another cee-gar." We took a cigar to assist our meditations over the almost human- like foibles of this motor 'bus engine. If we suffered on that journey, why should I pile the agony upon my readers with full details of our adventures? Suffice it to say, we did make progress for a time. We did about two mites in thirty minutes. Then variety was" added to the daring dash by an alarming crash, somewhere about the engine. The driver examined the damage, .his report was a broken crank and a bent piston. "Sorry this mishap," said our guide, "should have occurred before you had really seen is hat the 'bus can do, but you see we cannot always rely upon machinery; no man can make it per- fect. You might give us a hand to push the 'bus to the roadside." _

• 4"..• V...., ,61,44,111.7,64../• We gave the necessary help, tramped two miles to a station and got a train home. The 'bus was towed to its depot by two horses. If it is not now in the British Museum, as a motor 'bus curiosity, it ought to be. Enormous changes have, however, been effected since then, and Warrington, by waiting, has reaped the advantage of the improvements. The new motor 'buses now running are of the latest pattern; they ate certainly a credit to the town traffic department. I may frequently ride upon them; in fact, ever since the first time I rode upon one, motor 'buses have fascinated me, but I have bow made up my mind that the stirring adventures of the first "joy" ride on the ancients pioneer of motor 'buses are not ' likely to be repeated. Warrington motor con- veyances are too respectable and reliable for that.

W. D. B.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OFFICIAL RESIDENCE

The yamen is invariably enclosed between. high walls with the gateway opening on the south side. The gate, whien is of wood, con-s sista of three portions, the central or bigger portion is reserved for the mandarin and his guests of equal or higher degree, the two smaller gates for his servants and subordin- ate officials. The offices and all apartments where busi- ness is transacted are in the front court of the :Tamen, the doineltie, a .ments beingfour in well-kept courtyard. - to oe trweealrv,e sets f the" "harem" rem having generally apartments surrounding a i commodious and The wives of a mandarin are grrded into classes. The "first class" or only legtiniate wife is generally left at the ancestral home during- the absence k-if her lord and master, for as no mandarin may act in his own province he i% often too 'far away to look after the interests of his family, ana a " second' class wife is taken to the temporary official residence. MORNING ABLUTIONS.

part

The mandarin does not waste time over his toilet in the—m-Orning, for he sleeps in his clothes, merely removing the outer garment and loosing the waist band, collar and ank- lets' Of his linen: Tha first thought on rising is to brush his teeth, and the necessary ap- paratus consist of a large mug, a toligue scraper and a brush, the operation being carried on in the courtyard. All ablutions are performed in the open, and manj, of the arrangements -would seem to us be still a savagely primitive state. A great institu- tion in China is the rag" which next makes its appearance. It , 3s, thank good- ness! an institution quite peculiar to that country, for to Europc.an ideas it is distinct- ly unhygienic. It is simply an old dish-cloth , soused in boiled water, and used by each member of the family in turn. With it the mandarin washes his face, head, neck and arms, then gathers in his trousers at; the ankles, puts on his robe and leggings, andi with a general hitch together is ready. for his morning meal. Pending its arrival he enjoys a smoke. Breakfast, consisting of tea, barley cakes, sesame rolls and sweetened biscuits is soon despatched, and the sedan chair is brought to the door, in which, after issuing orders to his various secretaries, the high official is carried off to transact his daily routine. If. be is a person of great at-- count his process:on through the town seems little short of a triumphal march. First come the rold-clearers with their long whips, which they do not scruple to use with or carriers,' foot guards', high executioner, etc., while another long string of servants wifbout provocation; next in order come the gong beaters, banner bearers, title bear- ers, mounted guards, regalia bearers, rac,ea state gong nad gbeoaetes , incenser fal and foot soldiers fall in behind the chair and bring up the rear of 'the procession. • OFFICIAL VISITS.

The official visits may last many hours; this personage,' being an influential man, must be conciliated, that one heckled, ex- cuses and Oreats exchanged, until, to the same accompuinmnt of .gongs and ear split- ting: noises clic mandarin once more _arrives at his own tom. _

However ired he may be he cannot sit down to his dinner yet, There will probabli be his granamother and mother to visiC—S mark of respect paid .twic,e a day, and it is not until 't;sii;%, elaborat-,? -Compliments have been passed between them, and the official robes exchanged for some less costly that the high-born one is at liberty to call 'for hia pipe and prepare for dinner. A FRUGAL MEAL.

It would be a sign of unutterable .disre- speci for either his wife or son to sit in their lord's presence, therefore he usually dines alone, unless some visitor of high degree happens to be in the neighbourhood. In that case many dainties are placed before him, sea-slugs, sharks fins and birds' nests,. although when alone the daily menu is usual-- ly extremely frugal. Many a mandarin, even in this comparatively enlightened age. simply squats back on his heels' and wita bowl and chopsticks in hands, gulps down his rice like the meanest beggar at his gate. There are none of the dainty table ap-. pointments with -Which we are familiar, not even those which we look upon as absolute necessaries, no table cloth or napkin, no turives, forks yo,—; halt or pene-_- _ fore him taining small quantities whilegreat cabbage,at salt duck, pork and shrimps, bowls of rice and soup in which float eggs of a good old age aro conspicuous among the dainties on h.* board. After he has done jus- , tico to his meal he washes it down with a few caps of tea, smokes a pipe and the, feei ' ready for a sleep. DUTIES AT TUE COURT.

t sour

From this he rises refreshed; to attend to Isis duties at the cou.s.t, where usually there are many cases waiting for his decision. This often occup:es many hours, and it will probably be five o'clock when at last be finds time for a little relaxation. Relaxation -he

may get, but exercise is prohibited, for un- der no circumstances may he been eeu on foot in the province over which he rules. His only recreation therefore is reading poetry, listening to music—if it can be dignified by the nanie—drinking wine, playing cards, or, if he feels particularly energetic be may spend a few tours will' bow and arrow in the garden.

THE POOR ARE TAXED AND RATED.

Indirect Taxation.

BY C. E.

Mr. F. W. Kolthammer, M.A., has just pub- lished an interesting memoranda on the Incidence of Taxation on the Working Class Family- At first this might seem to be a simple task, as the necessary figures can,' to a great .extent, be obtained from the publica- tions of the Board of Trade, which give in detail a reliable basis of the average consump- tion of food of a, sufficiently .large :number of families, earning a variety of wages, in differ- pt parts of the country. From these the 4"-iter separates the consumption of taxed s, and calculates the taxes so paid in pro- ion to the incomes.

LOCAL TAXATION. So far, by the hell) of Mr. Kolthammer, I haVe dealt with Imperial taxes only. Nor can a small minority of workinclass families in four per Cent. of their income there be much doubt that we must add to these percentages a part of the shopkeepers' rates, for part is certainly thrown forward on to con- sumers generally.' He gives the following example to illustrate and confirm his conclusion:— ~ A certain row of houses in an urban area returns to its owner a gross 10 per cent. The ,tenants pay 265. per quarter. or 2s. per week, i.e., 2s. of the rent goes in rates.

SUOMI ILLUSTRATES AN ANOMALY.

The complexity of the problem may be illus- trated by such an article as sugar. It is easy from the figures available to get approximately at the amount of sugar consumed, by average families, but the indirect consumption of sugar

is considerable, and has to be taken into account and added to the direct consumption. Sugar is one of the ingredients in syrup, beer, jam, confectionery, condensed milk, &c. The problem the writer, has attempted is therefore one of considerable difficulty. We will not follow the details of this investigation, but only give the' broad results. This is best accom- iiiished by reproducing the diagram supplied in the publication, which illustrates the follow- ing,fact.- The lower the family income the IS-S the expenditure as a whole, but the greater the percentage of the income taken in taxes. In other words, regression is practically con- tinuous. The lower the standard of comfort the larger the percentage of expenditure" on taxed food. The smaller the amount available to purchase the necessary food the greater the proportion of the foods subject to taxation.-1 Thus the cheaper jams containing taxed sugar displace the untaxed butter or margarine, as also the cheaper condensed milk displaces the tax-free but dearer cow's milk. Thus necessity drives the family consumption to some extent from -untaxed articles 'on to the taxed. The bottom line in the diagram illustrates the per- centage of taxation paid.: by variously paid workers in the food stuffs they buy. -

7aXO'hal7 c 75

selection of inferior taxed food, but the sudden change (especially in the food line) in the direction at 225. must be the result of inability to buy sufficient. It must be remembered that oasis of these lines are the result of aver= ages of large number of family budgets. In individual cases actual shortage in food may, and probably does, begin considerably above the 225. limit. /The BOard of Trade statistics show that while the consumption of taxed food may be greater, proportionately at tire poorer end, it is not greater in amount, and the only explanation I can offer for this is that below a certain income level appreciably less food is consumed. It, therefore, we adopt the prin- ciple that all citizens should contribute to the management of the nation in proportion to their income, that the load should be propor- tionate to the strength of the back, the present system is seen to be very unfair. According to their ability and earnings, the financial bur- den of citizenship falls proportionately heaviest on the poorest, and on those least abl#to bear it, while those who pay the least proportion- ately are those just under the Income Tax level. Before- we come to any exception to this general rule we have to bring into account the Super Tax payers.

BEYOND iiislATiitivs CONTROL.

A boy fourteen years of age was charged at SL Helens on Monday by his father with F-teal- ing. a pair of boots, value 3s. 6d. They boy had attempted to pledge the boots, and, failing to do so, had thrown them away. ECCLESTON HALL

TO BE CONVERTED INTO A SANATORIUM. The St. Helens Corporation has decided to acquire Eccleston Hall and its spacious grounds for the purpose of a tuberculosis sanatorium, and to that end is seeking powers to borrow the sums of 5,100 and £7,900. -A Local Government Board inquiry into the matter was held at the St. Helens Town Hall on Wednesday by Dr. R. A. Farrar. ' Dr. .Buchan, medical officer, said that there would be accommodation for fifty-eight patients at the hall, and there would be a resident medical officer, a matron six nurses and six servants.

Dr. Farrar: Is your provision entirely for in- sured persons? Dr. Buchan: It ij be for insured persons, their dependants, and non-insured persons. The hospital, Dr. Buchan added, would be primarily for the treatment of hospital cases, but it would also be available for early cases. The ..conditions Eccleston Ball were .e;ccep- tional, and cJnsiderably better than was usual nt -tuberculosis sanatoria. The site Was an excellent one,-and the need for an early cura- live sanatorium would not be so great owing

to the ample accommodation at .the ball. There was no 'opposition to the 'application. Dr.-Farrar said there would be no opposition from the Board, it was simply a matter now of going into one or two details. In the matter of a grant from the Treasury he was empow- ered to say that the Board desired that the St. Helens Corporation should have generous, and if possible, exceptional treatment.

JAMES SEXTON-CANDIDATE.

A FIGHTING LABOUR MAN.

Mr. James Sexton, secretary of the Liver- pool Dockers' Union, is prospective Labour candidate for St. Helens. "Jim," as he is known to his friends, will put up a keen fight in the division, for he is a born fighter. There are few better known personages in Liverpool, and whenever there is an industrial fight he is sure to be in the thick-'of it. Dur- ing the docker? strikes he was in his element, but, although he is a hard hitter when the blood is up, he acts reasonably, and has done a good deal to bring about harmonious rela- tions between employer and employed. A member of the Liverpool City Council, he is conspicuous in debates there, and ts.appre- ciately -listened -to by all, no matter how they may - differ personally from his views and politics. He is' the leader of the Labour group in the Council, and was nfade a J.P. by the Liberal Government.' • -

CHESHIRE MODEL SCHOOL.

: The Cheshire County -Council has just com- pleted a 'model rural elementary school for the county at liaslington, near Crewe, and this will be affiliated with the new training college for 'teachers which has been erected about a mile distant. The new buildings will be opened next week by Colonel Dixon (chairman of the County Council). Students from the new training college will take courses of practical teaching in this school in elementary chemistry, botany and gardening and a portion of the curriculum will include the examination of seeds, roots, plants, &c., and the various descriptions and qualities of soils, &c. A large plot in connection with the school grounds is reserved for school gar- dens. This will be sub-divided into separate patches and sown-with all the various kinds of seeds, cuttings, fruit trees, &c., in which the scholars will receive practical demonstration lessons on the culture of flowers, corn, vege- tables, fruit, &C. There is provided also a handicraft centre for the combined teaching of both woodwork and metal work.
