THE BARREL OF GOLD.

Retold from the French of Anatole le Braze

BY H. M.

It was the height of the sardine fishery one evening in July; the boats were returning, one by one, and amid a sonorous creaking of quickly lowered sails, ranged alongside the quays of the_harbour,stretch- ing themselves out likeiired beasts at the accustomed hour of rest. This daily homecoming of the fishing fleet during the busy season supplied the one amusement of the little town, always bringing some news from the little sea- ports round about. Just as in inland towns one goes to the railway station to see the trains go through, so here everyone went to the quays to assist at the arrival of the boats. And there is nothing more majestic or captivating. On the splendid calm of the water it wends its way along in the rays of the setting sun like an immense caravan. The large foresails of a deep tan red, glow with, beautiful tints, and even the hard faces of the sailors have a noble and majestic look, and when the boats pour out their myriads of glittering sardines the women who pile them up and measure them in baskets seem to be stirring up a marvellous booty of pearls, carbuncles and jewels. This evening there-was a great crowd on the quays, the girls were especially in evidence, as the incoming tide which brought back the fleet coincided with the close of the day's work in the canneries. One hundred and fifty boats had unloaded, the men being overwhelmed with jokes and questions' when at the approach of the hundred and fifty-first a rumour went round and rapidly spread from group to group through the crowd. Something had happened on board the "Espere en Dieu." What? Nobody could say exactly, but it was very serious, very serious indeed. One of the sailors was nearly dead, another had lost a lot of blood, the master had his head bandaged the cabin boy—in brief, it was a floating hospital. and the rumours kept increasing. The "Espere en Dieu," however, came alongside, and the master jumped ashore. He had a very wild appearance, his clothes were torn and dirty,, and a handkerchief soaked in sea-water covered half his face. "Hallo, Gavendel, what's the matter? You're knocked abont," said the syndic. "Yes, I am," saieGavendel, "but the business is not finished, they wish the Commissary to settle the matter. We're going there now." And without any further explanation, he called out to the two sailors, "Come Mireux, come Trefentec, this is not the time to slip away." But neither of them had any intqition of slipping away. What had been said was said, but they must certainly make themselves a little more presentable, for the fact was they had a great need of it. Both of them were covered with wounds and bruises,their faces were scratched, their noses swollen, and skin ripped with bloody furrows, which their washing , with salt water had greatly aggravated. Cleaned up as much as possible they strode along the quay. As they followed in the steps of the master, Leontine Capderert, the girl engaged to Joachim Miroux, crept up to her lover. "It is really flattering you," sneered she, "but you really look like an ugly piece of butcher's meat." He took no notice of the compliment, but contented himself with saying between his teeth, "Don't forget 'that w'e -are in the right, for sure, I and Gab Trefen tec,andi stake my head on it." "You! to talk of your bead, it's a shame, andlor what have you got yourself into such a mess ? " "My faith, it was all about a barrel of

gold." " What ! a barrel of gold ? " Stupid with astonishment, the gir remained silent and walked behind the sailor waiting for him to explain this strange affair. But he was anxious to join his companions, who had distanced him, and began to push about with his elbows to force a passage through the dense crowd; so that the girl, surrounded by eager questioners, was obliged to confess that all she knew was that there was a barrel of gold in the matter. That was enough, the I report flew from mo'th to mouth like lightning. And to the anxiety which suddenly seized everyone at the sight of the bruised and bloody sailors, succeeded a wonderful exaltation which .caused the crowd to press toward the town. In the twinkling of an eye the quays were deserted. All the activity of the little seaboard town was concentrated on the Square, decorated with a few poor elms which gave it an appearance of verdure, before the offices of the Commissary. Surprised by the sudden crowd, the shopkeepers, who were just _putting up the shutters, came out to seek information. "What's all this commotion about?" " What ! don't you know? A barrel of gold, M. Pouliguen." "These are the men of the "Espere en Dieu," who have found a barrel of gold t" "As if that were possible!" "Sure and certain, they have nearly been killed on account of it and are hasten- ing to the Commissary to declare it." "A barrel of gold, did you say? You mean a small keg perhaps ? " "No, a barrel, a barrel ! " "Have you seen it?" "Seen it?. Well, not exactly, but mon Dieu, Joachim Miroux wouldn't tell a lie to his best girl, would be?" But others came along who repeated the actual circum- stances. There is hardly a country in which tradition or myth •hatches more spontaneously than in Brittany. There were those who soon gave the exact dimen- sions of the barrel and valued the fortune which it represented. They were not the less certain as to the manner of its finding. Miroux, it appeared, in drawing in his net, had felt some resistance like a heavy body floating even with the water; then he had called Gab Trefentec to lend him a hand in good fellowship and the barrel had emerged. Upon my word, it had not been easy to hoist it on board. You can imagine what an awful weight it was and what stupefac- tion when they had knocked in the bung They had expected to see sherry or brandy or some other wine spout out. Yes! but it was gold which had poured out, bright yellow gold, clear and ringing, a flood of yellow boys! "Piaqtres or rupees," as serted Captain Guevel, an old salt, who having knocked about on every sea had definite opinions on all things ; and he added to the amaze- ment of the audience : "The rupee is worth nearly 40 francs." For him the adventure was nothing out

ASHTON.

of the common. Such a windfall had happened ten times, if not to himself, at least to people he had known, down in the Southern Countries. And he could explain it mathematically. Didn't they go in ancient times to America to seek for vessels of gold just as they go to Norway, nowadays, to seek frir barrels of cod! And how many of these s .ips, badly built, badly sailed, and oadly captained, went down! Why, no farther off than Vigo, on the coasts of Spain, a whole fleet was engulfed, immense riches lie there 300 fathoms deep, a cemetery of millions which would give a King's income to all fisher- men present and to come. Supposing a hull is dislodged by some submarine disturbance,the cargo lightened, rises, is set adrift, and is caught by the first current it encounters,and note that the currents come through the Bay of Biscay and there you are And the demonstra- tion appeared so convincing that nobody had the slightest doubt that the barrel in question came in a straight line from the galleons of Vigo. As for the battle it had stirred up, one need not be very smart to divine the motives of it. Discussions on the division evidently. Gavandel was known to be greedy. It was on him that everybody threw the blame. "You will see that under the pretext that he is the owner he will have said that half must be his." "The half? more likely the whole." The three men had turned the angle of the square and reached the kind of carriage entrance above which was displayed, sur- mounted by an anchor, the bold inscrip- tion of the maritime registry. An ante- chamber reserved for the public preceded the offices. It was a big dreary place, on the walls hung the tattered remains of official notices, the floor was filthy and damp with the imprint of sabots smelling of sardines and the sea. There was no one there except the gendarme Moreau. who slept half his time in a chair with a kepi on his knees and his tunic unbuttoned. The entry of the three sailors made him open one eye, and he had no sooner spied those battered and bruised heads than he called

"Well, my beauties? " "We are rather dirty," said Gavandel, somewhat abashed, "but we should like to see the Commissary at onee." The gendarme laughed hugely and spluttered : " The Commissary, ha, ha, that's good, it's his hour for card-playing, ha, ha !" Then between his ha, ha's— " But he mustn't miss this ; wait a minute, I'll go and find him." He got up, buttoned his coat and rushed out into the street puffing like a grampus. Gavandel sat down by the window on the bench which ran round the hall, and at the other side sat the two gold-finders Joachim Miroux and Gab Trefentec. The rages of seamen resemble the storms of summer in that they burst and pass quickly away. Our friends, forgetting the blows exchanged, began to ask each other, not without some vexation, what the devil they had come to find in this gloomy hole, when it would have been so easy for them to have made the peace before a flagon in the neighbouring inn. The tumultuous laughter of the gendarme had acted upon them like a cold douche. They suddenly saw the ridiculousness of their position; moreover, the place seemed like a judgment hall, and they didn't feel sure that their litigation was worth all the trouble. The Commissary would most likely consider that they had spoilt his game of cards for a very insignificant trifle, and, if he took it ill, there would be the devil to pay, for the Commissary was not always a pleasant man. If only they had been free to walk out! Just at this moment the gendarme re-appeared. He had not had to go far, the syndic having been before him. "These gentlemen," said he, "will be here directly." He was no longer the facetious Moreau, but grave and solemn and not without some bearing of respect and admiration. His short absence had evidently changed his ideas, and it was easy to see that the three beauties he had so recently bantered had, during his absence, taken in his eyes a prestige which transformed into glorious scars the grotesque maulings of their battered faces. " Ah, my friends," said he, "you did not tell me that—mon Dieu ; it is not to us of the gendarmerie that such a piece of luck happens." The two sailors looked at one another astonished. The Captain believing it to be a new form of sarcasm, shrugged his shoulders and growled.

"Don't you humbug yourself, M.Moreau, it might happen to you sooner than you think; a few blows of the fist and there you are 1 " "Yes, yes, but look what you've got for it ! "

"Got what?" "You understand! But here comes the Commissary," said he, in a low voice, saluting in military fashion. The Commissary entered, followed by the syndic. He opened the door of his private office, and, addressing the men of the " Espere en Dieu, " with a jovial familiarity he was not accustomed to use, he said, "Come, my brave fellows, come in, tell me all about it." The poor devils could hardly be called brave at that moment. They walked in single file to the rug which furnished the floor of the office. There Gavandel hesitated, as before an insurmountable obstacle, a kind of sacred barrier. He began to remove his sabots, as a mark of respect and to gain time. "Tut tut," said the Commissary, who had taken his seat at his desk, "not so much fuss." He even begged them to sit down, yes, to sit down in his presence—luxurious seats, which the breeches of sailors had never before touched. They sat down with embarrassment, in the awkward and con- strained attitudes of penguins out of their element. The gendarme and the syndic stood in the doorway with uncovered heads. A few moments' solemn silence ensued. Then the Commissary spoke. "Let us proceed in order," said he. "I know most of the story; the syndic told me a little, and Captain Guevel reported, on the way, what he had learnt of the matter. But it is from you that I must get the whole story and take down your declaration. Before interrogating you, it is my duty to recall to your minds the instructions of the maritime code relative to wrecks." "That's it," exclaimed Gavandel, with visible satisfaction, "explain the law to these imbeciles, for they are asses, truly, asses I " He had recovered his anger, and with it, some little assurance. The Commissary smiled and continued in a benevolent tone: "Listen then, how the laws of 1681 and 1791 express the rules of the matter." Miroux and Trefentecucrossed their arms and stood like statues of granite. The Commissary read in a loud voice,

===== 0002601_19130802_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
"Goods lost in the sea by storm or other- wise and afterwards recovered are sold by the administration, a third of the proceeds of the sale [you understand well, a third] is allotted to the finder, and the remaining two-thirds go to the State, if no one puts in a legal claim for a year and a day. In this case, I don't think you have any to fear." The three men interpret- ing the observation as a little pleasantry, smiled. "Oh, no," said they with one voice, I wiping their lips with theif caps. The Commissary proceeded: "So you quite understand, don't you? You are three finders if I am not mistaken. You will have a third to divide among you. You have then each a right to a ninth part of the goods recovered." "There you are," cried Miroux and Trefentec, triumphantly pointing to Gavandel. The master, livid with rage, bounded from his chair and shouted, "Excuse me, Mr. Commissary, but you have forgotten the portion of the boat." "The portion of the boat ? " "Certainly, it has a right to its share, hasn't it?" The Commissary replied in a soothing tone, "In your rules of the fishery, certainly, my dear Gavandel. The boat with its gear and apparatus is an instrument of work, an essential implement; it represents, in your association, the capital; it is according to this title that it receives its remuneration. That's quite right, but here the case is different. The " Espere en Dieu " has not been specially fitted for the seeking of wrecks, that I know ; it happens on one by chance. That's nothing to do with the boat. The finders have their share, as I have stated, and that's an end of the matter." "Certainly," said the twb sailors. Gavandel gave them a furious look, and U ming his back on them sat down again,

saying, " Then the law is not right. No. no, Mr. Commissary, you may break my head in pieces like a flint by the roadside, hut you'll never make me understand that". " It is on account of this proposal that you fought among yourselves? Ha! you are all the same." "Yes, and I am ready to begin again, there should be justice for boats as well as for men." "That's enough," said the Commissary, who waxed impatient. And as Gavandel rose to go out he harshly ordered him to sit down. "Let us pass to the declaration, please." Perceiving that the master was no more use to him, with his savage obstinacy, he addressed himself now to Miroux, whom public rumour had pointed to as the chief finder of the marvellous wreck. "Come, my friend, tell me what you know." The sailor blushed furiously, and nudging his companion with his elbow, said, "You tell, Trefentec." But Trefentec's mouth was all swollen and his lower jaw was grievously wounded. He excused himself. " Well," said the Commissary, "when you are ready, I am waiting." So Miroux was obliged. The affair happened simply enough. They had come into the bay across the islands, the fishing being ended. He, Joachim Miroux, was at the helm, with Trefentec at his side. They were talking on trivial matters to kill time, for the breeze had died down. Then apropos of nothing, Trefentec had begun to tease him about Leontine Capdevert, his betrothed, asking him how soon they were going to be married. Then he had answered, "When I have found the barrel of gold which they say floats in the waters of the islands." "A most singular coincidence," said the Commissary. Miroux thought he saw an opening for a digression. "It's a woman's story, you understand. People believe that in the waters of the islands between the Chevre and the Jument rocks, ever since a storm many years ago—" "Yes, yes, come ba3k to the facts." " He bad just mentioned the barrel of gold when the master, who was dozing close at hand with one eye open, called out, Oh, but you wouldn't have it all, you'd have to divide it.' " ' That's true,' said Trefentec, there would be my share, your share and that of the master.' "Well, that was right enough, the law is the law, isn't it? You may imagine that after that the master had no more to say,but you doti't know Pierre Charles Gavandel. He swore he had a right to two shares, one for himself and another for his boat." Never in this life, had Trefentec declared and Miroux had supported him. Hence the dispute. They would go to the Commissary. Gavandel knew he was right, they were asses—he was a fool, and finally a free fight, blood and oaths, the apprentice and the cabin boy,watehing from under a sailcloth and applauding the best blows! " What I do you mean to say that you were fighting before you had even dis- covered the barrel ? " "It was not before, that's sure" "Not! what are you driving at, fellows ?" In the doorway the syndic and the gendarme were almost choking to prevent themselves crying out. " Well," said the Commissary,-:" where and when did you discover it?" "Discover it ?" repeated the young fisherman, opening his stupid eyes ; while Gavandel, who had pretended not to listen to the sailor's narrative, suddenly becoming attentive, said, "Discover what ?" "The barrel, man, good heavens, do you intend to keep us here till to-morrow ?" Miroux looked at Trefentec, who looked at Gavandel. "What barrel Mr. Commissary ? " "What barrel? Why the barrel of gold ! " "But there is no barrel of gold ! " "No barrel of gold ?What are you say- ing? " You have found no barrel of gold? It was now the turn of the Commissary to be mystified. Said Miroux, "We should not be here, you may imagine, if we had found it." The Commissary•bounded from his chair in a rage, white to the lips, his eyes althost starting from his head. "It's for this, then, that you have dis- turbed me, it's for this that you upset the w hHo el e toouwl dn g, ot hnaot —fur"ther, it was too much for him. The words stuck in his throat. The three men trembled like leaves (Continutd, n next witimn.) ; they

I "SPEECH DAY."

to link up, for educational purposes, different parts of the country into areas. The only fault ever found with the Act of 1902 was that it did not make Lancashire one area."

===== 0002601_19130802_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
CO-ORDINATION WANTED.

If the urban districts,- non-county boroughs and county boroughs had been combined, con- tinued Sir Henry, there would have been in- finitely greater co-ordination, and certainly they would have saved money in the adminis- tration of education. He hoped that when the Government did something on the lines sug- gested they would attempt to do something definitely to co-ordinate education in the County Palatine. (Applause.) He hoped to see that, although he was not at all dissatisfied with the way education was being carried on in their county area, and in the county boroughs like Warrington. The destiny of the county, and secondly .the destiny of the country, depended, to a larger or lesser degree, upon the quality of the education given to the children. • Sir Henry extended his congratulations to the staff on the success which had attended the sehoOl in the past year, and he congratulated the prize7winners ,upon their success.

EDUCATIONAL EXTRAVAGANCE-WHERE

THE FAULT LIES.

"In these days," proceeded Sir Henry, "when enormous sums are spent on. education, there is an enormous amount of criticism. I rarely 1.:0 on to the floor of the . Manchester 'loyal Exchange without being taunted with the so- called . extravagance Of the Education Coin- mittee of the Lancashire County :Council. I should' indignantly derl such a. Charge if this were a proper place to do So. I content myself, however, with saying undoubtedly a large amount of money is wasted on education, but the fault is not with those who have to ad- minister, but that rather lies with those who fail to take advantage of the opportunities offered. (Hear, hear.) I believe if we are to maintain our,position as a commercial country it is absolutely necessary that our boys, who will be our merchants and manufacturers of to-morrow,-should be thoroughly well equipped educationally for the battle of life." (Applause.)

TIM UNEDUCATED BUT tSUCCESSFUL MAN.

There were three reasons why he supported a good education. They must live, and there- fore they must educate, their people so that they could gain their lii.elihood. On that first ground education was absolutely necessary. He had met successful men who said they never had received education, who proudly said, "Of education I have had none at all, and see the sacess I am.". The man who made that remark entirely forgot the fact that to a large extent he had made his money by using the brains of other people. Machinery which had helped him was the production of educated genius.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATION IN NIACIPAL LIFE.

Even in municipal affairs, continued Sir. Henry, science had now stepped in. In order that public undertakings, many of them of a semi-scientific character, could be successfully carried out by municipalities, they must have educated members of corporations. It did not do to rely entirely upon permanent officials, no matter how expert. Thirdly, education brought joy into' life, and it should make people courteous and respect- able, bAide bringing enjoyments of an intel- lectual character within 'everyone's' reach. In this. conneetimi Sir Henry paid a tribute to the parental sacrillce. -,vltiCh. the 'lmparting Of sound education to the cgildren often in- volved, and he made' special mention' of the incalculable value of a good mother's influence, Wisely exerted.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
A MOTHER'S INFLUENCE

NV itliout venturing upon controversial aspects of the question, he said there was an enormous field in the world for feminine influence. "The hand that rocks the cradle rules the world," and he did not think the average woman real- ised the great influence she had for good. He appealed to parents, as one who had the best interests of his country at heart, to continue to make sacrifices. They would be rewarded, not only by the satisfaction of knowing that they had done their duty to their own children, but

by the fact that they had contributed a very valuable asset to the national welfare. He was pleased to hear that the duration of school life was longer in that school than in most schools he had visited. As an 'educationalist, and as an . Englishman, he thanked the mothers of Warrington for that, because he was sure they were at the bottom of it. "Persevere on those

_ lines,' added Sir .Henry. "You 'have_ macle sacrifiees. Make a few more and you will be abundantly rewarded." (Applause.)

"ARBITERS OF YOUR OWN FUTURE."

Sir Henry took strong exception, to the com- mon saying, "Enjoy yourself now; because you will only be young once"— as untrue an expres- sion, he said, as had ever been voiced. He had passed his sixth decade and he. could enjoy himself quite as much to-day as ever he could. (Applause.) The character of one's en- joyment altered with the passing years, but enjoyment was always possible if one had a. sound mind in a sound body. !` Don't make it an, excuse," continued Sir Henry, "when you ought to be doing your evening lessons and want to go to sonic place of amusement, that' you must go now.. because you can only he a boy once. -Remember this—that at :no period in your. .life will you.be\.able Jo* acquire know- ledge as easily as you do when you are Young, and that if you do. not take advantage of your ' Opportunities now. the day is, riot far distant when you will bitterly regret it. ,In these democratic. ..day-4._ a.reJ. -practically the. arbiters of your own future, and your ultimate success in life depends to a very large extent upon the education, you get now, and the habits you form. Therefore, as good boys and as trying to give some return to your parents, let me ask you to- throw your whole heart and soul into the lessons which are set you by your masters, and I can assure you, On, the woyd of one who has had a great deal of responsibility, and who was not born with a silver spoon in his mouth, that you will never regret it." (Applause.)

OLD INSTITUTIONS HOLDING THEIR OWN

Alderman SNIF:THeIiST" proposed a vote of thanks, to Sir Henry Hibbert, aiki appealed to the boys to take .to heart the excellent advice which he had given them. "Never turn against your fathers and Mothers," he said, and always be true to your sisters and brothers." 'Applause.) _ _

E. C. E. CARLETON Se(.ollCled. He said the 'old institutions of the country—at any rate, Liaise that were worth preserving—were still holding their own, and had mot altogether succumbed to modern competition. As re- garded education, he did not think the time had come when they could do without that institution, which had now been in existence for some 400 years, and, in point of 'age, might be classed with the important, Grammar School of Manchester. (Applause.) They probably could not compete with ,modern educationists in the type of buildings they provided, but it should also be borne In mind that they did not spend as much money. However, they got the results which, after all, were the best test of usefulness. (Applause.) • The vote was .heartily accorded.

A CITIZEN'S DUTIES. Prize Essay by a Grammar School

Boy.

The Warrington Chamber of Commerce offered two prizes of .a guinea each to boys of the Boteler Grammar School for essays on "The duties of citizenship." The best paper sent in from the Upper School was written by J. C. F. Fern, of Legh-street, and the prize essayist in the lamer School is Frank Ldwe. Below we print Frank LOWe'S -prize essay:

===== 0002601_19130802_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DUTIES OF CITIZENSHIP.

This means the duties which belong to a citizen. The question arises, What is a citizen? it is an individual who has banded himself with other men into a community, and to be a good citizen is one of the noblest aspirations that can animate a human being. Very many years ago men banded themselves together principally for fighting purposes. Nowadays, i whilst it s equally true they do so for such a purpose, the chief reason for their doing so is for friendly intercourse. Shakespeare says: "To thine own self be true, and it must follow as the night the day, thou canst not then be false to any man." As regards the duties which are involved on a citizen himself he has to think of the best possible means by which he can keep his body, mind, and soul in a healthy condition; healthy in the sense that his limbs have natural freedom; healthy in'the sense' that' hie mind is alert and interested in things about him; healthy in the sense that his sour is at peace. That is doing justice to himself in every way. It will be part of his duty to give play its proper place, to mix it with his work, as it were, in the proportions that make for perfect health. As head of his household, on him rests the responsibilities of educating his children morally, intellectually, and spiritually, for their respective positions in life. If they would be builders of houses, educate them for building houses, if they would be builders of nations, educate them to that end. Early training makes or mars the man. If "An Englishman's home is his castle," it behoves him as king of that castle to rule it wisely and kindly, and gain respect and love by his family. There are points a citizen should observe. It will be a part of his education to find out what elections are about, why people have votes, and how they are used. Elect God-fearing, high- principled men to govern the affairs of the community. A right-minded citizen will attend his Church regularly and always be ready to support it in every possible way; he will see that the streets -are wide, and clean and well kept, that the loafers do not live on honest people. and that the deserving poor are helped and given employment, and encourard fo be temperate, thrifty, and clean in their homes. England stands as the greatest nation for Peace, which we must try to maintain, and it is by our peaceful relations that we have gained the strong position we now hold. As a citizen of a peaceful nation it is part of his duty to build up the Emnire. All cannot be prominent Statesmen, but all can at least be loyal to their King and country, and pay due observance to its laws. Then again he ought to select suitable members for Parliament and give them his full support. One has only to point to the " Baden-Powell" Scout movement to recognise the fact what good is being done by him. General Baden-Powell has said that his object, his highest object. is to help the boys to become good citizens. We are nroud that on record in the late Boer War. Lord Roberts, Generals Buller, Kitchener and White fought so bravely for their 'country. ' Livingstone dying aniOng the poor savages, -Gordon's lonely sacrifices at Khartoum, '• and, more recently, Cantain Scott and Captain Oates, who lost their lives on the Polar Expedi- tion. • Lives of great men all remind us That our lives should be sublime; And departing. leave behind us Footprints on the sands of time.

MR. AND MRS. LONG ON TB

Cordial congratulations have this week been extended to Mr. and Mrs. William Long, of Thelwall Heys, Grappenhall, and Cleabarrow, Windermere, on the celebration of their golden weddir g anniversary. It was on July 28th, 1863, at the picturesque Lakeland church of Grasmere, that Mr. and Mrs. Long were united in the bonds of matrimony by the Vicar of the parish (the late Rev. E. Jeffreys), and they have,

throughout their married life, been closely associated with Warrington and district. Monday was the fiftieth anniversary of their wedding day, and the occasion was marked by a happy gathering of relatives and friends at Thelwall Heys. It was a source of pleasure and satisfaction to all to see Mr. and Mrs. Long looking so well, and good wishes for the future were most sincerely expressed. In addition to messages of congratulation from all quarters, Mr. and Mrs. Long were the recipients of many handsome gifts. They were particularly touched by the manifestation of respect and affection on the part of fifty-three past and present servants, and others-with whom they have been brought into close -contact, who joined in presenting thein with a beautiful carriage clock. The inscription on the clock is as follows : W. L. • E. J. L. 1863. 1913. Presented to Mr. and Mrs William Long by their employees and others who have been in close touch with them for many years on the occasion of their golden wedding - 2 Bth July,

1913. Accompanying the gift was a letter, signed by the subscribers, in the following terms :

_ To Mr. and Mrs. William Long, of Thelwall Heys, near Warrington, and Cleabarrow, Windermere. The undersigned employees and others who have been in close touch with you for many years desire to offer you their sincerest congratula- tions on the 50th anniversary of your wedding day and to wish you still many years of health and happiness. They feel that the occasion of your golden wedding would not be fittingly passed without -expressing their sincere thanks and appreciation for the many acts of kindness and deep interest taken in them at all times during the many years in which they have been connec Led with you. With these expressions of regard and good wishes they ask your acceptance of the accom- panying carriage clock as a slight token of the respect and esteem they have for you both. The Right Hon. Joseph Chamberlain, M.P., and 'Mrs. Chamberlain, who are old friends of Mr. and Mrs. Long, sent a telegram of congratulation,- and a 'gold cup was received from Miss Chamberlain. Mr. Long, who belongs to a well-known Cheshire family, has for many years taken a useful part in county affairs, although his public interests have chiefly centred in the borough of Warrington. Commer- cially his name is, of course, best known as the head of the firm of Messrs. William

PhaGgraph by Birtlea, :IR GOLDEN WEDDING DAY,

Long and Son, tanners, Grappenhall, which was founded by his father. Seven years ago he relinquished active association with the business, and he has, since then, spent the greater part of his time at his beauti- fully situated home in the Lake District. Mr. Long is a justice of the peace for the county of Chester, and for many years pre- sided over the Bench in the Daresbury Division. He is a visiting justice of Knutsford prison. Mr. Long has done a great deal to promote the remarkable pros- perity of the Warrington Savings Bank— an institution which is particularly dear to his heart. He became a trustee in 1879, was elected vice- chairman in 1880, and has held the office of chairman since 1896. During the period of his chairmanship the number of depositors at the bank has increased by 6,000, while another indication of its great progress is that the amount due to deposi- tors has advanced by £140,000. Mr. Long's interest in the intellectual life of Warring- ton is evidenced by the fact that he is the president of the Warrington Society, which has its home in the Old Academy and- has played a very worthy part in preserving the highest traditions of the -town. He was a governor of the Boteler Grammar School, prior to the new conditions of educational administration brought about by the passing of the Education Act of 1902, and he is at present a manager of People's College. Mr. Long is an unostentatious but generous supporter of all deserving insti- tutions and good works. A position which he greatly prizes is that of a vice-president of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispen- sary, and he has always been a cheerful and willing helper of that noble institution. Mr. Long has rendered good service in the parochial life of Grappenhall, and was formerly a member of the Parish Council, and a manager of Grappenhall " school. In his earlier days Mr. Long was a keen follower of the hounds, and even now, at the age of seventy-eight, he delights to ride on horseback. In politics Mr. Long is a staunch Radical, and has often aidvd the progressive cause in this district by logical and persuasive p.atform advocacy. Although, latterly, he has not been so prominently to the fore in political controversy, it is very rarely that any Liberal demonstration of import- ance in Warrington and the neighbourhood is allowed to pass without a message of encouragement from his pen. Mrs. Long is also a member of a family which has long been honoured in our midst. She is the eecond daughter of the late Mr. W. E. Milner, who formerly resided at Springfield, Warrington, and has ably seconded_ the efforts .-of her husband in social and philanthropic spheres. Athong those who took part in the celebra,tions- at Thelwall Heys, on Monday, wcre Mrs. Thornely, (Mr. Lorg's sister) and Miss Milner .(Mrs. Long's sister), who were both bridesmaids at the wedd ng, Mr. and Mrs. W. M. Shipman, of Altrincham (son-in-law and only daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Long), and the Misses Shipman (their three grand-daughters).

===== 0002601_19130802_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FORBIDDEN -FRUIT.

A fruit .supposed to bear the mark of Eve's teeth is one of the many botanical curiosities- of Ceylon. The tree on which it grows is known by the significant name of "the forbidkn fruit," or "Eve's apple-tree." The blossom has a very pleasant scent, but the really remarkable feature of, the tree, the one to which it owes its name, is the fruit. It' is beautiful, and hangs from the, tree in a peculiar manner. Orange on the out- side and deep crimson within, each fruit has the appearance of having had a piece bitten out of it. This fact, together with its poisonous quality, led the -Mohammedans to represent it as the for- bidden fruit of the Garden of Eden, and to warn men against it. The mark upon the -fruit is attributed -to Eve. Why the bite of Adam did .not also leave its mark is not known, but as only one piece seems to be missing, its loss is ascribed to the woman.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
CONCERNING SEA SHELLS.

Walking along the seashore recently, with the gentle waves washing over heaps of small shells, says a correspondent of the Scc?tnnan, I was struck with the richness and brilliancy of their colouring. The general effest was yellow, but on closer examination I 'found such a great variety of shades that it occurred to me to see how many I could collect, graduating the colours with as small a step as possible between each one. Making a start with one which was as nearly as possible black, I worked up threugh many shades of grey till I came to practically snow-white. Then I went on through cream- colour, yellow"-white, and pale yellow, to actual. yellow. Continuing, I gradually added greener yellows, but for some time was unable to get

===== 0002601_19130802_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ABSOIXTE GREEN.

At last, however, I found two shells of a bril- liant grass-green, but this colour was not actu- ally in the shells, as in the case of all the others. It was caused by a vegetable growth, which could be scraped off, and I thought it a fact worthy of notice that these two shells were the only ones in the whole'ooliection which contained the living shell-fish. From this, vivid green I graduated the colours through the olives, to brown, maroon, and deep red shading into orange and yellow again, getting paler and Paler, till at last I arrived back at pure white. The whole array censisted of fifty-three shells, and no doubt, with time and patience, the range of colours could have been still further augmented.

MANAGER OR OWNER?

Messrs. Peter Walker and the Shop

Hours Act.

AN IMPORTANT PROSECUTION.

'An interesting case, which involved a ques- tion of how the ,Shop Hours Ace applied to licensed premises, was .debated before the Liver- pool Stipendiary on Wednesday. As unique points of law were concerned the Stipendiary reserved judgment for a fortnight. "Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, • Ltd., brewers, were summoned for not specifying, on. a notice in licensed premises in ',Rice-lane, Liver- the day of the week in which the shop assistants were to receive a half-holiday. - The brewers contended that the manager was responsible, but the police argued that the manager was technically an assistant, and that his name should appear on the notice. Evi- dence wits detailed' in support of the prosecu- tion. Mr. Rigby Swift, for Messrs. Walker, said he should raise the short and sole .point as to whether Messrs. Walker were the occupiers. The offence was one committed by the occupier of a shop, .and.what they said was that Messrs. Walker were not the occupiers, and he was not going to discuss, the question whether an offence had been 'committed. by anybody else. Mr. Deacon: Who do you suggest is the occupier? • • Mr. Swift: We s 4 that the. Manager, Todd, is the occupier. • Mr. Duder, for the Police, quoted a legal deci- sion that the occupier was the person to whom the business actually belonged. Continuing. he ' said the rate-books since 1906 would show that the rated occupiers had been Messrs. Peter Walker. Among legal cases which he could quote was that of an appeal (in Dunning v. Owen) heard by Mr. 'Justice Darling in 1907. referring to sale of beer in St. George's Hall by Owen, who had no licence.'

===== 0002601_19130802_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLD TIIROUGFI AN AGENT

In the present case tliet7persons who sold the beer in the house wereishe contended, 'Messrs. Walker. They sold it through the agency of assistants, one of whom was termed by them a manager. The magistrate' had on many occa-

sions in that court had before him the cases of limited liability companies with -multiple shops, who were summoned and convicted for not hav- ing put up the notice in their branch shops. Mr: Duder declared that it appeared to him that a person who .owned a number of grocery shops was not different from Messrs. Walker, :who, merely happened to be brewers. Their. agent was a person who was required to have 2, licence from the 'magistrates. John Moothonse Todd said he was the licensee of the house. He Paid no rout, and he and his. wife lived on the premises.- Ho received the takings of the business, and after deducting his owii and his assistants' wages, together with incidental expenses, he paid the money into the head office of the firth. He possessed a Parlia- Mentary, but not- a municipal, Vote. When he applied for his: licence he was -asked ;whether 'he • with the. tenant Or ..the manager, and had told the -Bench that he was the manager. Answering the Stipendiary, witness said one Of the conditions of his employment by Messrs.. Walker' was that he was Ha* to dismissal .at a 'moment's -notice. Mr. Swift submitted that he had no case to answer. The prosecution had entirely failed to' make out that Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons were the occupiers of the public-house. The Stipendiary stated that he should re- serve judgment.

IN THE COURTS.

HAMMER AND POKER.

Alleged Wounding Case at Warrington

An elderly man, named Thomas Fernley, of 3, Silver-street, appeared before the Mayor and other magistrates, at the Borougji Police Court, on Monday, on charges of unlawlully wounding Elizabeth Bishop, a neighbour, by striking her on the head With a hammer, and of assaulting John Bishop, her husband. The .Chief Constable explained that at 10.30 on Saturday night, P.C. Cameron was on duty in Winwick-street, when he was informed of an alleged assault at 4, Silver-square. On going to the house he found Mrs. Bishop lying uncon- scious on the sofa. It was alleged that prisoner had struck her on the head with a hammer, causing her to lose a large quantity of bitiod. She was conveyed to the Infirmary, but some days would elapse before she was fit to appear in Court. A further allegation was that the prisoner had assaulted the husband of prosecu- trix, inflicting an injury which had to be dressed at the Infirmary. When charged, pri- soner said: "She had a poker, and I had to defend myself with something." A remand for eight days was ordered.

THE MARRIAGE LAW.

Warringlon.Magistrate and " Nlon- strous "Inequalities."

"I quite agree with Mr. Plowden, that the sooner mutual divorces come about, the better," said Mr. A. Browne, in an extraordinary domes- tic case at the Borough Court on Tuesday.— Edward Savage, of Dewsbury, applied for the annulment of a maintenance order on the ground of-alleged misconduct of his wife, Selina Savage, of •Winwick-road, Warrington.—Mr. Browne appeared for the wife, and Mr. Steel represented the applicant. Councillor Henry Roberts presided over the magistrates. When the case was before the Court a week ago the wife summoned her husband for arrears of maintenance, amounting to £3 14s. 6d. Mr. Steel then applied for the discharge of the order, but, owing to the absence of one of the principal witnesses, it was decided to adjourn the case. Mr. Steel now called Selina Savage, the 16-year-old da‘ighter of the parties, who gave evidence relating: to, sir incident which occurred ;at her mother'whouse.dnring a visit, one Satur- day, three months, ago: She- had left home, but went to see hermuthers.. Tile doors were locked, but eventually she got tlirough a window. Hear- ing a noise upstairs, she was going to see what was the- matter, when her mother came out of the back bedroom, screaming. She (witness) was frightened, and fell downstairs. She found a man's cap on the kitchen table and threw it outsid'e. She went outside and watched the house, and ultimately saw a man. Edward Savage (the husband) said the order was made on May 24th, 1910, for the payment of 10s. per week. He admitted to Mr. Browne that he went away four years ago without let- ting his wife know where he was, and that he never sent her a penny whilst he was away. Mr. Browne: Were you accidentally discov- ered in Dewsbury by Inspector Wainwright, who happened to go over there?—Yes. And you had not sent your wife a penny for four years?—No. Who brought up your children?—T'others. There was about .23 a week going into the house when I left them.

You thought it your duty to leave?—Yes, at that time.

Asked why he left home, Savage alleged that it was on account of his *He's drunken habits, and her practice of continually throwing things at him. "I had more cuts and bumps on my head than anybody," ;he added. • Did you go to live with a Mrs. Goodall?—Yes.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
"ABSOLUTELY MON.STROVS.7

_ I put it to you, that you have kept that woman and her children?—l have not kept-any- body. Mr. Browne, on behalf of the wife, said the man did- not appear with clean- hands: At the Previous hearing tile Magistrates' Clerk gave it as- his 'opinion :that -it was immaterial, in such a case whether the.man miscondUcted himself or not. That meant- that. the law was absolutely. monstrous, if carried to its logical conclusion. The Chairman: I quite agree with that, but it does not alter the position. Mr. Browne said a woman could not make one mistake, but a man could make 50 slips, and it .did not matter. He agreed with Mr. Plowden, that the sooner mutual divorces came about, the better it would be. A suggestion had been raised that the husband should take the children, but even if the wife were guilty—a Point which his evidence would emphatically disprove—it was a question of taking them from frying pan into the fire. He submitted rthcro-4-. confidence that the man was equally guilty. After evidence, had been given by Mrs. Savage and several witnesses, denying the allegations made for the applicant, The Chairman said the Bench were unani- mously of the opinion that misconduct had been proved, and the original order would be dis- charged. They made no order regarding the custody of the children. Each party would have to pay their-own costs. Mr. Browne applied for the payment of arrears under' the order, and mentioned that a sum of nearly £2O was owing, but said that that amount was not recoverable. He asked if the Bench found that the man had been guilty of misconduct. - The Chairman: I am afraid we cannot go into that question. Personally, I feel most strongly about the fact that for four years he has left the woman without money. The inequality of the law in a case like this is absolutely mon- strous. It is a case of an extremely unworthy app)icant getting the advantage of the law as it stands. The arrears will be paid by arrangement.

RECORD SUNSHIN E IN SOUTIT

DEVON

Perfect holiday weather still prevails at the' South Devon resorts. No rain has fallen for the past two months. The days are warns and beautifully tempered with refreshing breezes, while .the evenings are delightful. , The most glorious sunsets are being witnessed. Excellent arrangements have been made at the various: towns for the entertainment of visitors. Popu- lar concert parties are playing to large audi- ences. At the Royal Theatre and Opera House , Torquay, Mr. Arthur Roberts, the famous come- , . dian, and his full London company, are appear- ing with success. ' Preparations are . being forwarded for the annual regatta and sports meeting at Babbacombe on August Bth and 9th. The new third-class restaurant ear of the, G.W.R. is a topic of conversation in South Devon. markets. A wider outlook than an obsessed Tariff Reform advocate can apply to the question would surely reflect the fact that a nation in the midst ot a prosperous set of nations would be much better off than a nation in the midst of a poverty-stricken set of nations—just as an enlightened trades- man would expect to do better among prosperous neighbours than he would among a set of bankrupts. So we need not be cast down by the success of others as well as ourselves. We have only to realise these elementary truths, and we shall know quite well that Free Trade must hold its own against those perilous experiments that, would inevit- ably bring ruin in their train.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, AUGUST 2, 1913.

WHAT WE MUST REALISE

We have only to realise that Free Trade has been unable to prevent a rise in the price of food-stuffs; we have only to realise that with the rise in prices wages have gone down—says the Tariff Reform orator—and it is clear how greatly we have suffered under a disL credited fiscal system. Of course, we have only to realise that the very oppo- site has really occurred, and it is clear how much we are indebted to Free Trade. It is the passing on of half- truths that does the trick from the Tariff Reformers' point of view. True, prices have risen in England. but the movement is world-wide, and, as our persistent monitors never tire of telling its, the rest of the world favours Protec- tion, The difference is that while Free Trade Britain has succeeded- in keeping the increases within the -limits readily covered by a boUnding prosperity,-Protectionist countries have been unable to do so. In the United States, for instance, the ten years' average of the cost of living shows that prices last year were 58.0 per cent. more than they were a decade ago, and the tendency is still upward. For instance, we recently published a letter from a Warringtonian in Chicago, who said that bacon had risen Ili per cent. So that it seems, however miserably Free Trade may have failed, Protection has done infinitely worse.

Then as to wages. So far from their having declined, it is common know- ledge that wages have been raised, and ate being raised, in almost every trade in the land- This common knowledge is fittingly reflected by official returns.. -During the first five months of this year the rate of increase in wages paid to the -workers included in our Board of Trade Returns was £117,513 per week higher than during the corresponding period 01 1912. Turning from wages to the kindred subject of unemployment, and on this occasion comparing England with that other Unionist. paradise of Protection—except when insurance and similar democratic subjects are the matters in hand—Germany, we find that during the period January to May, the average percentage of unemploy- ment was in the United Kingdom L 9, and in Germany 9.6, the German figures, for reasons explained by, the Board of Trade, being an understate- ment of the facts. The Cornhill speaker this week drew an awful picture of the way in which Germany is treading upon our heels in the markets of the world. The markets of the world were not exactly made by a Divine Provi- dence as preserves for this country, though we have been favoured to such an extent that, small as we are, we have—under Free Trade—managed to get the lion's share, of the trade in those

,

===== 0002601_19130802_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM THE COLONIES TO INDIA.

The Tariff Reformers have recently made an effort to answer the challenge to "talk about. India," which was un- successfully 'made to them for .many years of the fiscal controversy. --Mr. BONAR. LAW grasped The nettle badly at Cheltenham; on December 4th, 1912, and got badly stung. He said to India: "If you want to put on tariffs, put them on against the rest of the world, but be a Free Trade country to us, and we will be a Free Trade country to you." Sir ROPER LETHBRIDGE has just published "The Indian Offer of Imperial Prefer- ence," in which he tries to help Mr. BONAR LAW out of his mess, and adds certain proposals of his own for dealing with India. He asserts. that India has "made us an offer," and that the Tariff Reformers are prepared to accept it; while the " Cobdenites "—the scornful epithet appears some forty times in the shOrt book—are too stupid and too compassionate of the interests of the foreigner even to consider it.

What is the " offer "? It is contained in a resolution as follows, moved 'by the Hon. Sir GANADHAR CHITNAVIS in the Legislative Council of the Governor:7, General of India on March 17th last : That this Council recommends to the Governor-General in Council the desira- bility, in view of the loss of opium revenue, of considering financial measures for strengthening the resources of the Government, with •special reference to the possibility of increasing the revenue under a system of preferential tariffs with the United Kingdom. and /he -Colonies.

Sir GANGADHAR is apparently an out- and-out Protectionist. He would like to see India protected against the com- petition of the Mother Country; but as he sees that this is never likely to .be. accomplished, he made a proposal, the substance of which IS contained in the following passage from his speech on the occasiOn referred to It is superfluous to add, Sir, that a scheme of preferential tariffs with the United Kingdom and the Colonies pre- supposes the introduction in India of a tariff on the imports of these countries, coupled with a heavier tariff on the goods of foreign countries, which latter tariff may or may not be protective in character. The basic idea is, of course, protection; but the system differs from a pure protective tariff in that the units of the Empire act in con- cert to obtain protection against the rest of the world. It is to be noted particularly that this preferential scheme " pre-supposes " the continuance of the Indian Tariff on British _goods, if not an increase in .the existing tariff. In this it-differs funda-. mentally from the Tariff Reformers' plan, as blurted out by Mr: BONAR LAW, and which is set out by Sir ROPER LETH- BRIDGE in the 'following words

• The advocates of Imperial Preference for India, headed by Mr. Bonar Law, Mr. Bal- four, and Mr. Austen Chamberlain, recog- nise the odious character of the excise duty on Indian cotton goods, and 'propose to abolish it, so as to meet the legitimate wishes of the whole of the Indian peoples; but they insist also that its abolition must be accompanied by the simultaneous aboli- tion of the import, duty on Lancashire and other British goods. Note that our Tariff Reformers "insist" on the abolition of Indian import duties on British goods, while the Indian " offer " is based on their continuance, if not their increase. This is a gap which Sir ROPER has made no attempt whatever to bridge; but he pretends not to see the yawning gulf. We slia:l doubtless hear much in the future en the Tariff Reform side about this new 'offer,' and it is well, therefore, to understand of what it consists, and how far it has been " accepted " by the Tariff Reform leaders. .

WARRINGTON AND SANATORIUM BENEFIT. An important statement by the President of the LOcal Government Board. (Mr. John Burns) with reference tb the 'provision made for the treatment of tubercul-viis, sht ir left unex- plained, create an impression that there had been a certain amount of dilatoriness by the authorities in attempting to le with that great problem. Warrington Warrington . grapp Is included by Mr. Burns in a list of county borough councils which have not yet notified the appointment of a unole p•lit tine tlil&r. culosis officer, and, to the uninitiated, it might appear that we had been slow in availing our- selves of the machinery set up by the Insurance Act. As a matter of fact, the direct 9 posite is the case, for Warrington may well pride itself upon being a pioneer in the matter of sanatorium- treatment of consumptives. For some considerable.. time before the passing of the Insurance Act the Smallpox Hospdal at Sankey had been in use for that purpose. The building has .been provisionally approved by the Local Governmeqt Ow (-fling of sanatorium benefit under the Act, and at the present time the -duties of tuberculosis officer are discharged by the Medical Officer of Health. This provisional scheme has the sanction of the Local Government Board, and the sanatorium has, so far, proved equal to the demands upon it, both insured and non-insured persons under- going treatment there. The Corporation has, however, prepared a new scheme, which pro- vides for an extension of the sanatorium, and plans and estimates were forwarded to the Local Government Board some time ago. We are informed that, in all .probability, a whole- time tuberculosis officer will not be appointed in Warrington, but there will be a resident inedical officer at Sankey, working under the direction of the Medical Officer of Health, who will COntinue to act as tuberculosis officer. When enlarged, the sanatorium will accomino- dtae 40 patients and the necessary staff, as compared with 18 under the.existing conditions. The estimated capital expenditure on the pro- posed extension is £5,400, half of which will be defrayed from Imperial funds. In order to facilitate the home treatment of the disease. a tuberculosis dispensary, with waiting, dressing and consulting .rooms, will be erected on land at the rear of the Health Offices in Sankey- street. It was with every, ground for self-congratula- tion that the ReceptiO*COmmittee, Which had charge of the arrangements for the nOyal - visit to Warrington, pleasantly rounded off its duties this week by apportioning credit where it was due. The twz).,speople generally will endorse the special vdte of thanks tendered by the committee to the Mayor, for. so tactfully presiding over the many meetings. of the com- mittee and its sub-committees, and fott his un- tiring efforts to make the brief sojourn of Their Majesties in the borough as pleasantly memorable as it proved to be. The committee expressed great satisfaction that the whole of the rejoicings passed off without any accident, and placed on record its opinion that the arrangements were satisfactory in every respect. The services of the Mayoress were also warmly acknowledged. We are pleased to learn that the Watch Com- mittee, at its last meeting, heartily thanked the Chief Constable for the admirable plans made by him for the preservation of order, and the convenience of the,fiublic. Tribute was also paid to the conscientiousness and good humour with which the menThers of the several Police Forces on duty in the streets did their-respon- siblb and oft-times trying work. The coininittee were especially gratified to note the total absence of disorder,' and expressed their ack- nowledgments- tO the. public generally for their eXeellent behaviour..

"PHEW. THE HEAT !"

We were born grumblers, said a wag, .and we grumble even at death. The greatest grumble we possess is against the weather. it Is either too hot or NO cold, too moist or too dry. This week we have been sneaking in the shady side of the Warrington streets, mopping our perspiring brows, and bewailing the fact that it was, intolerably hot. It may sound like treason to say it was net exceptionally hot any day this week, so .far as Wn- arringto was con- cerned. The idea that we had got a tropical temperature was a-popular delusion, shattered at once by the truth-recording thermometer. The highest sun temperature was 79, recorded on Tuesday. 13y yesterday it had fallen ten degrees, and still we proclaimed yesterday a warm day. Thus, alt week we have been grumbling at the Vat, without cause or reason. We have not nearly come up to record mark. In the past we have become so accustomed to Arctic summers that when a little heat does. favour us we grumble, or, at least, mildly com- plain. It is not fair—to the summer.

TiOLiDAY PROSPECTS.

, The holiday Spirit has got possession of us once more, and, in his largeness of heart, the Clerk of the Weather promises us an abundance of. genial 'sunshine, tempered with cooling breezes. The ohtlook for Bank Holiday is most promising. We are in the midst of a wide-reaching- anti-cyclone, and there is every probability of this continuing over the holidays. Resorts report rising barometers, fine tempera- tures, and brilliant sunshine, with every pros- pect of these being maintained. Thus the workers, who will ,be patrons of St. Lubbock on Monday, may confidently expect favourable weather.

THE CHIMNEYS.

A curse upon the chimneys,

With their black and baleful breath, That dulls God's skies of splendour, And dooms the flowers to death. Like horrible dark giants In grisly crofts they stand, And vomit forth destruction In a "green and pleasant land"

A curse upon the chimneys, That fill the streets with grime, Where the little children wander, .‘nd grow old before their time; That darken all the windows Looking out upon the sky, Where the clouds are fairy islands In the blue infinity:

A curse upon the chimneys, And the things they represent-- The grasping greed for riches, The poor man, puniOnuent ; The loss of joy and freedom, And the glad, sweet, golden air. And the world of waving branches With the thrushes singing there;

In the name of all the daisies, And the snowdrops, milky white, And the wonderful sweet lilies, And the roses of delight— Of their many-coloured sisters, Whom I vainly seek to nurse Into health, and strength, and beauty. I will register my curse.

Ay. and in the name, far deeper, Of the darkened souls who pine In, the pestilential alleys, Which are scarcely fit for swine; Of the pale-faced little people Who are blinded from their birth To the glory of the heavens, To the. gladness of the earth.

Of my fellow men and women In their multitudes forlorn Of the night which hangs for erer In a world ablaze with morn: Of the Eden which has faded, And the 'desert man has made, I will curse the god called Mammon, And the altars reared to Trade

AIR POLLUTION. A Manchester Effort to Clear Murky Atmosphere.

Considerable intervst will be taken in a scientific attempt which i's • being made in Manchester to mitigate air' pollution and pro- vide the city with more sunshine.

The Air Pollution Advisory Committee, which has been appointed by the Sanitary Com- mittee at the request of the City Council, is the first of its kind.in England. and is the direct outcome of a deputation from the conference. of Chemical Trades which waited on the Lord Mayor some time ago. The work of the com- mittee wilt be that of investigation and research and its proceedings wilt come before the Sani- tary Committ,.,e, and later before the City Council.

• Moral for the Dog. Two little black kittens were basking in the sun upon a shop doorstep in Lord-street. An inquisitive toy Porn invaded pussies' domain, and, with a yelp, made for one of the black twins. The second kitten rushed to the rescue. jumped upon doggie's back, clawed the Pom's head so effectively that the little terrier rushed howling for the shelter of its mistress's arms. The moral for such doggies is never to attack one when there are two kittens about.

, Lord Morley'S Compliment. Morley, at one of these banquets, looked up at the balcony glittering with lovely ladies and exclaimed: "Ali, now I understand the meaning of the Biblical phrase, "Chou limiest man a little lower than the angels.'"

Come Home, Darling !

"To bbtain a tealli-gbod new story nowadays one has to have it at firsthand, and it mu4.be true. A friend last Saturday- night was watching the return of a cyclist who, had been ()tit, in the. country for other purposes than admiring Nature. He was coming along Wilderspool-road, and was in that state when the breadth .of the road is a good deal • more impostant than the length.

He fell off his machine and got mixed up generally with it.. He mounted again, but before it hag carried him twenty yards it threw him again, and he evidently thought it was time for remonstrance. He held it at arm's length, as soon as he got himself on his feet, and his mount on its wheels.

" Well, if you won't carry me," he said, with that knowing smile, " will yo: come home witil me?"

Police Court Pleasantries.

INT'ot guilty to fighting, but guilty to getting - good hiding," was the modest plea of a defendant who, made his appearance at the Borough Police Court on a charge of CoMmit- ting a breach of the peafe. •

An Irishman who was making himself a nuisance to passers-by in the streets of War- rington on 'Saturday night almost implored a constable to lock him up. "It is like being in heaven" was his description of the Police Station. According to the Chief Constable, however, the tesliinonial is not to be applied locally. "We have no record of the prisoner," said that official, "so it must be in Ireland that the police stations are like heaven." • If is not often that prisoners combine a sense of humour with destructive tendenAes, but a man who stood in the dock at Warring- ton boasted those exceptional accomplish- ments„ We have all seen the enterprising advertiser's poster which depicts a broken window, With the legend, ," Who threw that brick and knocked our prices down?" The prisoner in question was seen prowling about near the very establishment in whose interests the poster is issued. Ilalf-concealed under his coat was a brick, find he told a constable. that, having seen the advertising devise, he meant 19 add the realistic touch by hurling the missile through the shop window. The constable, hbwever, did not • appreciate the humorous possibilities of the feat and unkindly escortea the would-be window-smasher to the Police Station.

William ascended' the derrick, eight 'feet above the top of the voile. There were persons below .who gave the boy credit for this feat. • "1 would not risk him in that," said William —physical strength, as well as other things, is needed ,to climb-the derrick."

- On the top, the boy was asked whether he could see his home in Manchester-road, and other points in town, as well as distant places, and the rising land in the neighbourhood of ,Bolton. He saw all, and in answer to the question how he was, he said, "All right." That he was "all right" was proved by his volunteering to climb over the edge of the scaffold to begin the descent .to save his brother the trouble of unscrewing the board to allow of his easy passage to the ladders; but this risk was not taken. However, the boy walked down as he had walked up, but this time his brother Jack was his close-attendant guardian.

Needless to say, William Hewitt is very proud of his young brother's expjoit. He himself was taken up chimneys at an early age, and other youngsters have been similarly "broken in," but he considers the climbing of a steeple 281 feet high, at the age of eight, to be a record.

Steeple Climbing.

Record Verformance for a Boy of Eight.

a NATURAL APTITUDE.

Much interest has L.c..it taken during the past week in the movements of Mr. William Hewitt, the clever steeplejack, who is at present engage:l upon the delicate task of currying a lightning conductor up the Parish Church spire, and put- ting the weather-cock at the top (.4 that high structure into working order. Mr. Hewitt, as is well known, was bred to the business, being the son of the late Mr. Hewitt, who was the local steeplejack for many years, and who died as recently iS last Decem- ber. ' He is a single man of 22 yeitrs, v.ith a steady eye that encompasses rather I ti 3 #1 pierces. • He lives at 200, Manchester-i onti, al- most under the shadow of the Steeple upon which he is now working. He is the oldest of five brothers, but in deference to the wishes of his mother, no other member of the family has essayed to follow in the business wake of his late father. • The youngest of the brothers

aißeTar. ttetween this boy and William come Stanley, who is a wiredrawer at "the Firth " ; Jack, employed at the Grappen- hall Tanning Company's Works; and Harold, who is in the velvet cutting business. All, however, are natural steeplejacks, • and the elder ones frequently assist William in his occupation, especially during the week-ends. Several times of late the five brothers—includ- ing the schoolboy—have been on the scaffold at the top of the spire at one and the same time.

Dr. Lumb's Prospective Appointment.

We understand that Dr. John fum- b, who was in June, appointed assistant medical officer of nealth for vr arrington, has been appointed, sub- ject to the approval of the Board of Educa- tion, .scnool medical officer for Jarrow, at a salary of J.;250 a year.

Cheshire Lines Railway Extension. As mentioned in our columns a short while ago, the Cheshire Lines Railway have opened their new coal and mineral yard in Battersby- lane,Warrington.We noticed yesterday 'morning that the enterprising firm of Ackers, Whitley and Co., Ltd., Bickershaw Collieries, Leigh, had already established an office there, and were busily engaged in supplying their noted house coals to this populous district.

The new voters' lists for Warring Ton nold3 their appearance oil the church doors and at her public places on Thursday. The over- seers tphuubsacnticipated the official date by one ot day. There was no eager scramble by proEpec- the voters to ascertain if their names appeared on the lists.

Nearly a Smash.

What might have proved a nasty accident was averted in Suez-street on Wednesday after- noon. A trap, containing ladies, was bcing driven along, when the horse tripped over 14::me wooden setts, which, at this point, are over an iiich higher than the tram lines. The horse's feet struck one of the high patches and the animal just escaped being thrown down.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
Motor Smashes Sign Posts.

At -noon yesterday; while Harry Oxley, of 114, Welllield-street, Warrington, was driving a motor car past Ecoles Town Hall the car skidded on the tram lines, and, turning right round, ran into and smashed a couple of iron signposts. The impact was severe, and the car was badly damaged. Oxley, who was severe;c shaken; was taken to Patricroft Hospital. Presentation to Mr., Arthur Slack..

An interesting presentation took place in the boardroom of the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Company, Ltd., on Tuesday last, when Mr. Arthur Slack, who is to be married on •t, 7th instant, was the rccipient of a I.ands.nro, cabinet of cutlery and a silver-plate" ten !Ind coffee service. Mr. H. M. Thornton (managing director and vice-chairman) in making the pre- sentation on behalf of the officials, staff and representatives. wished Mr. N. Slack a happy married life. Mr. Slack suitailly responded.

The Farmers Cheery. The spell of bright weather made the farmers, who attended Warrington market on Wednesday, quite optimistic. The sunshine, they reported, was just what the majority of the crops required. Green and cereal crops were said to be thriving immensely. The only thing regretted was that the turnips were re- quiring some rain. The Board of Agriculture have reported slight outbreaks of "black wart" amongst the potatoes in Lancashire, but, so. far as could be ascertained, the disease has not affected this district.

Infirmary Musical Festival.. The name of the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Choir was unfortunately omitted last week from our list of those choirs which have kindly consented to make up a mixed voice choir for the open- air musical festival, to be held in the Park on Sunday, August 17, under the auspices of the Working Men's Infirmary Committee. A meet- in F of the full committee, prealed cvf.r by 3t r. Hipkiss, was held at Toft's Assembly Room, and confirmed the , arrangemonts that u ere Made last week by the Exeeative Committee, and reported in last Saturday's "Examiner." It was reported that Messrs. Peter Walker and Son had. again very kindly undertaken to pro- vide the stand.

A " Striking " Advertisement. A boy named George Donoghue was 'enjoying a trip on one of: the new motor 'buses last Sunday; and would no doubt have been highi deliflited with his ride if he had not met with a slight accident. When passing along Sankey- street the boy was standing on a seat on no upper deck of the 'bus, and was caught on the head by a swinging sign abave a trades:oar. s premises. The boy's head was eut a,til I.e vas conveyed to the Infirmary, where it %cos found that his injuries were only slight. The boy, who is 12 years of age, is the son of George Donoghue, carter, 27, Howard-street. The sign is about 14ft. from the ground, and protrudes over the footpath to the extent of about 12 to 15 inches.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
A Fatal "Lift.?'

The death took place at the Warrington In- firmary on Thursday night of Daniel O'Brien, aged 36, a general labourer, of no settled abode, from injuries received through being run over by a motor waggon in Buttermarket- street. The deceased, along with a -friend, was tramping from Clitheroe to Warrington, in search of work. When about three miles out- side the borough they got on a trailer attached to a heavy motor, carrying goods from Oldham to Liverpool. In attempting to alight in But- termarket-street from the front of the trailer deceased was struck by the waggon and knocked to the ground, the wheOls of the trailer pass- ing over his thighs, and the left leg being almost severed. Re was removed to the Infirm- ary, where he died a few hours later from loss of blood.

Mr. G. A. Leach's New Appointment.

Mr. George A. Leach, of Warrington, has been promoted to the position of superintendant of the Doncaster district of the Blackburn Philanthropic Friendly Society, and left this town on Tuesday to take up his new duties. Hitherto Mr. Leach has been engaged as assist- ant superintendent of the Warrington district, which is in charge of Mr. Joseph Fairn.wst. During his thirteen years' business experience in Warrington Mr. Leach has taken an active part in its social life. He was the first secretary of the 'Warrington Citizens' Temperance Leigue,, which was formed to support the Gov- ernment's'Licensing Bill of 1908. He helped to organize some of the largest temperance meet- ings ever held in Warrington, notably the one at which the Bishop of Liverpool was the prn- cipal speaker. Mr. Leach .is an ardent co- operator, and was a member of the special com- mittee formed to arrange the celebration of the jubilee of the Warrington Co-operative Socie-y. Living in Grappenhall. he has taken a great interest in parish affairs, having served as a member of the Parish Council. Mr. Leach has made a special study of the breeding anzi rearing of poultry, and has given the people of Warrington the benefit of his knowledge on this subject by a series of interesting and prac- tical articles, contributed to the "Examiner" for the past four years. under the nom-de- nlume of "Redcap." The excellence of Mr. Leach's work in this direction has freouently been recognised by "The Feathered World," one of the leading poultry journals. Mr. Leach has also acted as corresnondent to the " Examiner" for Grappefthall and district. His many Warrington friends will cordially wish him success in his new sphere.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
DRINK AND POVERTY. Sober Democracy Essential to Solution of Social Problems.

" Why should you work like horses and spepd your money like asses?" asked Mr. .G. E. Win- terton, who addressed the weekly meeting of the Warrington Citizens' Temperance League at Cornhill on Wednesday. The Rev. A. J. CHEMIN, who presided over an exceedingly large attendance, said he had only been in Warrington a fortnight and had seen plenty of facilities for drinking. He was, how- ever, glad to see that the Temperance cause had such a number of supporters. They came there that night to plead to the people who had votes to use their influence in the interests of the Temperance cause—to rid them of that which was eating like a canker into so many systems and help them to make the Borough of Warring- ton a little better, brighter and happier. (Ap- plause.)

DRUNKEN NATIQN HELPLESS TO TACKLE SOCIAL EVILS.

Mr.W'r'rT!'"lNT n very interesting address, in which he dealt with the povery question as affected by intemperance.

Why, he asked, should men work like horses and spend their money like asses? Smiply because men who drank did not think. Imme- diately they began thinking they would see the folly of drinking. When they had a sober com- munity they would be able to deal effectively with the question of poverty, and all things which tended to the social amelioration of the workers. A half-soaked, intoxicated nation could never deal with the mass of poverty we had to-day. It was in their own interests that working men should endeavour to get rid of that which was destroying and blighting so many good lives. (Applause.)

Yesterday we received the following intima- tion, which will be as interesting to our readers as it is satisfactory, to ourselves:—

Manchester, July 31st, 1913.

T. Weston, Esq., "The Warrington

Examiner."

Dear Sir.—ln reply to yours of yesterday's date, I have to say that my Executive Council have had before them a report presented by our President (Mr. J. D. French) and myself, on the interview we recently had with you and the Chairman of your Company.

They were pleased to know that it is your intention to conduct your letterpress printing department in harmony with the rules and regulations of the Typographical Association, and after the assurances given in your letter of July 21st, my Executive Council will have pleasure in placing your establishment on the list of offices recognised as "fair" by the Typographical Association.
