SCHOOL'S SCOPE EXTENDED.

The committee had pleasure in once more recording their fullest confidence in the Head- mistress (Miss Flood); her internal administra- tion of the school continuing to give complete satisfaction.' • They w9Old cOrdially endorse the. following opinion exp-ressed by the inspec- tors in their report: "The Headmistress is in every way well suited kis- her post. A sound, classical scholar and a very capable teacher, she is possessed of very considerable powers of organisation, and in addition has a keen interest in everything that can affect the many-sided life of a girls' boarding school. Her relations with both staff and pupils are easy and pleasant." (Applause.) The number of scholars in the school remains practically unchanged. The average during the past three terms had been a fraction above 108, the full accommodation being 110. It was of interest to note that at the present time nearly 90 per cent. were daughters of clergy, the re- mainder, amounting at the present time to 14 scholars, being daughters of Church of England laymen. This extension of the scope of the school had been of great benefit in many ways. The committee regretted a slight falling off in applications for admission from the clergy in the three dioceses. Thlit, however, was partly accounted for by the extra expense entailed by those on the list of expectant major. bursars. The Headmistress reported the conduct of the scholars during the year as excellent. The health of all in the establishment had also, on the whole, been very good. Only one case of infectious disease occurred,'and that on the day after return from the summer holidays. The patient was at -once isolated in the sanatorium, and removed later to the Whitworth Hospital. The disease, fortunately, did not spread, and the school was thoroughly disinfected through- out.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
EXAMINATION DISTINCTIONS

The successes of past and present pupils re- cbrded during the year had been :—Past pupils: R. C. Lamburn, a further classical scholarship of £5O awarded by the University of -London; Muriel Clayton, B.A. degree, London Univer- sity; c4wen Lamburn, B.A. degree, London Uni- versity, Present pupils: Winifred Marion Read, classical scholarship of ~t6O a year for three years at RoYal Holloway College; W. M. Read and 0. Knight, Division I. London Matricu- lation. Cambridge higher local: Honours 11, distinction 2, passes 7. Oxford and Cambridge Joint Board examinations: Higher certificates and lower certificates. Cookery examinations: London City and Guilds' certificates. Royal Drawing Society: 56 honours, 37 passes. Music examinations—Entrance examination to Pres- ton DomestiC Science School: P. M. White, who obtained SO per cent. of marks in all her papers except one.

THE FINANCIAL POSITION.

The HONORARY TREASURER (Mr. H. Greenall) reported that the income from investments, &c., showed an increase attributable largely to interest on capital deposits awaiting investment. The collections had decreased by .238 12s. 6d., and the subscriptions by £34 10s. 10d. The donations had increased by £7 19s. 6d. The

year closed with a credit balance of ".30 17s. 6d.. after commencing with a debit balance of £36 12s. 2d.

GRANTS TO WIDOWS AND ORPHANS.

The Rev. G. B. THURSTON (honorary secretary of the widows' and orphans' fund) reported that the following grants had been approved by the committee :—Chester: Widows 16, grant £295, orphans 21, .2295. Liverpool: Widows 15, £235, orphans 17, £260. Manchester: Widows 39, 1:590, orphans 13, £l6O.

LEGACIES AND DONATION.

The HONORARY SECRETARY announced that the following gifts had ,come to the fund during the current year: A legacy of £lOO from the late Miss Emily Jane Massy, of Latchford; a legacy of £5OO from the late Miss Ann Farrer Bride- cake. 9f .Croft; and a munificent donation of £5,000' from an anonymous donor, through the Lord Bishop of Liverpool. The Honorary Secretary further stated that it was a pleasure to him to record that during his seven years' tenure of the office of Secretary, the amount of capital in the Widows' and Orphans' Fund (including Canon Webb's legacy) had increased from £26,117 15s. Id. to £40,380 9s. 2d.. an increase of £14,262 14s. id. Above £2OO had been recovered from the Income Tax, and higher contributions than at present were expected from the dioceses, through the new Finances Boards. Having regard to all these circumstances it was hoped that the debit balance of £2Bl is. 2d. would be ntirely wiped out, and that more adequate grants would be possible in future years. (Applause.)

HOLIDAY -RAILINGS

The North Wales Steamship Company have made the following .sailing arrangement's for Bank .Holiday: The St. Elvies will leave the Prince's Landing-stage at 9.15 a.m. for Llan- dudno (the Naples of Wales), allowing a stay ashore of about 51 hours, hus affording passengers a splendid opportunity of viewing the most interesting spots with which this sea- side resort abounds. MODERN TWO-FEEDER WHARFED.

This week the linotype machines which are to set the type used in the " Examiner" have got to work, and the more rapid-moving machine which is to print the paper from, and including, to-day began running last night. We hope the results, as shown by our present issue, will be pleasing to our. readers. The addition of a column to each page will give us ample scope for dealing adequately with all local news and current topics when the holiday season is over and the reading season comes round.

As to the mechanical appliances that are to aid us in our work of reflecting local history in the making, our illustrations will speak for themselves.

By a curious coincidence, the enlargement of the " Examiner " and the resignation of Mr. Ernest Iddon, who, has been chief reporter of the " Examiner " for eight yoears, a position he has _occupied with the fullest satisfaction, not only to the proprietors, but the many speakers Whose utterances it has been his duty to record, have happened at one and the same time. Mr. Iddon resigned much to the regret of the whole of his colleagues, whose best wishes will follow him to his new appointment. In his stead we have appointed a gentleman of considerable ability and ripe experience as' a journalist.

Mr. Black, Mi.. Iddon's successor, has this week taken up his duties, and much of his work appears in the present issue. He is a native of Edinburgh. Destined for the medical profes- sion, he studied at Edinburgh University, but

WOMAN'S SPHERE.

Bishop Weldon on Increasing

Opportunities.

PRIZE DAY AT DARLEY DALE.

Tuesday was the annual prize day in connec- tion with St. Elphin'a School, Darley Dale, Mat- lock, which is endowed for the daughters of the clergy and laity. of the dioceses of Manchester, Liverpool, Chester, and Southwell. The foun- dation originally was situated at Warrington. The Bishop of Chester presided over a large gathering, and the prizes were distributed by Bishop Wplldon, Dean of Manchester. He said that anyone who attempted to address an audi- ence like that ought to be the father of at least three girls—(laughter); he had not contributed his -share to the prosperity of the school. He did not look on the Board of Education of the country, as an enemy as some of his brethren did—yet he did believe clear, dogmatic religious education was essentially the first element in high edueation. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19130802_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
ADVANCE OF WOMANHOOD.

He reminded the young ladies how great had been the advance of womanhood during the last half or three-quarters of a century. There were persons who to-day made a mistake of 'for- getting how much had been achieved in the past. It would be possible to sympathise with strong agitation if it could be said that women had not been allowed to make any advance. Why, they had everything now excepting, per- haps, the political franchise, and that they would get it they wanted it. The field was open to women all along the line. - It had been said that dentistry was the only career not open to women, but when he him- self was in India, at Darjeeling, he enjoyed the experience of a female dentist, who had been left in charge for the tender mercies of den- tistry. He could assure them that her dis- suasive methods rather persuaded callers to wait for the arrival of the man.

Referring to punctuation and spelling, he said how appalling some of the spelling was he found in some parts—not at Darley. (Laughter.) There was too much tendency to-day to build a superstructure without the elementary founda- tion. (Hear, hear.) He wished the school every success. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19130802_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
HANDSOME ANONYMOUS GIFTS,

The BISHOP or CHE§TER announced how great was the financial support they had received at that school. In seven years they had received £14,000. (Applause.) The widows and orphans fund of the three dioceses hadjust had £5,000 through the Bishop of Liverpool atonymously, and £l,OOO for that school. school was well managed, and deserved well, and had earned a high position, and he looked forward to better fruits still in the future.

&CHURCH OF ENGLAND SCHOOL.

In the course of the annual report, submitted at the business meeting, the Rev. G. B. THURS- TON stated that the chief event of the past year had been the fOrmal recognition of St. Elphin's by the Board of Education as an efficient secondary school.. A full inspection by the Board was held on May 21st and 22m1, 1912, and the report contained certain suggestions as to the buildings, staff and curriculum. A second visit by Mr. F. E. A. Trayes. 1131. Inspector, on May 13th this year, satisfied him that the sug-

gestions had received adequate attention, and the order of the Board placing the school on the official list was received on June 3rd. This recognition was regarded a 4 a distinct step for- ward in the school's history, and should be an assurance to the subscribers that the school was under the most expert supervision that the country could provide. , At the same time the special character of the school as a Church of England school was not in any way impaired, as no Government grant was received or asked for.

MARKET "SLAVES."

Girls on Duty Sixteen Hours at a

Stretch.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
REFORM SHOPKEEPERS WOU LD WELCOME.

(" Examiner" Special.)

Dilatory shoppers inflict a grave injustice upon the assistants, who wait so courteously upon purchasers, at the stalls in Warrington Market. The hours these willing lasses have to put in on Saturdays are beyond reason. Many are on duty as much as sixteen hours at a stretch, and their lot on Saturdays is far from a happy one.

The conditions of the Market employes could be vastly improved by a little sweet reasonable- ness displayed upon the part of the shoppers. Many,..would-be purchasers leave their shopping to the last moment', then they scurry round to the Market, and the waiting attendants have to serve, no matter if it is nigh midnight. A little forethought upon the part .of these ,Fate-shopping dames—women are the chief offenders—would considerably curtail the hours of stall assistants. "Shop early," should be the. motto for them to remember, and, if they did remember, the stall, lasses would not be (MFe the slaves of trade that they are at present. flint to, Committee.

"Yes it is surprising the amount of 'slavery' to be found in the Market," said a townsman, who is deeply interested in the question, when interviewed by the "Examiner" representative. "And I think it is quite unconsciously per- mitted by the powers able to end it.. A great amount might be done to alleviate- the long hours worked by the 'stall 'assistants. Our Market Committee should take action.."

"Does not the Shop Hours Act. apply?'

"No. It is Vrue. the Shop flOurs Act limits the number of hours a young assistant may be employed per week. The young girl at a Market stall chiefly ranks as a casual worker, and, consequently; she is outside the scope of the Act.

"So long as our Market Committee allows the Market to remain open until midnight on Sat- urdays, so long will these female assistants be called upon to work fourteen', fifteen, or sixteen hoilis at one stretch., and that often without getting time to enjoy a proper meal: There Is no means of preventing such lbng, arduous hours, except by the intervention of the Market Committee on the lines indicated. Hard Cases.

"Some hard cases have come to my notice, and these will serve to illustrate what/ really does happen. There are known instances where girls, after having worked, for fourteen hours in a shop in town, have attended at a Market stall on Saturday night for another one and a half or two hours. If the Marketi was compelled to close at an earlier hour; the shops closing at the same time, all this excessive work would be avoided."

"What are the shopkeepers' views about the matter?" he was asked.

" Shopkeepers generally would he' glad to have a definite time for closing on Saturday nights. They would be pleased to close earlier, but that they cannot do as the Market—a rival trading centre, which does not contributemearly as much to the rates as the shops—is •allowed to keep open until nearly Sunday morning. The shopkeepers want earlier Saturday closing, but they cannot do it in face of the Market opposition.

Shop Assistants' View. "How do the shop assistants view the posi- tion thus created?" was the next query. "Like the shopkeepers, they. are far from satisfied with the Market Committee -permitting: midnight closing- of; the Market on Saturdays.. EmplOyers and employed are distinctly unani- mous: upon that. point, for each. find 'that .time is wasted, lighting is wasted, and much, annoy- ance is caused by the Market being open to an untimely hour. "Earlier closing of the Market. would enable the other traders to shut at a reasonable hour. If this was brought about, many of the assist- ants, who live in lodgings, would be able to get to their homes to spend the week-end, a boon which would be much appreciated in sum- mer, and welcome at any time. "If the Market Committee did as we desire, it would prove of much advantage to the assist- ants. The public Would soon learn to buy earlier, and no loss would be sustained by the Market traders. It is a matter the Committee should tackle promptly."

===== 0002601_19130802_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOD 70 BE DEARER.

Some of the Reasons Why Bacon is

at Record Price.

(" Examiner" Special.)

Careful housewives will learn with alarm that there is a marked tendency of a ftirther rise in the price of provisions. The wholesale markets in the North. report advances in price; the Southern markets have moved in sympathy. Bacon, in the wholesale market, has reached a record rate, and by the weelv-end prices will have advanced in the retail market. Warring- ton will not escape this any more than other Lancashire towns.

Bacon has now ceased to be a food; it has become a luxury, and many people have been constrained to remove it altogether from the breakfast table menu. Still, the price mounts up, although fewer people are consuming it. "In skimmer the consumption of baton de- creases," said a Warrington provision dealer, "and if prices fluctuate it is then. Despite the lesser demand', prices are now jumping np• at an alarming rate. Ham is up in proportion, eggs are advancing, but this is seasonable fluctuation. Canadian cheese is rising."

Is the Market Rigged? The cause for the increase is partially ascribed to the increased freight by railway and canal companies, but this does not account entirely for the abnormally high price of bacon. Pig-breeding is becoming a lost art in parts of the country where the rearing of porkers used to be a staple trade. Paddy, on his Irish croft, is doing better, and it is not now so necessary for him to "grow a pig to pay the rint," and this once -fruitful source of supply, exploited by Sir Thomas Lipton, in his early trading days, has been considerably curtailed. Canada and the United States, in the past, were good bacon purveyors for the English market. The increasing population there has diverted a large _percentage of American pro- duce, and will dolpore so in the future. There is also a suspicion that the powerful bacon trusts, which have sprung up in America, are manipulating the supplies to increase their pro- fits. There are grounds for supposing that the Yankees are assiduously rigging the Eng- lish markets.

Why Not a Bacon Strike?

If all the housekeepers instituted a bacon strike, as the American women ran the success- ful beef strike to defeat the price-raising tactics of the meat trusts, it might lead to a reduction in price. "Custom," said the 'Warrington dealer, "has made bacon-eating almost a fetish with the English people. The ,better-to- do _invariably consunie it daily, and the less fortunate eat it once or twice a week. Bacon is, nowadays, as much an English national dish as roast beef or plum pudding. The people will have it."

"What would be a good substitute for bacon?" he was asked. "Ham !" Came the retort. It was no good to pursue the matter further; he dealt largely in these commodities. At one time porridge was a popular dish in Lancashire. , A new generation has arisen, however, who know not porridge. This would be a good substitute for the breakfast table of those -i(:) care not to pay, or cannot pay, for the bacon boom. Oatmeal is cheap and nour- ishing, and it is evident if bajo.. and ham con- tinue at famine prices some of us will have to resort to the oatmeal, which Dr. Johnson sar- castically described as food Lor horses in Eng- land, and food for people

WATER TROUGHS. Sir,—l am one of the many Gee-Gee's who work hard for a living and during the past week the cry of each and all of us has daily been, "I thirst." At our last" Stable Talk" there was a general nodding of ^ heads in support of your appeal on our behalf, and as it was only for WATER we 'asked, tha hope was a general one, that the Council would not say neigh." We have no wish to appear to be " nag-ging " them, but feel -something might be done to relieve our suffering in the way you suggested. As your first appeal has failed please try, try and try again in the name of COMMON HUMANITY.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL VETO AND LOCAL OPTION.

--- Sir,—Your correspondent, Mr. Edward Tucker, ought to be aware that he is in error in stating that "The people in any district are not to be trusted to say whether they would like more or fewer public- houses, they are only to be allowed to reduce the number of public-houses or pro- hibit them altogether. A majority in favour of an additional licence is to be powerless"

This is simply not true. The Licensing 'Bill of 1908 had the support of practically all temperance workers. If Mr. Edward Tucker will refer to Clause 2 of that Bill he will see that he has written without -knowledge. July 9.Bth, 1913.

THOS D. LESLIE, U.K.A.

AGAINST CONSCRIPTION.

Sir,—l have just had brought to my notice an article, which appeared in a recent issue of your valuable paper under the heading "Against Conscription; the Advantages of Voluntary System for Island Nation." I fear I am unable to criticise Mr. Elwell's advocacy of Free Trade, but I must strdngly protest against the wild assertions of Mr. Ternent. Every Tory, Liberal, or Socialist will agree that Sir Edward Grey has proved himself to be one of the greatest Foreign Secretaries who has ever guided our relations with Foreign States, and few people in Europe will deny that up to the present, the Balkan War has been localised mainly by his exertions. Mr. Ternent went on to say: "Everyone in the country benefited from a peace policy, and if anyone was hit by the disaster of war, it was the wage-earning section of the community." With this assertion I heartily agree. Should this country ever be invaded, the working classes would be the greatest sufferers. This being so emphasises the fact that the proposals of the National Service League for the introduction of universal military training are in the best interests of the masses.

Mr. Ternent proceeds: "At the time of the Peninsular War the Militia Act was in force, and men were compelled by ballot to serve in that campaign." Quite true. The Ballot Act—which means con- scription—was in force in this country at that period, but not one conscript was compelled to take part in the Peninsular War.; many thousands, however, volun- teered to do so. 'As a matter of fact, Wellington's army at Waterloo was largely ,composed of conscript Militiamen, who volunteered to serve With regiments of the Regular Army-. If the Militia Ballot Act had been in force during the Boer War, there would have been no lack of volun- teers fully trained to fill up the gaps in the fighting line.

-Coninuing, Mr. Ternent says; "Our first line of defence—the Navy—was impreg- nable." Mr. Ternent must be aware that three years in succession during naval manceuvres the attacking force has been able to escape the vigilance of the defenders and have affected a landing on these shores, although the defenders on each occasion have had double the number of ships of the attackers. Only a few days ago the attackers took possession of Grimsby and landed troops. Just one other absurd con- tention. Mr. Ternent said: " If the Germans contemplated invasion they would have to smash our Navy as a preliminary, and even then they would not think of landing, but would starve us into submission." If Germany or any other country succeeded in smashing -our Navy it is ridiculous to think that they would rest content by attempting to starve us out. Mr. Ternent should know that to attempt to blockade out 1,500 miles of coast line could not be carried out by the combined navies of Europe.

Ddring the five months' siege of Port Arthur it is a fact that, notwithstanding the vigilance of the Japs, the. Russians managed to get supplies into that fortress during the whole period, although the Japs had only 20 miles of coastline to blockade. MAJOR D. STRACHAN, Organising Secretary, National Service League. 57a Drury Buildings, Liverpool, 29th July.

WELSH DISESTABLISHMENT.

Sir,—l see in your issue of July 26th a letter by Mr. Broadbent, purporting to deal with a speech by me at Bridge Foot. This gentleman in his baste apparently could nott wait for the extended report of the meeting, which in your issue of July 19th you were good enough to say would appear in the next issue, and so taking the short account you gave, the correspondent makes certain suggestions as to statements I am supposed to have made. First of all, let me say that as he was not present at the meeting, and secondly, as he did not wait for the full report which appeared in your issue of the 26th, his letter is irrelevant, and he is not in a position to judge. I would suggest that your correspondent should read the full report. I have no intention of entering into a „Press correspondence with Mr. Broadbent, as he had every opportunity of being at the meeting and putting what questions he chose to me.

First: The nation, of course, WI S the nation of Wales, and whether your corre- spondent likes it or not, the Celt was a nation before the English Were in existence, and Wales has' never lost her nationality, having maintained her distinct language, literature, characteristics and all essentials that are generally recognised as con- stituting a nation. Second: Your correspondent refers to opportunity being given for a declaration of the Welsh people. He says it has been asY ed for in vain, but that plea for a religious census or a referendum is simply a dodge, for when in the House of Commons the Opposition have been asked whethor, after according a religious census, the Welsh opinion is conclusive on the point, could the Bill be proceeded with, and the answer has been "No." The declaration is very emphatic. Prior to the last election the bishops issued their solemn warning, the Churchpeople did all they could, and the result was tairty-one members for Disestablishment and Disendowment, and three against.

Third- : The Archbishop's quotation. I notice your correspondent .tells me the quotation that I gave was made at the 'gress. If he will refer to your

wansea Con,

report, there is no need for me to tell him where the quotation was made. He is evidently thinking about something altogether different. Four: The average majority on the Bill in Committee was .116. :Your correspondent could take the whole of the Irishmen from the House of Commons, and the fact remains that there is a substantial majority in Committee. I am not concerned with snap divisions and attempts at snap divisions, or divisions on minor details of the Bill, it is sufficient to know that on e very important division,even in Committee, independent of the Irish, there is a large majority in favour of the Bill. Your correspondent refers to the scant considera- tion given to the measure. Twenty-nine days of last session were devoted to it. I dealt with the petitions which your correspondent refers to, and if he knows anything about them at all, he must be aware that many of the "over half a million people who have signed the petition in Wales" were English visitors to Wales, and many of the .signatures are in the same handwriting. ( Vide Petitions Committee.) Your correspondent should n,ot be in such a hurry to say any statement is ridiculous. Evidently in this instance he has been in a hurry. Fifth i Again your correspondent says I said not a word about the dismemberment of the Church. Once more he should have waited for the report. There can be no dismemberment of the Church as a spiritual organisation, and there is nothing in the Bill which forcibly separates the Church except in its political aspect as an establishment. When he compares the dismemberment of the Church to the modified arrange- ments proposed by the Nonconformists in relation to their minor pastoral arrange- men ts,he betrays a great lack of knowledge: This arrangement includes no question of doctrine, no financial arrangement of any importance, and nothing that affects their working details, and no real objection was , offered to the proposal made, only it was thought that it would be better for organ- lising purposes that the present insigni- ficant relations should remain.

My first impulse was to ignore your correspondent, but as he imputes to me certain- statements which I did not make, and suggests certain omissions, which I very emphatically dealt with in my speech, I thought it well for the benefit of your readers just to state that your correspondent is in too great a hurry to criticise, and should wait and see before Committing himself to paper. F. RHODES SMITH. Welsh Disestablishment Central Campaign Committee. 134, St. Stephen's House, Westminster, London, S.W. -- MODERATE DRINKERS.

Sir,—At a temperance meeting held at Latchford last week, and reported in your issue of the 26th inst., Mr. Quinton said: "The so-called moderate drinkers were the worst enemies of the temperance cause. A person who had the will to take a drink and leave it was the most liable to cause others to follow his example." It is often necessary to remind temper- ance, or rather teetotal, orators that " temperance " is the exact equivalent of "moderation," and implies just exactly the true balance of life or reasonableness. "Because of this " balance " or " self- con trol " the conscientious moderate drinker is enabled to use alcohol and other material things without abusing them, and proves the truth of the Christig.n teaching that the root of evil lies not in material things, but in the perverted will of man. Total abstinence, after all, is only a negation, and no mere negative reform is of much moral value. The fact that most of our greatest teachers and thinkers in the past were moderate drinkers,of itself proves how utterly absurd and untrue are the words of Mr. Quinton. EDW. TUCKER. July 30th, 1913.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 2, 1913.

A FEARNHEAD BEAUTY SPOT.

Councillor T. Birtles' Attractive Country Retreat.

PEACE AND HARMONY. Few gardens take a more attractive form than that which surrounds the residence of Councillor T. Birtles at Fearnhead. Two of the smoothest of lawns, a fish pond and peaceful retreats with bush, standard and rambler and climbing roses in the greatest profusion make a picture that will not be easily surpassed.

NICE DISHES.

STIUFBED SOLE.—Skin the sole, cut it straight down the middle of the back, raise the fish from the bone, till it in with stuffing made as follows: Breaderumbs, butter, salt, pepper, and, chopped parsley. Sew the seam up, put the fish on a dish in a moderate oven, and bake for half to three- quarters of an hour. Pour a little melted butter over, chop up a hard-boiled egg, and sprinkle it over the top (middle). Each side should have a layer of chopped beetroot, and garnish the dish with parsley. FRENCH FRIED EGGS.—Put two ounces of butter into a frying:pan, and when it begins to fritter add the eggs, season with 'pepper and salt, and fry_ When .done, remove them- care- fully on to slices of toast in the dish in which they are .to be served.. Then put another ounce. of butter in the -pan, and fry till it is a brown colour,. when one tablespoonful of French vine- gar is added. Boil the whole together for two minutes, then pour the butter over the eggs and serve. •

RtssoLEB.—An/ kind of game, poultry, or meat ean be ued. Pull the meat to pieces with two forks, chop it a little (not too fine), season with salt and pepper, thyme, and sauce or gravy; cook some potatoes (cold will do if they are warmed up again), mash them, and roll them out like pastry; cut it about three inches squsire, put in a roll of the meat, and roll the potato round with a knife (it is best to wet the knife or it may stick); fry the rolls in plenty of fat from five to ten minutes.

RHUBARB. COMPOTE.—Choose some stalks of very pink rhubarb, and divide it into two-inch pieces. Make a thick syrup of three-quarters of a pound of auger and a teacupful of water to every pound of rhubarb. Boil the syrup for fifteen minutes. and then add the fruit and the thinly-pared peel of an orange, allowing the whole to simmer very slowly for a quarter of an hour more. Then remove the rhubarb, pick out the orange rind, and reduce the syrup until it is thick. When cold, dish the rhubarb, pour the thickened syrup over the top, and decorate with split praline almonds.' GOOSEBERRY SNOW.—Take one pound of goose- berries, stew till tender, rub through a sieve or colander, and to half a pound of pulp add half a pound of castor sugar and the *whites or three eggs (well beaten); beat all together for about twenty minutes. Make a custard with the yolks of the three eggs, one pint of milk and eight or ten pieces of loaf-sligar. When cool pour into a glass dish, and drop the snow upon it with a large spoon.

'•• In his quarterly report, Mr. De Jong (medical officer) stated that the.population of the dis- trict -was 5,057. Twenty-two births and sixteen deaths had been registered in the district. Seven Lady Lever's death. persons had died over 70 years of age, others at 90, 88, 82, 80, 72 and 71. Four infants had died. There had been two cases of tuberculosis, and 22 cases of infectious diseases, viz.: five, chicken pox; eight, measles; three, erysipelas; and _diphtheria. He had been fortunate enough to meet the direetors of the Fish and Manure Glue Works, and they had promised to do their best to mitigate the nuisance said to be prevailing there. The Statham School was closed on account of diphtheria and theeltildren had been examined by himself. Routine work had been carried out under the Housing and Town Plan- ning Act, and /nuisances arising, from offensive trade processes had been attented to. The report was_adopted. ALLEGED OFFENSIVE SMELLS. Two petitions had been received from resi- dents in the Statham district, asking the Coun- cil to. pay instant attention and to use their powers to prevent the evil smells prevalent in the district for the past few weeks. It was hard to submit dates and hours, but special mention might be made of June 29th, July 2nd and 11th, say, from 10 a.m. until 8.30 p.m. The smells were most powerful," and strong com- plaints had been made by the men working iii the fields and on the Lymm Golf Course. Resi- dents had also been compelled to close their doors and windows to keep the. smells out of the houses. Complaints of a nuisance arising fronal smells in another part of the urban district were also received. In both cases it was decided to write the firms from whose works the nuisance was alleged to emanate, to the effect that if steps -were not taken to abate it, legal proceedings would be entered upon. CAUTION IN TOWN PLANNING. _

-- Mr. Holt gave the i:ePo-ri Ortlte—CTierk and himself to the Conference ot the Urban District Councils' Association at • Llandrindod Wells. The conference was Most .iustructive and educa- tiOnal. • At one time be thought it a waste of money to send .delegates, but now his views were changed. After what was said about town planning, he was of the opinion that they as a district would be well advised to let the matter stand. over for some time, as no .one seemed to give an Opinion as to carrying out a scheme, which was in its infancy. Housing and town planning problems were also discussed, and he was of the opinion that they woul3 :!‘) well to use caution before they took any steps in that matter. Mr. Mullard•thanked the Council for allowing him to attend the conference, which was a very interesting and instructive one. A vote of thanks was accorded the de:.ntation for their report.

DISTRICT NEWS.

LY

CONSUMPTION.—There were during 1912 eigh- teen cases of tuberculosis notified in Lymm. One person, a non-resident, died from the disease.

POPULATION.—According to the report of the Medical- Officer, the population of Lymm is now 4,990, and shows a tendency to increase through the development of the salt industry.

INFANTILE MORTALITY. - Infantile mortality reached rather a high total in Lymm during 1912. The figure given by the Cheshire Medical Officer is 124 per 1,000, ;the sixth highest in the • county.

WHEN MEASLES PREVAILED.—The year. 1912 will be long remembered in Lymm as the "measles year." According torP.T. Young, the county medical officer, there Weie 307 cases, which con- stituted a record. The next highest figure was 161 cases in 1904, while in 1909 there was only one case, but of the 307 cases last year there were only two deaths. CHOIR TRlP.—Acc6rding to the number of local parties who have visited Southport dur- ing the last few years, it appears that this combined garden city and seaside resort is quite a popular one with Lymm people. On Monday the members and friends of the Congregational Church Choir made the above town the venue of their annual picnic. Favoured with a beautiful day, the various members of the party explored the sights of the charming streets and parks, and participated In the allurements of the shore.

SUNDAY* •SCHOLARS AT Sornirown—The Lymm Church Sunday Schools' annual excursion took place on Wednesday under the choicest of weather conditions. This year the trip was arranged for Southport, and a large number of scholars, parents and friends travelled by the special train which left Lvmm at 7.30 a.m. On .arrival at ;this pleasant Lancashire resort the visitors were left to enjoy the many and varied attractions as their fancy dictated, the "White City" and the Marine Lake being great favour- ites: The party' assetnbled at the Temperance Institute for a tea, which was -much appreci- ated. A safe return to Lymm, after a happy outing, was achieved at about 10 p.m.

BAPTIST SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVEILSARY.—TIIO scholars of the Baptist Sunday School celebrated their anniversary on Sunday, when the Rev. Percy Burnett was the special preacher. Appro- priate and helpful sermons were preached at the morning and evening services, the subject at the former being " Christ blessing the children," and at the latter the text of Zech- ariah viii. 5. Miss Ina Jenks and Mr. Wallace Ray, both of Warrington, were the soloists at the morning and evening services respectively. Miss Brundrett and Miss M. Brundrett sang a duet at the latter gathering. In the afternoon Mr. J. H. Holt presided over a children's ser- vice, at which a solo by Miss Jenks, and hymns by the scholars were contributed. There was also an excellent children's address on "A penny," from which the lessons of being (1) round, (2) true, (3) useful, and (4) bearing the image of the King, were given. Miss IL Brun- drett was the organist. The attendance was very satisfactory at each of the services. INDEPENDENT METHODISTS' NEW ORGAN.—Follow- ing the purchase of a new organ, the Lymm Independent Methodists held a special opening service in the Sion Chapel on Wednesday even- ing. Mr. G. L. Welford conducted 'the service, which was very well attended, and in a few words explained the reason for the gathering and introduced the preacher, the Rev. W. Paw, of Altrincham. Speaking from the words: "And when they had sung an hymn," Mr. Paw told of the incidents that surrounded the text, and said that though Christ knew of the trials that Awaited Him, He could still join in singing• a hymn. The preacher showed how the " masters " had interpreted the various phases of life in their music, .and said that in the realm of devotion it was a fitting handmaid to Christianity, being an inarticulate expression of speech, or an expression of the inexpressible. Music was the most spiritual of the arts, and was used in worship because it strengthened the .soul. Christ had met His troubles with song, for, after the singing of a hymn He Went out to more than sorrow—His death. Though .our knowledge of-heaVen was-not very extensive, we could be sure that there would be music., no doubt even grander -than that which we knew. There was nothing to which this ambitious art did not strive to rise, and it 'was, therefore, very -fitting for use in worShiP. The preacher 'desired that in the use. of the new 'organ for worship, its strains would so enter the lives of those that heard it that4heir future deeds and words would be rendered into a harmonious psalm. Miss Eva Tanner, of Warrington, was the soloist, and gava• pleasant renderings of "Beyond," and "Lead, kindly light." The organist. was Mr. F. Atkinson, of Warrington, whose playing throughout the evening brought out to the full the beauty of the instrument. The organ, which is one of the celebrated Karn manufacture, has a compass of five octaves, with nineteen stops, nine sets of reeds, and the usual couplers: The case is composed of some beau- tifully figured oak. The volume of the instru- ment, which is well sustained, is of a rich quality, together with a wonderful sweetness and purity of tone.

PENKETH.

GARDEN PARTY AT PENKETH LonoE.—Mr. and Mrs. C. E. Parker entertained the members of the Wesleyan Men's -Bible class, 'their wives and friends, at a garden. party at Penketh Ledge on Saturday. The beautiful gardens hie looking their best, aiml the varieties of roses called forth admiring comments. An excellent tea was served, and afterwards cricket, bowls and other games were indulged in. The com- pany expressed their -warmest thanks to the host and hostess, on the' proposition of Mr. J. J. Foden, seconded by Mr. G. Carman.

DEATH OF MISS DALE.—We regret to announce the death, which took place on Monday, of Miss Emma Dale,- Warrington-road, Penketh. The deceased had been ill for some weeks, buE the end came rather unexpectedly. Miss Dale, who was 54 years of ago, was greatly respected. The funeral took place on Thursday at the Sankey Cemetery, the Rev. J. R. Jones officiat- ing..

IMITATION.

The Editor, Dear Sir, Being myself of a fanciful turn of mind I am much given to dreams at night by which my rest is greatly disturbed, and my mind often not a little perplexed during the day for the remembrance of my dreams is then very present with me. Concerning one of these dreams. lam especially in won- der, and I have at last conceived the idea of recounting the same to you, thinking that You may be able to offer some solution of the mystery. I leave it to your consideration. ' Coming home somewhat late from a supper party held at the largest, newest and there- fore most popular p:ace of resort for such festivities, I dismissed my servant, who was waiting for me, and putting on a smoking- jacket sat down by my bedroom fire. I suppose the cold air without, et. pos- sibly the vintage of which I had partaken= it was not of my choosing and I thought it thin and yet heady--rendered me suddenly sleepy. Be that as it may, I found myself, it seemed to me, standing alone in a large and mpty place. All around me was grass, poor and much trodden down; tracks also ran across it marked by a white and dusty substance. Near me was the remains of a thick hawthorn hedge, trodden and broken down in many places, and those leaves which remained were nearly hidden by the same whitish, dusty substance. Through the gaps in the hedge I could see what had been, it seemed to me, a garden or an orchard, for here and there still stood apple and sear trees of good size—l knew them by their form of growth; their leaves and fruit, if they bore any. were obscured like the hawthorn by the whitish deposit. Nearer at hand were the remains of 'a pleasant parterre on which some graceful leaden statues lay in broken heaps of confusion. Across the edge of the parterre were planks hoth new and old, and a large heap of the white material was flanked by piles of bricks and drainpines. To my left, cuttincr sharply into the sky-line, was a huge half-finished structure, formless and ugly. To my right, stretching rrom the plain on which I stood, as it seemed, to +he horizon, ran rows of small hou,es—parallel rows without number, built of red brick, but all unfinished, and all surrounded by scaffold- ing:. I was looking about me for some one to question when a man suddenly stood beside me—where he came from I know not. But to him I turned, and rnestioned him. "Where am I?" I said. "What is this place?"

"You are in the newest of the kingdoms of this world," he sairl. "Are Ton a dweller in it ?" I asked him, a,rd I turned to look at him more closely as I said it. _

" Oh, yes," he said, "I am a dweller in this kingdom. I was not born in it, but I am a naturalised subject of many years' stand- ing. I married, a fellow subjeet. and our children are natives of this realm." He paused; the light grew clearer and. I saw that my interlocutor was a man in the prime of Ffe, that is to say between forty and forty-five. He wore a high silk hat, a by no means perfectly cut frock-coat, and dnric grey trousers. He carried an umbrella with an unobtrueive handle, a folded newsparer and his gloves—which were of a serviceable brown dogskin. "We are rather An the outskirts Of the realm here." he said pointing to the mnfin- ;shed building and ,the demolished parterre. "Here building has only just begun. but in a few months this will be a large centre with shops, recreation ground, town hall and all that can be desired." "What are the limits of the realm ?" I said. "What are the, boundaries ?" He shook his head "There are no limits. Th;s kingdom, unlike all others, enlarp:es its boundaries every day. Did I not tell you that it was the newest of all kingdoms?"

The light increased. and with every mo- ment the place in which we stood seemed less attractive and more desolate.

Can I see more of the kingdom ?" I asked. "I cannot surely have seen the most pleasant portion." "By no means," he returned confidently. "If you desire to see more come with me. You have but to touch the handle of my um- brella, and you shall be transported to any part of the realm I think best to show I touched the handle held out to me. The plain, the parterre, the small houses, all van- ished. and we found ottraelves standing In

===== 0002601_19130802_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
LITERARY GLEANINGS.

PRIDE OF BIRTH. THE WAR BETWEEN SERIOUS STUDENTS AND PEDIGREE- MONGERS.

Ancient'lineage ! I never heard of a peer with an ancient lineage. The real old families of this country are to be found among the peasantry; the gentry, too, may lay some claim to old blood. I can point you out Saxon families in Lancashire who can trace their pedigrees beyond the Con- quest ; I know of some Norman gentlemen whose fathers undoubtedly came over with the Conqueror. But a peer with an ancient lineage is to me quite a novelty. No, no; the thirty years of the Wars of the Roses freed us from those gentlemen. When Henry VII. called his first Parliament there were only twenty-nine temporal peers to be found. Of those twenty-nine but five remain, and they, as the Howards, for instance, are not Norman nobility. But let Lancashire remember, and just now with special pride, that among those twenty-nine a Stanley found his place. To be descended from Charles 11. (who had no legitimate issue) is the proudest boast of three English Dukes and one Scottish. Another of the same order exults in the double illegitimacy which connects him with the Plantagenets ; and similar instances might be found nearer our own time. Swinburne gibbeted these sordid splendours in verses which will scarcely bear transcription, though they do exceed- ingly well in the peroration of a speech against the hereditary powers of the House of Lords; but the worst of it is that while these discreditable descents are certain and historic, the most respectable pedigrees are honeycombed with fiction and imposture. When Thackeray traced the ancestry of Sir Thomas Muggins to Hogyn-Mogyn of the Hundred Beeves," he was only very slightly caricaturing the exploits of the pedigree-mongers; and these pedigree-mongers have always been at war with the historically-minded students of genealogy. Mr. Ardenne, who was Lothair's neighbour- in the country, "bad an ancient pedigree—which was all very well—but also knew everybody else's, which was not always so pleasant." Mr. Ardenne was, I believe, drawn from that accomplished antiquary Evelyn Philip Shirley, of Ettington ; and his function of abating the pride of false pediarees is admirably performed in the present day by Mr. James Horace Round, who derives our most exalted nobility from valets and pig-keepers and fish-mongers. These iconoclastic triumphs would have delighted Lord Beaconsfield, who, being himself descended from the most ancient and most famous race now extant in the world, could afford to laugh at the genealogical assumptions of" the flat-nosed barbarians" among whom he condescended to dwell.— The " Manchester Guardian" in a review of a work on "Genealogy."

===== 0002601_19130802_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
AS YOUNG AS A CHOIR BOY.

"He had the fine sense of honour, the intense attachment to the institutions of his country, the perfervid churchmanship, which are the root principles of the Con- servative party. His churchmanship, indeed, was a guiding clue to his opinions and his career. Bountiful nature had given this strong-built and lithe athlete a beautiful voice; and this hard-worked advocate, this politician with the long hours of court followed by the long hours of the House of Commons, gave his Sundays to the choir of his church, and sang there as regularly and as fervently as though he were a choir boy making his living."—Mr. T. P. O'Connor, M.P., writing on Lord Alverstone in the August issue of "The Pall Mall Magazine."

SOME NEW ANECDOTES OF MARK

===== 0002601_19130802_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
TWAIN.

Some interesting reminiscences of Mark Twain, written by Mrs. Allen, the lady in whose house, in Bermuda, he stayed during the last months of his life, appear in the August number of the " Strand Magazine." She writes that" Mark Twain, although the creator of the most lovable boy in literature, Tom Sawyer,' was really more interested in little girls, and it was through his interest and affection for my little daughter Helen that we came to know him so well and to share the last months of his life. He used to pretend that only girls were interesting, that boys ought not to exist until they were men. The fact was, he really was interested in any young creature. In one of the books he gave Helen he wrote: 'lt is better to be a young June-beetle than an old bird of paradise.' "One day when he came to 'Bay House' to bathe in the sea with Helen, he told us of an experience of his the night before at a little speech he gave at the hospital. He said he had been told that he was to be introduced by the Chief Justice, but he had not met him. Arriving in good season, he was shown to the platform, and there greeted by several old friends, besides some that he did not know, Presently the house filled, 'Royalty' arrived and was seated, but there was no sign of the Chief Justice. He grew uneasy, feeling it dis- courteous to keep the audience waiting, and was just about to say to the young man seated beside him—in fact, his mouth was forming the words—' If that infernal Chief Justice would only come, we might begin,' when the 'young man' arose and pro- ceeded to introduce him!

"It was during this stay in Bermuda that Mark Twain decided to have an aquarium of his own, 'with little girls instead of fishes and himself as the only shad in the pond.' And Helen was one of the first to be decorated with the badge of the order, which was a little angel-fish brooch, enamelled in the natural colours. He told me that sometimes, when he felt very humble, he would be a minnow, but he was afraid he would be the shad most of the time!

"He had the lifelong habit of under- scoring anything he thought true or beautiful in the book or magazine he was reading. I found this quotation much underscored in a magazine he read while he was with us: It has been said that a man's last will and testament best expresses his character. Does it? Do we not rather know a man best from the 'simple act, look or speech of daily life, when the consciousness is unaware? ' "

===== 0002601_19130802_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
COLD COMFORT.

Alas, why will you be so cold? My love for you is deep and strong; Forgive me if I seem too bold. I've waited for you, oh, so long!

You're so desirable and sweet, I may be poor, what do I care? You shall be mine—yet when we meet YOU chill me, I am in despair. Forget the shadows that have passed, The present sunshine shall suffice : You will not melt? Ah yes, at last, My slightly over-frozen ice ! "The World."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 2, 1913.

"I REMEMBER

MR. PIERPOINT'S STORY OF THE 1868 ELECTION.

A Dramatic Discovery.

HOW MR. RYLANDS SUPPLANTED MR. GREENALL.

[To THE EDITOR OF THE "EXAMINER."] Sir,—ln your issue of June 28th appeared an interesting article contributed by an anonymous correspondent. Perhaps some little amplification may be of interest. In the 1847 election there was a song : Greenall -for ever, Alleard Alleard 1- J Greenall in the river, C'atehing fishes, for Mr. Greenall's} dinner. for Mr. Alleard's I cannot suggest that I sang this song at the time, as I doubt my being old enough to do so. If I did a year or two later I cannot say which version I used, as at my then small age my political principles were incomplete. I remember hearing a great deal about the 1868 election at Warrington. I was not at home at the time. I was, then, in fact, an undergraduate at Oxford, where I listened to a speech of thanks to the electors of the city of Oxford delivered on the hustings by the late Sir William Haicourt, then Mr. Vernon Harcourt, after he had been elected to Parlia- ment fcr the first time as colleague of the Right Hon. Edward Cardwell. As to the election at Warrington, although I can speak only from hearsay, I think that the following will be found fairly correct. The election of 1868 was the last General Election of open voting. It was customary for each candidate's agent to send cards to voters marked with the name of the candi- date. At each of the polling places there were three clerks, one appointed by each candidate and one by the Mayor. Importance of Verbal Declaration. It so happened that at one of the polling stations at, I think, dinner-time, there arrived some seventy to eighty voters,who, with the intention of voting, handed their cards to the Mayor' clerk. These intended votes were nearly all for Mr. Greenall. Of course, the clerks of the two candidates did their best to record the votes as they came in. When the polling was over the books of both candidates, although the figures differed, placed Mr. Greenall at the head of the poll, and he went home satisfied with the result. However, when the figures in the Mayor's clerk's book had been added up and subtracted, it was found by the official return that Mr. Ryle.nds had a majority of twenty-seven. The figures WE re : Peter By lands 1,984 Gilbert Greenall ...... 1,957 Mr. Rylands was declared by the Mayor to be the elected member. It was discovered that in the hurry of business the Mayor's clerk, a very young man, had put those seventy to eighty cards into his pocket and torgotten them. He had had, no doubt, full intention to record these intended votes. There never was, as far as I know, the slightest suspicion that he had not meant to do so. Of course, a petition was lodged on the part of Mr. Greenall. It was tried by Baron Martin, who quickly ruled that the handing in of a card with a candidate's name on it was not a legal tender of a vote; that in voting a voter had either to say, "I vote for so and so," or on the question being put to him, "For whom do you vote ? " to answer, "So and so." On this ruling he confirmed the election of Mr. Rylands. Voting by Card. It happened that some years after this when a student at the Bar, I was a pupil of the late Mr. John Edwards, a prominent barrister on the Northern Circuit, afterwards Queen's Counsel. He told me that he had been junior Counsel for Mr. Rylands, with (I think) Mr. Quain, Q.C., afterwards a judge, as his leader, and that before the case came on Baron Martin asked them how long the case would take. The answer was, "A very short time." This reply was given because both Quain and Edwards thought that they had not a leg to stand on. To their great surprise the judge ruled as above, and they won their case in a very short time. It is not for me to say that Baron Martin's ruling was wrong, but there can be little doubt that scores of votes were tendered by cards and registered both in Warring- ton and elsewhere in the 1868 and other elections before that date. There was something of a tragedy at the end of this affair. The unfortunate but perfectly honest clerk went out of his mind and died at a very early age. I am, Sir, Your obedient servant, ROBERT PIERPOINT.

IN SHAKESPEARE'S COUNTRY.

Some of the Scenes and Interests of

Stratford-on-Avon.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
ANTIQUITIES AND MEMORIES OF A FAMOUS SHRINE.

Holiday pleasures spring oftenest from unexpected things. So came the chief happiness of Stratford-on-Avon. We had understood that the house in which Shakespeare closed his days had long since been rased to the ground and that no vestige remained. Except for scraps of brick foundations, seen through iron gratings, this is quite true. Yet strangely enough, it was this devastated spot that brought the poet most surely into vision.

Let the reader, if he will, study our ancient picture. The low building to the right is the Grammar School, of which tradition has made Shakespeare a scholar. More than this, tradition has marked the oaken pillar under which he sat with a tablet of brass. Upon the ground floor, in a long low room which served as the Guild- hall, we are shown the spot where his youthful eyes first opened wider at the sight of strolling players upon a stage. So says tradition: and nobody who sees the place will desire to challenge it. If Shakespeare did not come to the Grammar School he ought to have done, and there was surely no more suitable place in Stratford for a lad to get stage struck than in the dim and low-ceiled chamber of the Guildhall.

the chancel wall flows the stately Avon, above the tomb rise the walls of one of the most stately and beautiful of our English churches. THE CHURCH.

Quite apart from its association with Shakespeare this edifice would merit a serious pilgrimage. It is a collegiate church; though the monastic buildings have long since perished. Lofty and fine proportions give the nave a beauty which some cathedrals lack, and high clerestory windows flood it with light. A full score of interesting things cling to the church, some of which our readers may be glad for us to mention. THE SANCTUARY KNOCKER.

The door by which we enter carries a sanctuary knocker, with massive ring, and of a style that suggests the Thirteenth Century. Seven centuries ago when the knocker was first hung here fugitives ran to the church for safety, and those who could slip a hand through the ring were able to claim protection of the church for seven and thirty days. In the chamber above the porch, which may still be seen, a monk remained by day and night to open the door. We felt ourselves in a far, far age as we handled the ring and looked down the centuries.

GRAMMAR SCHOOL, GUILD CHAPEL AND NEW PLACE.

indifferent to the processions of motor cars that stop at its base, while a guide, in a measured minute, enriches the minds of open-eyed Yankees. This sweet old tower and chapel, ever so much more beautiful than this old and scarcely accurate picture suggests, appeals most strongly to the antiquarian. It rose in the Fifteenth Century as the finishing touch to a fine chapel which had been built at least a century earlier. The chapel with its painted walls would be familiar enough to Shakespeare, and we may speculate as to what mental impressions were made by the horrible fresco in which some truly appalling devils hook the bodies of the condemned into furnaces and fan the flames with domestic bellows.

Though familiar with the chapel from within, Shakespeare lived to be much more familiar with its walls from without. Its clock would stir him as he crept " like snail unwillingly to school," and he spent the last nineteen years of his life under its shadow. And an imagination like his must have taken wing at the sight of those crumbling stones. Did he turn back to recall that the hands which carved them were soon to be stilled in death by the black plague which devastated the land? Did he see its stones glowing ashen grey in the light of the fires that had swept his native town? The Curfew bell in its tower, that once rung bed andlsupper-time to the active lad, tolled now to the matured man as evening closed.

The note of

So the entry of the baptism. burial reads : 1616, April 25th, WIII, Shatcspeare, gent.

Close at hand is a chained Bible, a copy of the edition of 1611. THE FONT.

There is also the Fifteenth Century font, from which Shakespeare was baptized, a poor battered fragment of its former self. Flung out from the church it had not the good fortune to be appropriated as a pig trough, but, falling to human uses, became a water cistern. Not until 1823 did anyone wake up to a sense of its interest, and at a later stage the forlorn and shapeless stone was carried back to the church. DID THE POET WRITE HIS OWN EPITAPH?

We travel hence to the central point of interest, the stone that marks the poet's resting-place. It is a nameless slab, bear- ing only the quaint lines known the world over:

Good friend for Jesus sake forbear° To dillig the dust encloased heare i Blest beitye man yt spares then stones And curst be he yt moves my bones.

The origin and intention of the lines has stirred much curiosity. That our match- less poet should prepare such dogerell for his tomb is among the unlikely things. Tradition, very busy here, as in everything, has a plausible explanation. For the advantage of those who have heard and believed it we have traced it to its source. It is in a diary under date July, 1777 : "At the side of the chancel is a charnel- house almost filled with human bones, skulls, &e. The guide said that Shakes- peare was so affected by this charnel-house that he wrote the epitaph for himself to prevent his bones being thr9wn into it." That such bone-houses did exist in the careless old days is beyond question, and any sensitive man may have shrunk from the thought of vulgar disinterments. It is not impossible that Shakespeare wrote his own epitaph, and that he deliberately put it in the form of a rough threat. One thing is certain that either by virtue of the blessing or the curse his bones have lain secure.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW PLACE.

For Shakespeare's house and garden lay under the shadow of the Guild Chapel tower, with only the narrow Chapel-lane between. _ _ _

Of this house we must say a little. Alas, that it has to be ranked among the things of the past! It was built by Sir Hugh Clopton, in the time of Henry VII., of timber and brick, and war, in the words of Leland,who visited it." a pratty house." Shakespeare, coming back from his successes as a London play- wright, bought it for £6O, a sum perhaps equal to £5OO of our money. . It passed to his daughter Susannah Hall, and then to Lady Barnard, the last descendant of the poet. Later on, it came again into the Clopton family, and Sir Hugh adorned it by the addition of a new front. It is this ornate front which appears in our illustra- tion. From Sir Hugh it passed to a son-in- law, and thence to a Francis Gastrell, a minister, in 1753. This irritable gentleman has never been, ' and never will be, forgiven of the town of Stratford. Annoyed that people came to look at the Mulberry tree, which Shakes-. peare was said to have planted, he cut down the tree. Annoyed that he was charged with poor rate upon a house in which he only lived for part of the year, he declared, in anger, Lhat the house should never be assessed again, and gave effect to his vow by demolishing. it. Only the foundations and well can be seen through protecting gratings, but the site was bought in 1861 and is held by trustees. We can walk in Shakespeare's garden at will. The grey windows of the chapel rise above us, and the birds sing cheerily in a quietness that seems too rich and good for this troubled world. - -

The same ,thought had touched us both, and Professor Arnim put it into words: "It would be just like this 300 years ago; those walls would be the last things he would see. At last I feel quite close to Shakespeare." WHERE SHAKESPEARE RESTS.

It is not far from New Place to Stratford Church. The Guild Tower bell would toll an unwonted strain as his body was carried to the church and laid to rest within the altar rails, an honoured place of burial that fell to him as an owner of the tithes. It is an ideal resting-place for a lover of Nature and of the beautiful. A few paces beyond

THE MONUMENT.

Upon the chancel wall above is his monumert, too high to be examined, but familiar by reason of countless pictures. It is not a work of art, but gives us the most we shall ever know of the form of that matchless brain. The inscription reads :

Judicio Pylium, Genic) Socratem, arta Maronem. Terra tegit, populus meret. Olympus habet.

In wisdom a Nestor, in genius a Socrates, in art a Virgil. The earth shrouds him, the nation mourns him, Olympus guards him.]

Stay passenger, Why goest thou by so fast P Read, if thou canst, whom envious death hath plant Within this monument ; Shakespeare with whome, Quick Nature dide ; whose name doth deck ye tombe Far more than cost; ssith all yt he hath writt, Leaves living art but page to serve his witt.

Obiit Ano. Doi, 1616. Aetatis 53, Die 23. Ap.

Seven years later Anne Shakespeare, the Anne Hathaway of his love story, was laid to rest by his side, and her memorial stone carved with a Latin epitaph. Here also rest his daughter Susanna and her husband Dr. Hall, whose epitaphs space denies the quoting. Close by, in the chancel, are some fine old miserere seats upon which much might be written, especially as some of the carvings which decorate them are said to have inspired "The Taming of the Shrew." Whether or not they set his thoughts moving, these monkish fancies cut in wood, crude and painfully quaint, must have

Continued in next column

IN SHAKESPEARE'S COUNTRY.--Continued. given him keen delight, and set a-moving countless mirthful fancies. Though carved to adorn a church, the " Shrew " pictures could not now be repro- duced; no not even upon a Blackpool picture post-card. THE ORIGINAL OF OPHELIA.

In the North aisle is the Clopton Chapel which holds some worthy memorials of the dead. ,Here sleeps Margaret Clopton, said to be the prototype of Ophelia, whose body was found in the well in her father's garden in 1592. Beautiful windows may be studied in the church, particularly the " American " window given by admirers from across the Atlantic. But the church is not a show place. It is, and will be, we trust, for ever a very temple of peace. We climbed to the eyrie of the Memorial Theatre, looked with due suspicion upon "Shakespeare's gloves " and sat with matured scepticism in " Shakespeare's Chair." We studied the Droeshout portrait which hangs in the Memorial Gallery, and examined the myriad books and hundred prints that Shakespeare has inspired, and 'which would need a long, long article to I describe.

Best of all, we watched the water fowl below the bridge, the dive dapper that lives in the pages of the poet: Like a dive-dapper, peering thro' a wave, Who, being looked on, ducks as quickly in.

But perhaps our readers have not our own deep wonder at all that belongs to Shakespeare, and a long-drawn descrip - tion may not appeal to them. To Shakespeare lovers we have just a word. A visit to Stratford-on-Avon brings Shakespeare consciously nearer, and we doubt whether any can be true Shakes' pearians until they have been. . A. M. •

TILI:L

===== 0002601_19130802_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB. ,

A TALK ABOUT EARL SHAFTESBURY.

I don't suppose any of my readers ever heard of Marie Millis, yet England owes to her a great debt. She was a nurse girl in humble circumstances, and it happened that she became the nurse of the famous Earl Shaftesbury. This honoured gentleman was one of the greatest philanthropists our land has ever had. The good impulse which made him great did not come from his parents, who were worldly and aristocratic, but from his nurse, who taught him to pray and to love the poor. Maria Millis is a name unknown to fame, yet few preachers have ever preached more effectively or to greater purpose. The story of the love of Christ told to him before he reached his seventh year filled his heart and vision through life. In his old age he said: "My theology has never changed; I hold three tenets, a Divine Saviour, an atoning sacrifice and a coming kingdom," and he delighted to honour the memory of the servant girl who wrote that theology upon his heart. To his death he wore the old watch she gave him and would never exchange it for another.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHOICE OF A CAREER.

At the age of fourteen he chose his career. During an afternoon walk he wit- nessed the funeral of a friendless pauper and saw the body tumbled into a hole by drunken men. The sight touched him to pity and he vowed in that same hour that he would devote his life to the service of the friendless poor. The pledge was nobly kept. He literally gave up all that he had. He resigned honours of State, luxuries of home, time for intellectual culture, which was perhaps his greatest sacrifice, and spent all his income to help the helpless. The deeper the need the greater their claim to his service. When he was urged to accept a place in the Ministry of the realm, he replied, " I cannot satisfy myself that to accept office is a Divine call: but I am satisfied that God has called me to labour among the poor." When, a little later, a position of high honour was offered, he said, "One million and six hundred thousand operatives are still excluded from the benefits of the Factory Acts, and so long as they are unprotected I cannot take office."

===== 0002601_19130802_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
IN LABOURS ABUNDANT.

And now let us try to tell in brief and fragmentary fashion some of the doings of Earl Shaftesbury. During a public life of fifty years he was identified with more movements for the raising of the fallen than any man who ever lived. All his power of oratory, all the influence of his noble lineage, were given to outcasts. In the House of Lords again and again he pleaded the unpopular cause of the poor. In the face of bitterest opposition he carried Bills to shorten labour, to give educational advantages and sanitary improvements, to reform lunatic asylums, to relieve the crippled and blind, to restrict the sale of opium, and to prevent Sabbath labour. He held flower-shows among poor girls, rewarding them with prizes for the care of house-plants. In ringing sentences he called attention to the sufferings of chimney-sweeps, the cruel treatment of children for circus shows, and the snares laid to ruin girlhood.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
LORD SHAFTESBURY'S AUTOGRAPH.

We read of the Earl going out at night gathering in boys, whom he found in the gutters or huddled in beds of rotten straw in old cellars, and leading them to the ragged school, where he sat by their side and inspired in them longings for a better life.

On one occasion he visited a company of five hundred criminals of the most daring sort, without weapon or guard, and courteously told them how to get away from sin to an honest life.

At St. Giles's Refuge he was often the centre of a group of ragged homeless boys, listening to their confessions of wrongdoing and planning better lives for them. The bootblacks called him "our earl." During one hard winter the half-starved gamins were fed with ten thousand basins of soup and bread made in his own house. For thirty-two years he was president of the Ragged School Union, and when they presented him with a testimonial he said, "I would rather preside over the Ragged School Union than command armies or

sway empires."

AN EXPERIMENT.

Among the many strange people which make up the life of London, none had greater fascination for him than the coster- mongers. They lived together in poverty and dirt, and were very clannish and suspicious of outsiders.

The donkeys they drove were always members of the family, and lived with them in hovels they called homes. Lord Shaftesbury saw that to help them he must become himself a costermonger. He joined their society, and bought a donkey and barrow, which he lent to those that had none. Entirely free from all lordly airs, he mingled with the costers as their brother, encouraged Sunday rest among them, and offered prizes for the care of their beasts.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
K.G. AND COSTER.

By a stroke of happy and playful genius he entirely won their hearts. He'belonged to England's most famous Order of the Garter and could sign himself K.G. In his letters to the Coster Society he used to .sign himself K.G. and Coster. Surely there never was such a combination of titles before. It is no wonder the costers came to love him. On one occasion they decided to show their affection by the presentation of a fine donkey. He • rose to accept the gift, and putting his arm round the donkey's neck, said, "I ask for no epitaph beyond this, that with a patience as great and a resignation as unmurmuring as my donkey's, I may do my own duty." When the animal was led from the plat- form, he turned to the reporters, and begged them to state that "the donkey having vacated the chair, his place was filled by Lord Shaftesbury." Some most beautiful incidents are related concerning him. When he Was on his way to receive military honours as lord- lieutenant of his county, he gave up his place in the carriage to a lame old woman, and himself rode on the box with the coachman.

At a flower-show where a poor little girl had won a prize for the care of a plant, he gave the prize with his own hand, and then stooped to kiss the pinched face of the child, who did not care that he was an earl, but knew him only as the tenderest and kindest man her forlorn little life had ever known. It probably never occurred to him that it was anything unusual for a nobleman to go about with pockets full of toys for poor children, or to read and pray with the sick and dying. He had boundless faith in the power of the gospel to uplift and save. He opened Exeter Hall and city theatres for non- churchgoers, and himself led those vast meetings with the earnestness of an evangelist. They were largely attended,

and thousadds were unable to gain admission.

He grew old and feeble prematurely. He carried th!, sorrows and sufferings of a nation, and his great heart broke under it.

When dying he : "I feel old age creeping on, and know I soon must die. I hope it is not wrong, but I cannot bear to leave this world with all the suffering in it."

When in 1885 he died, a nation wept. The common people, who loved him, filled the streets that led to Westminster Abbey. Seamstresses, bootblacks, reformed criminals, factory-hands, each wearing a simple badge of mourning, and weepinc, in an abandonment of grietstood with royalty and the nobility as mourners around his bier and on his coffin were flowers from the Queen and from the London flower-girls.

PA WOULDN'T STAND FOR IT.

"There's a good deal said against horse racing," says august Belmont "It all makes me think of a little Hempstead lad. "This little lad's teacher set him the following problem : " If one horse can run two miles in 3 minutes and 10 seconds, and another can run two miles in 4 minutes and 8 seconds, by how much would the first horse beat the second in a six-mile race ? ' "The Jittle boy returned this problem, unsolved, with the following explanatory note: "' Pa says that I must never have nothing to do with horse racing.'"

===== 0002601_19130802_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
PARDONABLE DISGUST.

"Oh, I jusflove cake, and it's awfully nice ! "cried little Dorothy, regarding her chocolate-frosted dessert with high approval. "You should not say you love cake'" reproved her mother ; say, you like it,' and don't say 'awfully, say very.' Don't ' say nice.' but good. And by the way, the word just' should be omitted, and also the oh.' Now,, my dear, repeat the sentence correctly,' " I like cake; it is very good," repeated Dorothy. "That is much better," said the mother. But Dorothy was far from being satis- fied. "It sounds as if I were speaking of bread," she said, with an air of disgust.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN MEN "SEE RED."

To murder in their sleep unsuspecting soldiers with whom you smoked and. chatted, and played cards a short while before, is a crime .which most cultured peoples would recoil from in horror. The Bulgarians perpetrated it for their country's sake. A few others shrank from the atrocity. One of these—an officer— deserted rather than fulfil the task allotted him. He came to Bucharest and . made statements, some of which burned them- selves into my memory. He said: "My heart • sank within me at the thought of stealing I.IP eat-like to thest brave soldiers, transfixing them with bayonets and then butcher- ing these comrades while they slept. That is not soldiers' work. For the Serbs - were not on their guard against us. They had been, and still were, our friends, our comrades in arms. I had not the hearf to bayonet them. Perhaps it was the horrors I had witnessed during the campaign. against the Turks that unnerved me. They were ghastly. We ' saw red' con- tinually. After a time some of us were unable to look upon red wine without sickening." History will draw a veil over these abominations, and contemporariep will forget them soon. But there are times when it is well to realise how near to the surface the human beast lies vigilant in. some nations hastily set down as civilised. —Dr. Dillon, in the August "Contemporary Review."

ONCE A PART OF EVERY BIBLE.

In Harper's Magazine, James Thompson Bixby tells the story of the fourteen books which in the days of our grandfathers-appeared in the Bible betwLeen the- Old and New Testanients.

The name Apocrypha now attached to these fourteen books means primarily "The Hidden." Originally the word Apocryphal meant simply the books whose origin was obscure or whose usage and meaning were secret. Neither among Hebrew scholars nor in the early Christian Church did the word imply that these books were either untrustworthy. spurious, or unworthy of religious use and reverence. They have been printed, therefore, as part of Holy Scripture in the Septuagint, .the Vulgate, and most Roman Catholic Bibles up to the present time. In the Lutheran, Reformed, and English Bibles for nearly three centuries after Luther's break with Rome they were printed, and they have been read in Protestant pulpits during ' the same period as useful and good for edification, i although not authoritative n proof of Christian doctrine. The Homilies of the Anglican Church are still full of eltations from the Apocrypha on an equal level with the canonical Old Testament. It was not till the year 1826 that the British and American Bible Societies

BANISHED THEM AS 'UNINSPIRED from the company of the other Biblical writings with which for eighteen centuries they had been contributing to the moral instruction and spiritual nurture of Christendom. Have these ancient books between the Testa- ments deserved this sentence of banishment from Christian knowledge' and service? The custom- ary reason given for this ecclesiastical boycott has been that -they were "uncanonioal." Many Christian scholars, however, doilbt both th wisdom and the justice of this sentence. *For with the rise of modern Biblical criticism the reasons on vrhich theologians had leant, such .as 'hat they were written in Greek, not in holy Hebrew, that ancient rabbis and scholars, like Jerome, had doubted their canonicity, and Pro- testant council:: questioned their inspiration, have one by one lost their former force. Modern 4cholars think that the Divine Spirit was quite as tikely to dictate religious revelation in Greek as in Hebrew, and that, in respeot to scholarly xnowledge acd judgment of what is spiritual truth, modern critics and theologians are as corn- ?etent to decide as ancient rabbis or the Doctors lf Divinity of 100 or 200 years ago.

LIBERALS AND LABOUR IN CREWE

DIVISION.

A conference was held at Crewe on Monday night of representatives of the Liberal and Labour parties with a view to agreeing, if pos- sible, upon some arrangement by which a three- cornered contest at the next election might be avoided. At present Mr. Joseph Davies, Dinas Powis, Cardiff, is the prospective Liberal can- didate, and Mr. James Holmes is the prospec- tive Labour candidate. The conference was held on thei.initiative of Mr. Holmes. After a long sitting the conference was adjourned for a week.
