Well, au revoir, I shall only just be in time,,, And, with these words, Sir Charles Rodley hiirried out of his wife's boudoir. A minute later he had sprung into the high dog-cart, pacwhiretod towards the pony, and was off at a smart rPds Cockminster Station, which was seven miles distant. When he at last 'drew rein, the London ex- press was. just in. As Sir Charles reached the Platform some half-dozen persons were in the act of alighting. He hastily scanned their faces in search of his ward. There were three men, one woman very much the wrong side of fifty, and two young girls. Now, which of the latter was the young lady he was in search of? he wondered. But his doubts were quickly solved. He saw one young lady and her numerous packages claimed by an old lady at the other end of the platform. So, boldly tak- ing his fate in his hands, he marched up to the remaining young lady and, raising his hat, said: "Pardon me, but are you Miss Richardson?" The girl quietly bowed assent. Sir Charles had prepared a hundred pleae sant speeches of welcome, he had thought over all the little tactful remarks he would make, and he bad considered with exactly what degree of intimacy he should greet Angela Richardson. But somehow they all faded .away at sight of the girl herself, and his mind became a perfect blank. For he had also decided in his own mind very much what his ward would be like —tall and ungainly, he felt sure, with very red cheeks, straw-coloured hair, and nasty, insipid blue eyes. Now the young lady he was facing was the absdlute' opposite in every par- ticular to the girl of his mental vision. She was not tall, but decidedly below the average - - height; certainly not ungainly; for her every movement asti, word, thatapoke grace. Petite was per 'she best described her; and wcert head ainly dainty from to foot. a Her features, too, were small, but perfect in every detail. She had the dearest, tiniest mouth, and the nicest shaped nose, and fine dark brown eyes, with the kindliest expres- sion in the world. Her complexion was cer- tainly not very. red, but a delicate cream colour, not too pale, either, but rich and eoft, like' the petals of a maiden's blush rose. Her hair was brown, like her eyes, and coiled in a knot at the back of her shapely little head. Sir Charles just had time to notice that she wore a large black picture hat and that ha. dress was black as well, and to wonder how on earth such a pretty little creature could be in the least objectionable, when he had to rouse himself and say: What luggage have you? Is it in the van?" "One basket trunk," she answered, and was as pretty as her face. herni hvefec:3ltowed down the platform, where he quickly found her box. "Will you mind if I leave it to be sent up?" he asked,,her. "I brought. the dog-cart to meet you. "Oh, certainly. do Net as you like, Mr. Rodley," she replied. "Only one, Sir Charles? Right, it shall be sent up almost immediately." said a despe- rately civil porter, touching his hat vigorously. the while. The girl blushed crimson. "Oh, I beg your pardon. I did not know you were Sir Charles Rodley," she siid, quickly. Sir Charles laughed pleasantly. "Oh, that is of no couseqeenee; and now, what shall we call you, young lady?" " Miss Richardson, of course," she said, with a quiet air of dignity. "Tut, tut, the little girl's got a fine idea of her own importance," said Sir Charles to himself. "Miss Richardson indeed! Why, surely her guardian, who's old enough to he her father, might he allowed to call her Angela. I wonder what she'll expect Chris- tine to call her? Hou ever, here goes, any- thing t,o please such a perfectly charming. looking young lady. Just as you wish, of course, ' he added, aloud. "I hope you will be happy with us, my dear." "I trust to be able to please your wife and yOureelf," she answered, just a trifle stiffly he fancied. "To please us? Why, my dear, it is we who are going to do all in our power to make you happy with us." "You are very, very good," she said, with a sudden clomp of manner. Her dark eyes had filled with tears. "Oh, tut, tut, my dear, don't talk about that," said Sir Charles, hastily. Mercy! She was going to get on the subject of her father and her past life, and the hysterics would soon be comiiig. 'die must be very prudent, though, really, this girl did not seem in the least the cart of person his friend had de- sciibed in his letter.

So they chatted pleasantly on the weather, the surrounding country, the merits of the pony, and the shortcomings of railway com- panies, until they reached -the old Hall. Lady Rodley came forward to greet the newcomer, and gave her a kindly kiss. The girl looked decidedly surprised at being re- ceived so warmly; bilt otherwise her be- haviour was everything that could be de sired—perfectly deferential, courteous, and pleasant. As soon as the first greetings had been exchanged, Miss Richardson was handed over to a maid to be shown her room. When they were alone, Lady Rodley turned sharply round upon her husband: .

"Alf -Carislarooke must be mad," she said

"I think so, too," replied Sir Charles. And that was all that passed between them con-, cerning the shattering of their preconceived notions regarding Miss Angela Richardson. From that day forth she found her way into their, hearts, and before a week was over it seemed as if she had been always with them, and they treated her like a daughter. She waited on Lady Rodley hand and foot, till that good matron was almost nonplussed by her devotion. In fact, she made herself quite indispensable within the space of seven short flays. And there was another person to whom she was fast becoming equally indis- pensable—Robin Roclley, the son and heir. From the first hour she saw him she had con- quered him. Not only was lie struck with her beauty—they seemed utterly congenial charac- ters. Their, tastes were so much alike, and almost unconsciously she was bringing him out in a way he had never been brought out before. Yet she never once neglected Lady Rodley; but would be ready at all times to attend to her slightest want. Nor could it be said that she' in any way laid herself out to attract Robin; she merely made herself pleasant and agreeable to him, as she did to everybody in the house. For all that, a subtle influence was fast dritwing them together. Sir Charles and Lady Rodley could scarce conceal their delight at the turn events had taken, and the latter did. everything in her power to throw the young people more and more together. For now her personal affec- tion for the girl was added to the desire for her wealth. Only one thing puzzled Lady Rodley, and troubled her somewhat it must be confessed—a certain worried, perplexed expression which kept coming over Robin's handsome young face, even in the midst of his happiest conversations with Miss Richard- son. She would have been still more puzzled could she have seen her son, on a day just -one week after Angela's arrival, pacing his room with short, nervous steps, muttering to himself the while:

"Oh, I cannot, I cannot believe it of her. She is as pure, as spotless as the angels she is named after. And yet—l knew her face haunted me from the very first ! Though I cannot think my little darling guilty of deceit for whatever reason!"

That evening, as Miss Richardson was play ing to him after dinner, Lady Rodley quietly '.ft the room. Suddenly Robin caught the girl's hands in his and said quietly, but very tenderly : "Miss Richardson, I have something to say to you, something -which will affect both our future lives, something which I feel more deeply than you can possibly imagine. Dear- est, I want- to ask you to be my wife. But first—forgive me for even doubting you—l want to know if there is anything you feel you would rather tell me before I say any more. Listen, dear, I promise to stand by you what- ever my parents may say, nothing can make any difference to me, but I do long to under- stand what is at present an absolutely im- penetrable mystery. • The expression of the girl's face changed from shy pleasure to perfect bewilderment. "What on earth do you mean?" she said. "Oh, dear, surely you know what I mean? Tell me, is it only for the sake of a home or is it the money you want?" A little light came into the girl's face. "Do not let that stand between us," she said. "I confess that I was indignant at be- ing so beaten down. I felt it was unfair that I, a lady, should be paid far less than your cook. But I had nowhere else to go, so I had to come here. And oh, indeed, I have for- gotten everything in the intense kindness which I have received-here."

It was Robin's turn to look surprised now. "What, in the name -of goodness, do you mean?" he said.

" Why, when I engaged to come as com- panion to your. mother, I asked twenty-five pounds a year. I thought it was-" She never got any further. Robin inter- rupted her in a voice of utter bewilderment: Companion to my mother! Twenty-five Pounds a year! Why, you call yourself Angela Richardson, my father's ward, and in a year's time you will have twelve thousand a year in your own right." "Mr. Rodley, have you taken leave of your senses? My name is Pansy, not Angela. Richardson, and I am a penniless clergyman's daughter, not a rich heiress. I am your mother's companion, not. your father's ward...

Suddenly -S. strange light spread over Robin's face. He began faintly to under- stand. He drew her gently to him, and kissed her on the forehead. _

"My darling; forgive me, how could I ever doubt you," he said, tenderly. Then the humour of the situation struck him irre- sistibly: "A ease of mistaken identity," he cried, and burst into wild, uncontrollable laughter. Pansy Richardson drew herself up proudly. "What do you mean by your merriment, Mr. Rodley? ;' she said, coldly. But once started off laughing, Robin was unable to control himself. He laughed, and laughed, and laughed, till Pansy 'began to think he had taken leave of his senses. He stopped suddenly. He heard the sound of foot- steps outside, and a voice proclaimipg loudly and indignantly. The next minute the dooz was flung open to admit a tall, dark girl. closely followed by Sir Charles Rodley. The girl stood erect, with flashing eyes, and pointing dramatically at little Miss Richard- son, she almost screamed: "Ask her who she is, and let her dare deny her real identity, here, in my presence." " I am sorry to trouble you by this un- seemly disturhanoe, my dear Miss Richard- son," eaid • Sir Charles, courteously, " but this madwoman here" (the dark eyes flashed dangerously) " says that she is my ward, An- gela Richardson, and that You are an im- postor. Will you kindly set her mind at rest, and then I will not disturb you further." " Yes, -tell us your. name," put in the stranger; excitedly. Miss Richardson 's expression bad grown more and more blank during this conversa- tion. Now she answered quickly. ‘-4 I think -we are all at sixes and sevens. 1 can't understand it. There must be a big mis- take somewhere. My name is Pansy Richard- son, and I thought till ten minutes ago that I was Lady Rodley's companion. But now it seems I am—well, really, I don't know myself what I atn. I only know that I most emphati- cally am not what I have twice been called within the last ten minutes—namely, Angela Richardson. and your ward." You see, she can't deny her identity now she is brought to bay," burst in the other girl. "What is more, '1 believe she knew who you thought she was all along, and has played a deep game; but now she sees that game is up she is playing at injured innocence. Now, will you believe me when I say that 1 am An- gela Richardson, your ward, and- that I claim the shelter and protection of your roof, and an account of the way in which you are managing my money?" She paused a moment to gather breath,--for she had spoken hurriedly and excitedly. Then, giving vent to the full force of her fury, she literally rushed at poor little Pansy. 6* You thief—you impostor!" she shrieked. Cita would hava atruck her supposed rival.

" Well, will you listen to. me like a gentle- man, and I will tell you all I have gone through lately. I arrived at Cockminster Station just a week ago to-day. I had not the least idea what my- guardian was like. I knew notliing, indeed, except that his name was Rodley, and that he lived near a village called Gresham. I was standing at the door of my railway carriage, looking for a porter to lift out my boxes, and wondering if my guardian had come himself to meet me, when an old lady rushed up to me and said: am Mrs. Rodley; .are you Miss Richardson? ' Of course, I answered ' Yes ' to her question, and imagined that my guardian had sent his wife to meet me. We got my luggage to- gether, and the old lady told me she had got her carriage waiting outside the station. Her first remark when we had driven off was: 'Well, I trust we shall get on together, Miss Richardson.' I said: 'I'D also trust so. I am most anxious to see Mr. Rodley.' The old lady threw- up her hands in horror, and asked what girls were coming to nowadays. I made up my mind I should never stand that woman, and a terrible week I have had with her and her old husband. They tried to make me dust the drawing-room ; but I flatly refused. The old woman wanted me to read to her; and, of course, I wouldn't do that. They told me— told me—to drive into Cockzninster and do a lot of shopping. Needless to say, I refused. Well, so things have .gone on till this morn- ing. But at breakfast to-day 1 started giving them a bit of my mind. I told them that they forgot that they were answerable to my trustees for their treatment of me—their ward —and that I would have them to remember that I was no pauper, but an heiress in my own right. Then they both stared at me, and asked me, as you've had the cheek to do, if I was mad. I turned round to the old man and said he couldn't deny I was his ward. He answered that he most emphatically did deny it ; I was his wife's companion. I asked him if he was mad ; and then, after a hot argu- ment, we came to explanations. Suddenly old Mr. Rodley said: 'Good Heavens, my girl, I suppose you can't have mistaken me for Sir Charles Rodley, who 1 be- lieve lives hereabouts. You know, I've only just come here, and he's been settled some two miles off all his life, and his ancestors before him.' I didn't wait for more. I just flew upstairs, put on my hat and jacket, and rushed downstairs and into the carriage which was waiting at the door. I called out to the old man, I'm going to see if your story's true. Tell your coachman to drive me there.' He did as I told him quite meekly— he looked so bewildered. And so I came here, and as you say you have a ward, Angela Richardson, I know you must be my guar- dian. Deny it if you can." She ended in almost as great a fury as she had begun. "I begin to see daylight," said Sir Charles, in a voice' something between laughing and crying.. "This Mr. Rodley has had my ward and I his wife's companion. But I don't want to change." (These last words were said sotto voce.) "Mind yon, I shall demand proofs of your identity." And then and there Miss Angela Richard- son gave the surest proof of her real identity which she could have given—she did just what the letter had said she would, went into violent hysterics. Here Pansy Richardson broke in: "Yes, I see it all at last," she said. "Our names are the same, we were both of us Strangers to the people we were coming to, and so "—her voice broke a little—" we got mixed. Oh, I have been so happy here." Robin was at ber side in an instant, his arm round her waist, his strong hand grip- pinc,° her little tiny one. "My darling," he said, tenderly, "I want you to forgive me if for a. moment I doubted your truth. I saw you many years ago at your father's Vicarage. I remembered your face, and could not understand your being here as my father's ward, Angela Richardson. You don't remember me, darling." " Not until 'this minute; but now I hare the faintest recollection of the gentleman my dear father brought home one day. You are so altered." _

-And your father, how is he?" "He is dead. I have not yet left off my mourning for him." My poor little girl. Your life must have been very sad without him. But now, if you will let me, darling, I will make you happy again. I asked you a question a little while ago. I want your answer." "But—everything is so very different now. You thought me a rich heiress when you asked me to marry you half an hour ago. I am only a poor eompanion—" "A poor companion, yes, but my little love—my future wife."

So there was a wedding from the old Hall, after all, and the bride was as fair and sweet as ever Lady Rodley could have imagined in her wildest dreams. Only the fortune waF lacking. But, after all, money does not make up the whole sum of human happiness. "Kind hearts are more than coronets. When, after a year's tour abroad, Robin brought home his wife the old people gave her the warmest welcome imaginable. They had endured one year of Angela Richardson, and were quite willing to say good-bye to her thowands, seeing that they could also say good-bye to herself. DEAR BACON.

"Prices now ruling for bacOn. in the United Kingdom are the highest on record," says the "Grocer." "During the present year, owing to shortage of supplies, bacon has been uniformly dear, and opinions were freely ex- pressed some months ago that this summer would witness abnormally high prices. No change is looked for from the prevailing con- ditions until the autumn, when quotations generally decline. Sometimes, however, the unexpected happens, and prices may tempor- arily react from those that now obtain. Arrivals of bacon are barely sufficient for requirements. consumption having. been generally -good this year, despite the extreme rates ruling."

SCHOOLBOYS' JOURNEY.

TO CULTIVATE HABITS OF ACCURATE . OBSERVATION. On Saturday morning the seventh annual school journey of St. Giles's Elementary Boys' School, Reading, was begun, and will last about a fortnight. Twenty boys, conducted by the head master, met at the schools, and went by train to Basingstoke, and thence on foot to North Waltham. They will traverse Hamp- shire by way of Winchester, Fareham, Ports- down, and Havant, and thence into Sussex, through Winchester and Selsea Bill. The re- turn journey will be due North through Mid- hurst, Farnham, and Sandhurst. One great object of these school journeys is to develop and cultivate habits of accurate observation, and the towns and country the boys pass through are carefully observed for historical, archEeological, architectural, botanical, and geological study. In previous journeys parts of Berkshire, Ox- fordshire, Buckinghamshire, Wiltshire, and Gloucestershire have been explored. This, year the boys will be accompanied at different points of the journey by Dr. Price, school medical offi- cer; the Rev. P. de IN.f. Nixon, diocesan inspec- tor. and the Per. J. C .Gilmor, vicar of St.

WOMEN'S QUESTIONS.

Colours and Shades to Suit Seaside Complexions.

INTELLECT AND BALDNESS.

Women are taking a conspicuous part in this year's meeting of the British Associa- tion, which will be held at Birmingham from September 10th to 17th. They are not allowed to practise as solicitors or barristers, for one reason, because they would compete with men in a lucrative profession, but this objection does not apply to the study of science, and several lady scientists are numbered among the contributors , of papers and scheduled speakers at the British Association meet- ing. A subject one would like to see scientifically dealt with, and is well worthy the attention of the British Asso- ciation, very nearly concerns the fair sex, and that is the theory that as women pro- gress intellectually, so they will develop a tendency to bald-headedness. If women will persist in becoming more " brainy," they will endanger their tresses. That, at least, is the opinion of some eminent biologists, and if you point out to them that there is no evidence of this result among University women, they reply that it will be evident enough in future generations. AN EXPLANATION NEEDED.

There is no arguing with a prophet, but women will hardly be deterred from becoming the equals of men in intellectual attainments by the fear of becoming bald. For one thing, if the worst came to the worst, there is always the hairdresser to go to, and it is by no means certain that there is any connection between intellec- tual distinction and bald-headedness. Eminent poexs, like eminent pianists,almost invariably have a profusion of hair and it is of no use being a poet unless you have brains. It is true that a lack, or abnormal growth of hair, characterises certain pro- fessional callings, but scholarly attain- ments do not appear to have much to do with it. It is a-well-known fact that among military bandsmen there is a much higher Percentage of bald heads among those who blow into brass or wood, than among the stringed instrumentalists. A bald-headed violinist is almost as rare as a bald-headed artist or butcher. There has never been any satisfactory diagnosis of these various idiosyncrasies, and it would be interesting to have a scientific explanation of the mysteries that attach to hair-growth and baldness. NO WOMAN R.A .

There is another profession besides that of the law in which women are excluded from the benefit they would derive from membership in its leading societies—viz. in Art. The Royal Academy and the Royal Scottish Academy both close their doors to women artists—as far as membership is concerned—notwithstanding that their work in several instances is quite equal to that of members of either of these societies. The value of the magic letters R.A. or R.S.A. can scarcely be overestimated from a financial point of view. It means good places in all exhibitions, and consequently good notices in the Press. Furthermore, it ensures good sales, at good prices, and a reputation at home, abroad, and in the Colonies, from whence come invitations to the R.A. to exhibit, which scarcely ever reach the outsider. FOR THE SEASIDE.

In choosing gowns and suits for seaside wear, it is well to remember that there is a great difference in the light there and in London and the large industrial centres, and in its effect on delicately coloured clothes. The complexion when tanned by sea and wind will not be set off by a very pale pink or blue, that might be quite becoming to an ivory tinted skin: therefore for holiday wear deeper shades are to be preferred to the more delicate ones. The coat and frock idea lends itself admirably to holiday out- fits, because it admits of a great deal of change with a few garments, but each item should be carefully considered beforehand and a settled scheme of colour decided upon —and this must be carried out in the selection of shoes, hose and gloves. White and navy blue, for instance, may be interchanged, and a sunburnt straw hat made to do duty for more than two suits, if three different velvet bands, that harmonise or contrast with the different toilettes, be provided. The bands are simple affairs to remove and to put on, and take up but little space. A red or very scarlet linen suit may be varied with a white linen, or a box-pleated skirt of white crepon. The box pleat is perhaps newer than the accordion. When, how- ever, the former is very small, and put in closely together, the difference between the two is scarcely perceptible. A coat of brightly coloured ratine, cut in the djibbah style, and trimmed with Bulgarian embroideries in vivid colours, is a very useful garment to wear at the seaside with cotton frocks. THE FLOUNCED SKIRT.

The flounced skirt is a fact, but a t first sight it looks like a double • tunic, for the two flounces begin at the waist, and go no further than the knee. This gives abroad effect to the 'hips, and will certainly not be welcomed by the woman whose ample pro- portions are a source of anxiety aril dis- quietude. As narrowness at the feet is still an essential feature of all skirts, the basis of the flounces is of a clinging material, and there is no addition of fulness at the hem or above it. The flounces also are of some supple and soft fabric, and fall fairly full. On a tall figure they have a Continued in next column.

WITH THE WOODS.

Works Competition Semi-Final.

LIBERAL CLUB MAIN.

Mr. S. W. Jeffery Wins the " Crosfield ' Cup. Saturday last was a glorious day for local bowlers and, although there was very little done in the way of competition bowling, the various greens were crowded with enthusiasts.

The " Crosfield " Cup, given in connection with the Liberal Club main, was won by Mr. S. W. Jeffery, out of 64 competitors. Mr. Clay, the genial manager, had got the green to a splendid pitch of perfection and the running was all that could be desired. Mr. Jeffery played a skilful game and was at home on almost any length. In the final he defeated J. Gibson, who had also played most creditably during the afternoon.

In connection with the Liberal Club annual handicap, a number of games will be played to-day, the list of which was published last week. The second round will be concluded and the tbird round entered upon. The Bank Quay Green is looking well and borne good bowling ,should be witnessed to-day.

The semi-final games in the Warrington Works Association Competition were full of interest, the bowling being of the keenest description. We expresst d the opinion early in the season that the County Asylum men would not be far from the final, and on Saturday they failed to survive the penultimate stage by only one point, the St. Helens Cable No. 1 team being the victors.

It was a splendid match. The first seven men on the Asylum side recorded wins and the Cable bowlers were feeling somewhat downhearted. flowever, they were not many points behind. The last five men played exceedingly hard and at the conclusion they were declared the winners by the odd chalk.

It was only by 12 points that Rylands' No. 1. triumphed over R. A. Naylor's. Some good wins were recorded for both sides. The uireworkers were the most skilful team, but they had by no means an easy thing on. It will be a keen struggle in the final, but Rylands are expected to push about to "pull it cff. '

The annual main in connection with the associa- tion will open on August 16th. The entries, which closed last Saturday, are exceedingly numerous.

The Order of Druids had a greai time at the Raven Inn, Glazebury, on Saturdr.y. A bowling main was won by W. Thomason, after many exciting games had been decided. One of the best games of the afternoon was that in which J. Lloyd beat lai.t year's winner, W. Gould. They met in the fourth round. end it was a ding-dong game to the finish, at the 19th end. The winner, W. Thomason, is to be congratulated on being in the final two years in succession. He played excellent bowls all through the main, and his efforts were duly rewarded.

At the time of writing, the prospects are all in favour of a glorious " bowlers' day " to-day, and there is little doubt that all the local greens will be extensively patronised.

The final for the Warringtod and District Bowling competition between Senior Conservatives and Cemetery will take place to-day. The first match will take place on the Conservative Green commencing at 3.15, and the return match a ill probably be played next Saturday. The Conserva- tive team will be selected from the following : Messrs. H. Hughes (capt.), A. Broady, J. Holland, W. Thomason, W. Millington, W. Barnes, A. Scott, H. P. Turner, T. Horrabin, G. Richardson, J. Appleton, J. H. Gitten, F. Heesom ani R. F. Anderton. "THUMB BIAS."

THE COUNTY CHAMPIONSHIP. Victory of a Wigan Player. The Lancashire Individual Merit Championship was concluded on Saturday, when those competitors who bad qualified in the four f•ections of Liverpool, Manchester, Blackpool, and Southport, met at the Old Trafford Hotel, Manchester, to contest for the title of Lancashire amateur champion and the four prizes offered in addition. The weather was perfect and a large crowd witnessed bowling of a high standard. The winner—J. Hart, of Wigan Sub- soripton, a member of the famous Hart family of bowlers, and brother of the well-known Gerard Hart —played consistently well all through, and on it long length gave W. Clewarth (Swinton Liberal) little chance in the final, winning 21-9. All the Warrington bowlers were dismissed in the first roune. Of the four prizes, Wigan Subscription obtained two—first (J. Hart) and third (P. Flynn) ; Swinton Liberals second (J. Clewarth), and Southport fourth (W. Forshaw). Liverpool, by means of J. W. Ward, Royal Excelsior, obtained one place in the last eight, and be, with J. Grundy (Leigh Railway), F. Gardner (Roebuck), A. Halliwell Blackpool), and the four prize-winners, have earned the right to represent Lancashire in the British Crown Green Individual Merit Championship, which will be played at the Griffin Hotel, Bowdon- Cheshire, on Augnst Bank Holiday.

ANGLING.

Warrington Workingmen's Angling

Association.

(N.A. CENTRE.)

Specimen fishing I.as bf on out of the question for the past week or so, the contest having taken premier place. However, one or two fair specimens have been registered. On Sunday Mr. M. Manion was preparing for the contest when a fine perch came along and took his bait. After a few minutes of fine angling, the fish was brought to the net and landed succfssfully. Its weight was 11b. 10oz. 7dr.—not at all bad angling on match tackle. The Bay Horse club di4ributed their prizes for the Shield match on Thursday, July 24th. Mr. H. Hayes (president) handed the winner (Hr. F. Jolley) the beautiful shield and gold medal, after a few remarks on the progress of the club. A "smoker " was afterwards enjoyed. 0 PEN ING MATCH.

As we anticipated in our previous notes, the entry was a record as far as Northern anglers in War- rington are concerned. The total number fishing was 144. The customer's match was fished after the big contest. the successful competitors being: Gladstone Arms, Mr. J. Smith ; Bay Horse, Mr. Millington. The journey was made by taxi, char- a-banc and bus, everyone arriving at the river in time for the draw. Messrs. Stout and Putter got the men to business without delay. It was not long before the bank rangers were busy booking up fish, and it was soon noted that it was not a day for the novice, as the weights below go to show. ODDFELLOWS' HANDICAP.

The annual main in connection with the War- rington District of the Independent Order of Oddfellows (Manchester Unity) was played- on Saturday on the Bowling Green Hotel, Liverpool- rohd. A start was made just before 2.30, and a large number of members and friends were present. The green was in splendid condition and some very good bowling was witnessed, the Lymm brethren particularly showing good form which is evidenced by the fact that three out of the four players in the senfi- final and final belonged to the "Trafford " Lodge, Lymm. There as plenty of excitement both In the semi-final and final games, and particularly in the latter, although it was wtting dusk before the game was finished, but Bro. Arthur Clare of the Trdfford " Lodge managed to get the better of Bro. Mac Wilson, ids() of the " Trafford" Lcdge by 4 points, the result being 13 9. The final was concluded at 915 p.rn. Bro. Arthur Clare will therefore hold the Challenge Cup for the next 12 months. The prizes, together with the cup, will be presented at a later date, due notice of which will be forwarded to the various Lodges by the Hon. Secre- tary of the main, Mr. Wm. E. Clare. ===== 0002601_19130802_art0255.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ISLE OF MAN.

:BY SILVER-STREET.]

One of the less-felt advantages of the bicycle, but one that is no less solid, is that it takes its rider into so many new places whore the crush of the tripper is nct known, where the breeze is pure, where the smell of Woodbines is unknown, and the only nicotinian scent is from your own brier, where the only chips and fish are those your own line brings ashore, or those broken off the trees by the last pelting storm. For the cyclist is a glorified walker, and they et,j y it most who in their early days tramped their 15 or 20 miles on a Satur- day afternoon, and could take any footpath the farmers would permit them to use. The Isle of Man is pre-eminently the cyclist's and the walker's paradise, and although 1 apologised last week for writing on all kinds of topics except C) cling, I am going to write chiefly about walking again. By all means take the cycle, for of all places it is there that you will find it pay. The railways are so scarce, mad there are so few cheap fares, and the times are so long between one train and another that both in time and money your cycle tickets will be bayed three times over in a week.

And then again, so many of the interesting places can only be visited on a char-a-banc or other conveyance, while the mo3t charming of all are quite inaccessible except by private motor or carriage, or, by the cycle. Of these last we may mention Jurby, Andreas, and Bride, in the north ; Injebreck, Baldwin, and Sulby Glen, Foxdale, Silvei burn, Dalby, Glen Maye, and the Niarbyl in the centre ; and Fleshwick Bay, the Chasms, Sugar Loaf Rock, and Calf Sound in the south of the island.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0256.txt | included=true | included =====
A MOUNTAIN CLIMB

One round that I and my friends trok, ladies included, was by train from Peel to Kirkmichael. There we walked to the bottom of the village and turned up a rough lane towards blien Corn, and Slien Dhoo, our object being, of course, to cross the mountain range, and drop down into Snlby Glen by Druidale, far higher up the valley than visitors usually see. On the way up Lhergy-vreck we came across a patch of moor almost covered with the belutiful stags horns moss, and gathered a few sprays to bring home with us. The heat was terrible until we reached the top, and then a glorious breeze from the north met us, and also the view over the great level plain to the north. The fields were spread like a map, and of the same varied colour as a patchwork quilt, and far beyond the great blue sea were the hazy Mourne Mountains of County Down,and the long finger ends of the south of Scotland, ths Mull of Galloway and Burrow Head. On the other side we could see the little electric car climbing up the slant of the Snaefell, while another was feeling its way gingerly down that elope of 1 in 12.

At the top only sheep paths were available, and then came the descent into a valley of solitude, by no path at all, where the nearest farmhouse was a mile from us, and another from its next door neighbour. A cart-track led down to Druidale Farm, and then the whole beauty of the valley lay before us right down to the Sulby Glen station. We had tea at Tbolt y Will Hotel, the entrance to the Sulby Glen pleasure grounds, and one of the most beauti- ful hotels in the whole island, as well as reasonable. Eve!y visitor knows the fine scenery of the Sulby Valley, but few know that a far more beautiful gorge is to be enjoyed by going higher up than the hotel, as far as Druidale Farm, keeping to the foot- path on the south bank of the stream.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0257.txt | included=true | included =====
A.FINE WALK ALONG THE CLIFF.

Ancther fine walk from Peel is to Glen Mayo along the cliffs. It needs some nerve to thread one's way through gorse and bramble and long rank grass on the very edge of a 200 feet cliff, but it is well worth the few scratches to have the experience, and the beauties of Glen Maye are all right. The payment may he made on coming up out of the glen, for it is well worth the charge.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0258.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OLD CHOCK.

Somewhere in the neighbourhood of Swinyard Hall there is still in existence au old tricycle. It is composed as follow: the seat is in the middle ; on the left hand is an enormous geared driving wheel, and on the other side two little wheels one behind the other. All of them have solid tyres, but the rubber is not now continuous, gaps of three or four inches occasionally occur. How long is it since such an animal was generally found on the road?

===== 0002601_19130802_art0259.txt | included=true | included =====
CHESHIRE REST BOUSES.-G.

Mrs. Bragg's, Bollington. I am glad to say that this good old lady has, to a great extent, recovered from the operation to her eyes undergone some months ago, and there is no fear yet of her house being closed to cyclists and visitors. Of course,the great attraction of Bollington is Dunham Park, the magnificent estate of the Earl of Warrington and Stamford. A funny notice is found at all the entrances, to the effect that the following pernicious animals are not admitted —dogs, perambulators, and photographers ! You may take a baby in, but not its bassinette ; you may take your camera, but not use it. The last time I saw his lordship, he was flying a toy aeroplane on the lawn. He is most likely at home now, as the school broke up this week end. Let us hope that when the paint has worn off the present sign boards. that precious sign will be allowed to quietly fall into oblivion.

ATHLETICS.

Notable Series of Local Successes.

On Saturday at Rainford Cricket Club spot Is, W. Bolton, A. Sideall, and H. Holbrook were close together throughout the two miles team race, and, although no special taming is done tor this type of event, Warrington Athletic Club, as usual, werel amongst the prize, another lot of firsts bringingi this season's record to: - Five races, n firsts and 2 thirds. The local lads must be either better in class or usually in fitter condition than the opposi- tion, because these races are scratch not handi- cap affairs. In the handicaps Warrington is always well represented by sound effort, but well as Stauley Siddall ran in the mile and half mile, he found getting placed in such fast times, 4coin. 15secs. for the mile and lmin. 54secs. for the half, required more than comfortable effort. He was close up in both. J. W. Watson moved well in the mile and showed improvement. In the 100 yards W. J. Llewellyn and W. Siddall won their heats, but got no further. At 220 yards, J. E. Cartwright and W. Siddall had to do some running to win their beats, and the latter had only the third prize to repay a great effort to concede 21 yards to the sprint winner, vtho did a half yard worse than 9 4-5 and 22 seconds respectively, to win his double event. Charlie Berry won both cycle reces in a style which surprised many of his friends, lie always was a stylist, and his opponents suffered by com- parison. At Maltby, Sidney Grounds lost the 100 yards final by inches, after being penalised a yard for anticipating the start. He, however, made amends by winning both the 220 and 440 yards, so two firsts and a third made quite a good afternoon's work for thislpromising athlete.

SOLITARY CONFINEME.N. T IN PORTUGAL

Entombed in a vim castle on the outskirts of Lisbon are sonic of the most miserable men on earth. These are inmates of Portugal's. CHILDREN'S EYES.

Are They Damaged by the Books Used in School ?

INTERESTING LOCAL DISCOVERY.

The question of the growing defective eye- sight of children, attending elementary school,s, has become a matter of grave public import- ance. Just what the cause is due to has re- mained more or less a mystery, and experts upon the subject have been groping about to find the real source of the trouble, increasing at an almost alarming rate. Dr. Cameron Gibson, the medical inspector of school children for Mid-Cheshire, has made a discovery which. may solve the problem of defective eyesight in school pupils. He thinks the cause has to be sought for in the school books, and says that the type used for these is injurious to the youthful eyes. "Inspection of the reading books," says Dr. Gibson, "especially those used in the infant classes and Standard 1., shows a considerable variation in the size and clearness of the type used. Cohn, a pioneer investigator into the relation between school conditions and eyesight, affirmed that the type in school books should never be less than that with an n' I.smm. in height, and an interlineage of 2.5 mm.; later investigators, while allowing this or an ap- proach to it in the higher classes, demand larger type in the infant department—a mini- mum of 2.6 mm. for type and 4.5 mm. interline- age in the first year of reading. The majority of the books examined are over this minimum standard, but some are under it, especially in poetry or in the exercises which begin or end a chapter." The doctor examined a large number of books and found them' defective. He argues that the proper school books are those which have clear type and printed upon good, but not glossy paper.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0274.txt | included=true | included =====
DEAF SCHOOL CHILDREN.

In dealing with the ear diseases found amongst Mid-Cheshire children, Dr. 'Gibson says that in his district discharging ear was responsible for most of the cases. "It is difficult," he says. "to convince par- ents of the seriousness of this condition or the importance of having it cured; they frequently allege as a reason for non-interference that the child becomes ill if ever the ear stops running, and sometimes back this up with the state- ment that 'the doctor said. I had to keep it running.' Possibly what the doctor said had been misunderstood, but it: is regrettable that, in such a grave condition, medical reasons can be brought forward for non-treatment. "There were 154 children found somewhat deaf in one or both ears, while 38 children were very deaf. Deafness, even comparatively slight. interferes much with the child's educational progress. In the vast majority of cases the deafness is due, directly or indirectly, to the presence of large adenoid growths."

===== 0002601_19130802_art0275.txt | included=true | included =====
BAD TEETH.

Dr. Gibson found that out of 5,123 children examined. 3,821 had caries of three orttmore, teeth, and commenting upon this hti says : "The condition of caries is. however, so general, that the treatment should be univer- sal. The line at present offering most hope of success seems to be the stimulating of interest in the state of the children's teeth on the part of the children themselves, their parents. and others that have to deal with them, and one of the merits of the daily use of the tooth brush is that it directs the child's attention +o his teeth, and accustoms him to the expenditure of some trouble on their preservation. and so paves the way for any alteration in diet that may be necessary, or submission to the I..inds of the dentist. There can be no doubt that year by year an increasing amount of attention is being paid to the children's teeth, in the direc- tion of a fairly regular use of the tooth brush. "I am glad to record that in one instance a school manager has volunteered to supply tooth brushes to all the children, and I hope after my next visit to that schoill to he able to report evidence of the regular and universal use of the tooth brush."

===== 0002601_19130802_art0277.txt | included=true | included =====
"The Sneering Indifference of Respectability."

On Sunday evening, the Rev. W. Reid, preach- ing at St. John's Presbyterian Church, from the metaphor used by the prophet Zephaniah, of wine left to rest upon its sediment until it lost its strength and keenness, becoming dull and feeble, spoke of the danger of flagging in- terest and zeal in the cause of truth and holi- ness of life, and the pulling down of God to the level of the creature. The judgment Of God was preached by Zephaniah less upon disorder- liness and lawlessness than upon the sneering indifference of respectability, which did more to retard reform, crush out progress, poison life and. defeat the cause of God and man, than any amount of opposition. The Cross was claimed as the only remedy against this fatal disease, and the only assurance of victory.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0278.txt | included=true | included =====

Experiment Which is Appreciated at Stockton Ledge.

"BUMBLE ART" OF COOKERY.

Pupils of Miss Partington, Stockton Lodge, Stockton Heath, assembled in the Hall Cafe, on Wednesday afternoon, for the annual prize dis- tribution. A large lumber of _ parents and friends also attended, and after 'Mrs. Lyon, of Appleton Hall, had presented the prizes, an excellent programme of music was gone through.

--- At the outset, Miss 'PARTINGTON announced the unavoidable absence of their 'visiting music master, Mr. Darlington. In addition to the Oxford Local Examination results, there had been 13 successes in musical examinations— four distinctions anti:one honours—and 20 for the Royal Drawing Society's examinations, 16 with honours; Miss Doris Gandy had also ob- tained the L.T.C.L. for practical teaching and theory. They had to congratulate Miss Maggie Allen., an old pupil. of the school, .on obtaining the A.T.C.L. She had been prepared by Mr. Darlington. (Applause.) She regretted to inform them a the change in the staff -far next term, but she was sure they would join -with her in congratulating Miss Wigg on her approaching marriage. (Applause.) She would he ably fol- lowed by Miss Smyth, 8.A., who had had seven years' experience in private schools.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0286.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST ‘2, 1913. AIRSHIP CHALLENGE.

Lively Commons' Debate and a Frustrated Attempt at a "Snap " Defeat.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0287.txt | included=true | included =====
COLONEL SEELY PROVES HIS CASE.

Setting themselves forth to create a scare upon the army airship question, the Tory group, who pose as national benefactors—in the matter of aircraft 4nd everything else—were the means of occasioning a lively debate upon their pet theme in the House of Commons on Wednesday evening. These fat boys, in a Pickwickian sense, failed, however, to make the flesh of the British public creep. Colonel Seely, the Secretary , of War, effectively answered his critics. The tit-bits of the debate consisted of Mr. Bonar Law challenging Colonel Seely with misrepresentation, a comment which brought the censure of the Committee chairman upon the head of the Tory leader. Mr. Bonar Law'apologised. The Unionists had arranged for a snap division, which was reminiscent of that which brought about the fall of the Liberal Government over the Cordite question in 1895, but the Government's supporters rallied splendidly and the position was saved by 33 votes. The debate was the outcome of the dis- cussions on the Army Estimates, on June sth. Then Mr. Joynson-Hicks, M. P., raised the question of the number of dirigibles and aeroplanes at present possessed by the Army. He said that if Colonel Seely could show him 80 machines properly able to fly he would make the right hon. gentleman the most profound apologies. The War Secretary promptly took up the challenge. " I will arrange for him to start 10-morrow," he said. The Member for Brentford was accom- panied on his tour by Captain Sandy (C, Wells), who opened the subject in the debate on Wednesday night. He said that he and Mr.Joynson-Hicks visited the branches of the Army Aviation Department at Farnborough and Salisbury Plain. They were obliged to conduct the investi- gation without expert advice, because the Secretary for War refused to allow Mr. Du Cros, M.P., to go with them. (Opposition cheers.) They saw only three machines flying, but they asked the officers if the machines in the sheds could be flown, and accepted their word, and that was a concession to the Secretary for War. UNJUST REFLECTION:

'Colonel Seely said that was a reflection on the officers. (Ministerial cheers.) Mr. Sandys said he meant no reflection on the officers. They saw 24 monoplanes, but did not count them in, because they had not been flown for some months. At the Flying School they saw 18 machines ready to fly and at Lark-hill eight ready to fly. At Farnborough they found 12 such machines. Taking into account four machines at Montrose, which they did not see, and two Bleriot monoplanes, the total came to 44, or 51 if they included seven which were under test. (Opposition cheers.) _ _

--Yet Colonel Seely on June 5 stated that they had Mlf machines in first-class order. (Opposition cheers.) That was a very unsatisfactory state of affairs, and he would like to know how the right hon. gentleman reconciled his statement with the facts of the case. He had included machines which were under repair and damaged, and that was misleading the House and the country. (Opposition cheers.) in reply, Colonel Seely said that the c ontroversy would be interesting if the hon. gentleman could suggest that he had endeavoured to conceal the true positiOn as to the number of aeroplanes from the House. Mr. Sandys was the first to admit the exact contrary. He (Colonel Seely) had invited Mr. Sandys and Mr. Joynson- Hicks to see all that there was to be seen without detriment to the public interests, and they had been given access to all the official doe uments. THE TORY OBJECTIVE.

' It was quite clear from what the , hon. Member had already said that his (the speaker's) sole object was to displace the illusion that we had no aeroplanes to spe.tk of. His instructions to General Henderson were that everything was to go on just the same,that no aeroplanes were to be stopped in their work on account of the visit, and that every facility was to be given to inspect documents except those of a secret character. _

All this had been done, and now they had the hon. Member coming down to the House and trying to make them believe, for a moment, that he (Colonel Seely) had been trying to deceive the House when all the time he had had the advantage of all the information which could possibly be given to him. He would give to the House, the full number of aeroplanes which we had in our possession, and would continue to do so so long as the House insisted upon it; but he did suggest that it would be wise to call a halt in making public the actual number of aeroplanes which we might have in the future—(Ministerial cheers)—and for this reason, that no other nation did it. Other nations took great care to disclose nothing. Since the Army Flying Corps was started on May 13 last year we had bought and paid for 130 aeroplanes, the best that could be bought, and of these a small number had been damaged past repair. Many of them had been reconstructed from time to time. Aeroplanes were totally different frOm guns in that they had to be repaired more frequently, and the engines had to be dis- mantled after so may hours, and recon- structed. Then, in order to ensure safety the stays and struts had to be tested from time to time.

===== 0002601_19130802_art0288.txt | included=true | included =====
A REMARKABLE RECORD.

All this went on every day, and if the hon. Member did not' see that all the aeroplanes fly did heiuggest that they did not and could not fly? In all weathers, ahnost every day, thP Army flying men were flying all over the country. Over 135,000 miles had been flown by the military flying men without serious accident of any kind. That was a very remarkable record. All aeroplanes necessarily had to be constantly over- hauled. Mr. Sandys had stated that he was deceived by his officers.

Captain Sandys : I did not say that. Colonel Seely, continuing, said he did not care what was said against himself, but he strongly resented a statement that his officers 'were giving him wrong informa- tion. (Ministerial cheers.) If he had not more aeroplanes it was not because he had .not the money; it was because he could not get them delivered. But he was glad to be able to tell the Com- mittee that that time was now drawing to an end in the country, and he felt sure that in a very short time they would be able to get all the aeroplanes they required. There were now 191 officers and men in the Royal Flying Corps. Of these 82 had passed the highest tests, and 25 others would pass within the next fortnight. The Army was making a substantial use of its flying men, and he could promise the House that no effort would be spared to

continue to accelerate an aviation pro- gramme.

MR. .LAW APOLOGISES'

Mr. Bonar Law said he had always had the opinion of the Secretary for War that he intended to be fair, but he had never heard a case of more deliberate misrebre- sentation of what was said by a member of the House than Colonel Seely's respect- ing Mr. Sandys. Colonel Seely: On a point of order, Mr. Chairman, is it in order to say I deliber- ately misrepresented him ? Mr. Whitley (chairman of Committee) : 1 do not thinkwe should accuse one another of intentional misrepresentation. (Ministerial cheers.) Mr. Bonar Law: I bow at once and apologise. The right hon, gentleman did , unintentionally most completely misrepre- sent what was said by Mr. Sandys. He accused him of saying that lie doubted the word of officers. That was not correct. Continuing he said that, while they did not accuse Colonel Seely of an intention to deceive, the statement- of Mr. ' Sandys proved that the right hon. gentleman was rash in the extreme in the statements he made in the House, and it proved further that if in a case of this kind they could not accept a deliberate statement made in that way, he could not expect them to accept assurances made by him in regard to other matters in regard to which it was impos- sible to apply tests. (Opposition cheers.)

HOLIDAY BENEFITS.

Warrington Insurance Committee's Plan For Medical Treatment. DOCTORS' PROTEST. Now that the holiday season is approach- its zenith, attention is being directed to a phase of the National Insurance scheme which has a special interest for those workers who contemplate a sojourn at seaside or rural haunts. The majority of the holiday-makers.leave home. delighted with the prospect of joyous and health-giving days, without a thought of medical treatment or doctor's bills. There are others, less fortunate, who cannot so lightly throw serious considerations to the winds. Their state of health is so uncertain or indifferent that it becomes imperative for them to ensure that medical aid shall be within their reach, should occasion arise. They have paid their con- tributions under the Insurance Act and the question naturally arises: How are they to procure for themselves that free attendance and medicine, to which they are entitled, during absence from home'? The Warrington Insurance Committee, like most other authorities of the kind, has adopted the recommendation of the Insur- ance Commissioners whereby what at first sight appears a nice problem becomes a comparatively simple matter. Any insured person in Warrington, who anticipates that he will require the services of a doctor while away on ,'holiday, 'can, on applica- tirn to the Clerk t'o the Insurance Com- mittee (Mr. Alfred Tilling), obtain a special voucher, entitling him to go to any practitioner on the panel of the town which he intends to visit 'and receive free treat- ment and free medicine. Mr. Tilling informed an " Examiner " representative that a number of vouchers had already been issued, chiefly to con- valescents, and ,the necessary attention had been obtained by the holders. , Although the medical men in many towns object to •the arrangement, no single instance has yet eome to the knowledge of the War- rington Insurance 'Committee in which a holiday voucher issued by them has not 'been duly honoured. The method by whic'h the doctors are to be remunerated for .the exceptional work ,of the holiday season has given rise to some corrtroversy—in fact a large number of doctors are up in arms against the arrangement of the Insur- ance amunissioners. When an insured person applies for a voucher, he has to state the period over which his absence will extend, and a proportion of the •annual grant due to his medical attendant is deducted and placed in a central fund in London. The sums thus accumulated will be distributed among the doctors who actually attend patients from other towns during the holiday season, according to the extent of the services rendered.

WARRINGTON DOCTORS' PROTEST. The doctors of Warrington have passed a resolution protesting against the scheme of the Insurance Commissioners, and this, implies that they will decline to recognise the vouchers presented by insured persons from other towns. , 'Dr. Murray, of Penketb, the secretary of the Warrington Medical Committee, in con- versation with an " Examiner " represen- tative,said the only authorative statement he could make .was that the local doctors 'had objected to the scheme of the Com- missioners with regard to the deduction for holiday patients. It no doubt followed that they would refuse to recognise the special voucher, but they had not passed a resolution to that effect.

Asked why the doctors objected to the arrangement, Dr. Murray replied: "There are many reasons, but the principal one is that we don't think it will work properly. It is all imaginary and indefinite. The deductions and remuneration have to be worked out on the ground of the sickness there happens to be in the different areas. Consequently, if one area has a larger amount of sickness than another it will suffer more. The Insurance Commissioners have issued pamphlets explaining the scheme, but it is still very complicated. We want something simpler—that is the long and short of it. We have signed agree- ments to be paid so much per individual per annum, but if this scheme goes through we shall not get that amount. It will probably work itself out satisfactorily in time, but we have given notice• to thelnsurance Com- mittee objecting to the scheme."

MR. LLOYD GEORGE AND HIS

CRITICS.

Mr. Lloyd George, after a self-restraining Silence amidst much ..provocation, effectively answered his many Tory traducers in the great speech he made to an audience of 10,000 at Car- narvon on Thursday. "Many have regarded me," he said, "as per- nicious and mischievous; many felt angry that through no fault of .my own, but through the exigencies of my office, I have been the means of increasing their burden of taxation. It was no fault of mine, yet those people, particularly the Lords, cannot talk without a snarl at the person they imagine .responsible for the catas- trophe which .has overtaken them. " How can you expect them not to exaggerate language and to encourage and foment any calumny that affords the slightest hope of get- ting rid of. such a noxious personage?" He (Mr. George) acknowledged with pleasure the fact that the most potent Tory newspapers never degraded their :great traditions by join- ing the campaign of hysterical and hypocritical scurrility. 'However, the vitriol throwers had failed to kill, to maim, or-to deface him. •

THE DIVISION.

On the division (for the reduction of the Vote), the Government had a majority of 33-280 t 0.247. The announcement of the numbers was received with loud and prolonged cheers by the Opposition. The division was a well-calculated "snap." The attendance of Tories was very large. There were 25 pairs, leaving only about nine members of the Opposition unaccounted for. Sixty-eight Nationalists voted with the Government.

PUBLIC AI6IUSEMENTS.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

'A White Slave" the Holiday Attraction.

For the re-opening of he Royal Court Theatre on Monday next, the management have en- gaged Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Millane's powerful company of players in the great play on the burning question of the moment, the white slave traffic entitled "A White 'Slave." Miss Mabel Rose will appear in her original char- acter of Keerie, and Mr. John Milward will sup- port this charming and talented artiste. There will be a matinee on Monday. The white slave traffic problem has become such a burning question of late, that this play was specially written round the theme and teems with inci- dents prolific of dramatic surprise and apt dia- logue. Mr. Millane, by the kind permission of the Bishop of Manchester (Dr., Knox) has pub- lished a pamphlet with the Bishop's warning and advice to girls when outdoors. The Criminal Law Amendment Act, which will strike the death-blow at the infamous traffic it was designed to Suppress, will be hailed with the highest satisfaction.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Patrons of the Hippodrome have been exceed- ingly well pleased with the fare provided this week. "1,000 to 1 Against," which is presented by. Messrs. H. J. Corner and Lee. Ward's cora- pany, is one of the smallest sketches we have seen on the music hall stage. Dealing as it does with an exciting , adventure in the life of Dick Rutherford, the gentleman cracksman, there is plenty to satisfy those who delight in witnessing a really breezy sketch, which bristles with sensational incidents. An excellent com- pany make the most of their opportunities. Mr. Wilfred Payne proves himself a very clever actor in the role of Dick Rutherford, and in- vests the part with the necessary dash, vigour and cunning. Lawley and East are an excel- lent entertaining couple in a brisk musical comedietta, "A Chance Acquaintance.", The gentleman has a good round. voice, which is heard to advantage in popular numbers. The Berg Bros. are as entertaining as ever as trick and comedy cyclists, some of their tricks sur- passing anything hitherto seen, for sheer dar- ing. Mike Gray. is an eccentric comedy juggler, with many by-play gags, and scores a huge success. His dog " Dooley " is instrumental in adding to the popularity of the turn. Dan Whitley is as humorous as ever and greatly pleased, with his " Little Box of Ointment" number. The three Leonas give an acceptable equilibristic acrobatic act, and Lilian 'Carter uses a beautiful soprano voice to advantage. A very humorous picture, "Everybody's doin' it," is shown on the cinema.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. The holiday programme at the Hippodrome next week is of more than usual interest. Harry Maypen and George Pickett will present "The Big Joss," a Chinese episode of the gold rush of '49, by Francis Annesley. Other turns will be: Ernest Scharff and Assistant. instru- mentalists; Arthur Woodville, versatile come- dian; Samaroff and Miss Sonia, Russian Peasants; Colbert, juggler and hoop roller; Du Gros Trio, comedy gymnasts; Cora Sisters, in latest success, and Jack Roscoe, comedian.

THEATRE ROYAL.

Large audiences have assembled nightly at the .Theatre Royal, Scotlandsroad, where the programme of pictures submitted has been a most excellent one. " 1812 " is a striking pro- duction. -dealing with the great Napoleon's downfall, the chief incidents depicted being attendant upon the retreat from Moscow. This film, along with other interesting productions, will be shown to-night. -For next week the chief pictures for Monday, Tuesday and Wednesday will ,be "The Tum- bler's Wife" and "Famous Secret to the rescue." For Thursday, Friday and Saturday premier place is given to " Esther " : a story from. the Bible, and- "Nick Winter's Smart Methods."

THE PICTUREDROME. During the whole of the next week "Atop of the World in Motion." will be shown at every performance.

Take a pint packet of BIRD'S Crystal Jelly Powder (Lemon Flavor); 1 Sponge Cake ; 1 Stewed Apple: a few glaod or crystalised Cherries. Make the Jelly according to the directions; cut cherries in half and decorate the bottom of Nmould with them; pour in a little of the jelly just covering the cherries and allow to set. ?her: cut the sponge cake in four slices and spread with the stewe 1 apple. When the jelly in the mould is set, add to it carefullY the rest of the jelly which must be nearly cold. Place the sPeng° cake in the middle, and when the whole is set firm tura ou' in the ordinary way. SPORTS and PASTIMES.

Interesting Main at the Liberal Club. The" Crosfield "Cup is a much-coveted trophy amongst the bowling members of the Liberal Club, and, in consequence, there are always a large number of entries for the annual b:rwling main. This year's competition took place on the Liberal Club green en Saturday, and was favoured with glorious weather. The green was in excellent condition end acme capital bowling was witness€d There were nearly 70 entries and a large number of spectators assembled. After many interesting games had been played, S. W. Jeffery, who had bowled exceedingly well all through, beat J. Gibson in the final. On both lung and short lengths, Ig,r. Jeffery was very skilful and played l,is woods well He is the winner of the cup and gold watch. Mr. Gibsoo, wi,o won the second prize, received a gold watch, the other prize-wi riners s ere : 3, R. Fogg ; 4, C. Algie ; 5, J. Firth ; 0, C. Jordan; 7, E. Chilton 8, J. Lawless. The prizes v ere presented at the close by Alderman Manion, who expressed his regret that Mr. Joseph Wilkinson (chairman of the green) was absent through holidays. The Alderman was sure he Was expressing the sentiments of all of the members w lien he said there was no better member of the Liberal Club than Mr. Wilkinson—(applause) •
